# GMDSS Radio Operator explanations, review copy

Generated from `app/data/explanations/e7.json` by `tools/explanations/review.py`. 458 of 600 questions have an explanation. Each quote below appears word for word in the linked source. "Heads up" notes mark places where the current rule differs from the exam's answer key; "general practice" marks answers with no rule to quote.

Questions without an explanation: 6A4, 21D1, 21D5, 43F4, 48F4, 48F6, 49G5, 52H3, 55H6, 59I1, 59I2, 59I3, 59I4, 59I5, 59I6, 60I1, 60I2, 60I3, 60I4, 60I5, 60I6, 61I1, 61I2, 61I3, 61I4, 61I5, 61I6, 62I1, 62I2, 62I3, 62I4, 62I5, 62I6, 63I1, 63I2, 63I3, 63I4, 63I5, 63I6, 64I1, 64I2, 64I3, 64I4, 64I5, 64I6, 65I1, 65I2, 65I3, 65I4, 65I5, 65I6, 66I1, 66I2, 66I3, 66I4, 66I5, 66I6, 67I1, 67I2, 67I3, 67I4, 67I5, 67I6, 68I1, 68I2, 68I3, 68I4, 68I5, 68I6, 69I1, 69I2, 69I3, 69I4, 69I5, 69I6, 70I1, 70I2, 70I3, 70I4, 70I5, 70I6, 71I1, 71I2, 71I3, 71I4, 71I5, 71I6, 72I1, 72I2, 72I3, 72I4, 72I5, 72I6, 73I1, 73I2, 73I3, 73I4, 73I5, 73I6, 74I1, 74I2, 74I3, 74I4, 74I5, 74I6, 75I1, 75I2, 75I3, 75I4, 75I5, 75I6, 76I1, 76I2, 76I3, 76I4, 76I5, 76I6, 77I1, 77I2, 77I3, 77I4, 77I5, 77I6, 78I1, 78I2, 78I3, 78I4, 78I5, 78I6, 79I1, 79I2, 79I3, 79I4, 79I5, 79I6, 86J3, 86J4, 87J1, 87J4, 91J1, 100J5, 100J6

## Topic 1. Fundamental Concepts

The GMDSS is a worldwide, largely automated distress and safety system for ships. Its core idea is shore-centred alerting: rescue authorities ashore and nearby ships are both alerted, so they can mount a coordinated search and rescue.

- Rescue authorities ashore and ships near the casualty are alerted together, so they can coordinate a search with minimum delay. ([IMO](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf))
- Every GMDSS ship must be able to perform the communication functions essential for its own safety and that of nearby ships. ([IMO](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf))
- The rules apply to all passenger ships and to cargo ships of 300 gross tons and up. ([47 CFR §80.1065](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1065))

### 1A1

What is the fundamental concept of the GMDSS?

**A. GMDSS utilizes automated systems and satellite technology to improve emergency communications for the world’s shipping industry.**  
B. It is intended to automate and improve existing digital selective calling procedures and techniques.  
C. It is intended to provide more effective but lower cost commercial communications.  
D. It is intended to provide compulsory vessels with a collision avoidance system when they are operating in waters that are also occupied by non-compulsory vessels.  

IMO describes the GMDSS as a worldwide network of automated emergency communications for ships at sea, using satellite as well as terrestrial radio techniques to alert rescue authorities and nearby ships. It isn't a commercial-communications system or a collision-avoidance system, and it covers far more than DSC.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “is essentially a worldwide network of automated emergency communications for ships at sea”
- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “can be rapidly alerted through satellite and terrestrial communication techniques to a distress incident”

### 1A2

The primary purpose of the GMDSS is:

A. Allow more effective control of SAR situations by vessels.  
**B. Automate and improve emergency communications for the world's shipping industry.**  
C. Provide additional shipboard systems for more effective company communications.  
D. Effective and inexpensive communications.  

The GMDSS exists to automate and improve emergency (distress and safety) communications for the world's shipping. It is a worldwide network of automated emergency communications, not a system for company or low-cost communications. Overall SAR coordination rests with rescue coordination centres ashore, although ships take part in the search.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “is essentially a worldwide network of automated emergency communications for ships at sea”
- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “the whole operation is co-ordinated by a designated rescue co-ordination centre”

### 1A3

What is the basic concept of GMDSS?

A. Shoreside authorities will rely on reports from nearby vessels to become aware of Distress alerts.  
B. Shoreside authorities and vessels can assist in a coordinated SAR operation only after the correct chain of DSC relays takes place.  
**C. SAR authorities ashore can be alerted to a Distress situation & shipping in the vicinity can be requested to participate in SAR operations.**  
D. SAR authorities ashore wait to have EPIRB Distress alerts confirmed by satellite follow-on communications.  

The GMDSS is shore-centred: search and rescue authorities ashore are alerted to the distress, and ships in the vicinity can then be brought in to help in a coordinated search and rescue operation. Alerts don't depend on chains of DSC relays or on reports from nearby ships.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “The basic concept of the GMDSS is that search and rescue authorities ashore, as well as shipping in the immediate vicinity of the ship in distress, will be rapidly alerted to a distress incident so they can assist in a co-ordinated search and rescue operation with the minimum of delay.”

### 1A4

GMDSS is primarily a system based on:

A. Ship-to-ship Distress communications using MF or HF radiotelephony.  
B. VHF digital selective calling from ship to shore.  
C. Distress, Urgency and Safety communications carried out by the use of narrow-band direct printing telegraphy.  
**D. The linking of search and rescue authorities ashore with shipping in the immediate vicinity of a ship in Distress or in need of assistance.**  

At its core the GMDSS links search and rescue authorities ashore with ships in the immediate vicinity of a ship in distress, so both are alerted and can work together. VHF DSC, MF/HF radiotelephony and narrow-band direct printing are only some of the tools it uses.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “The basic concept of the GMDSS is that search and rescue authorities ashore, as well as shipping in the immediate vicinity of the ship in distress, will be rapidly alerted to a distress incident so they can assist in a co-ordinated search and rescue operation with the minimum of delay.”

### 1A5

What is the responsibility of compulsory GMDSS vessels?

**A. Every vessel must be able to perform communications functions essential for its own safety and the safety of other vessels.**  
B. Vessels must transmit a DSC distress relay upon receipt of a DSC distress alert.  
C. Only the vessels closest to a Distress incident must render assistance.  
D. Vessels must immediately acknowledge all DSC distress alerts.  

Under the GMDSS, every ship must be able to perform the communication functions considered essential for its own safety and the safety of other ships in the area, wherever it operates. The other choices are wrong: ships don't automatically relay DSC distress alerts or acknowledge every alert.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “every ship will be able, irrespective of the area in which it operates, to perform those communication functions considered essential for the safety of the ship itself and of other ships operating in the same area.”

### 1A6

GMDSS is required for which of the following?

A. All vessels capable of international voyages.  
**B. SOLAS Convention ships of 300 gross tonnage or more.**  
C. Vessels operating outside of the range of VHF coast radio stations.  
D. Coastal vessels of less than 300 gross tons.  

The GMDSS rules (Part 80, Subpart W, which implements SOLAS) apply to all passenger ships regardless of size and to cargo ships of 300 gross tonnage and upwards. The keyed answer names the cargo-ship threshold; small coastal vessels under 300 gross tons aren't covered.

- [47 CFR §80.1065](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1065): “apply to all passenger ships regardless of size and cargo ships of 300 tons gross tonnage and upwards”

## Topic 2. Equipment Systems

GMDSS equipment splits into terrestrial radio (VHF, MF and HF with DSC), satellite systems (Inmarsat, COSPAS-SARSAT EPIRBs), maritime safety information receivers (NAVTEX, EGC), and survival craft equipment.

- Every GMDSS ship carries VHF with DSC on Channel 70 and voice on Channels 6, 13 and 16. ([47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085))
- Every GMDSS ship carries a NAVTEX receiver for maritime safety information. ([47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085))
- The 406 MHz satellite EPIRB floats free and activates automatically if the ship sinks. ([47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085))
- Alerting equipment needs the ship's position from a built-in or connected navigation receiver, or entered by hand at least every 4 hours. ([47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085))

### 2A1

Which GMDSS system utilizes terrestrial radio techniques?

A. Inmarsat-FBB  
B. Inmarsat-C  
C. GPS  
**D. VHF-MF-HF-DSC**  

Terrestrial radio means ordinary radio between ships and shore stations, without satellites. VHF, MF and HF DSC are terrestrial systems. Inmarsat-FBB and Inmarsat-C are satellite systems, and GPS is satellite navigation.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “Although satellites play an important part in the GMDSS, terrestrial radio is still important.”

### 2A2

What equipment utilizes satellite communications?

**A. Inmarsat-C**  
B. VHF-MF-HF  
C. NAVTEX  
D. SART  

Inmarsat-C is a satellite communication system: it works through Inmarsat's geostationary satellites. VHF, MF and HF are terrestrial radio, NAVTEX is a terrestrial MF broadcast, and a SART is a radar transponder.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “within the coverage of an INMARSAT geostationary satellite in which continuous alerting is available”

### 2A3

What equipment is used in or near the survival craft?

A. NAVTEX  
**B. EPIRB**  
C. Fathometer  
D. COSPAS-SARSAT  

The ship's satellite EPIRB must be able to be carried by one person into a survival craft and must float free and activate automatically if the ship sinks, so it is used in or near the survival craft. NAVTEX and a fathometer are bridge equipment, and COSPAS-SARSAT is the satellite system, not a piece of equipment on board.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “capable of being carried by one person into a survival craft, capable of floating free if the ship sinks and of being automatically activated when afloat”

### 2A4

What equipment is programmed to initiate transmission of Distress alerts and calls to individual stations?

A. NAVTEX  
B. GPS  
**C. DSC Controller**  
D. DSC Scanning Watch Receiver  

The DSC controller is the unit you program to send DSC calls: distress alerts as well as calls to individual stations. A DSC scanning watch receiver only listens, NAVTEX only receives broadcasts, and GPS supplies position.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.5](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.5): “The distress signal is a digital selective call using an internationally recognized distress call format”

### 2A5

What system provides accurate vessel position information to the GMDSS equipment?

A. COSPAS-SARSAT  
B. EPIRB  
**C. GPS**  
D. Inmarsat-F77  

GMDSS equipment that sends automatic distress alerts needs the ship's position, which comes from a navigation receiver, in practice GPS, either built in or connected. COSPAS-SARSAT and EPIRBs are for alerting, and Inmarsat-F77 is a communications terminal.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “must have either an integral navigation receiver or capability of being connected to an external navigation receiver”

### 2A6

Which of these can be used to receive MSI?

A. SART  
B. EPIRB  
C. Inmarsat-F77  
**D. NAVTEX**  

NAVTEX is the receiver that picks up international broadcasts of maritime safety information (navigational and weather warnings), and every GMDSS ship must carry one. SARTs and EPIRBs are locating and alerting devices, and Inmarsat-F77 is a voice and data terminal.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “A receiver capable of receiving international NAVTEX service broadcasts”

## Topic 3. Sea Areas

The oceans are divided into four sea areas by which shore-based or satellite alerting covers them. A ship's equipment depends on the sea areas it sails in.

- A1: within range of a VHF coast station with continuous DSC alerting. ([47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069))
- A2: outside A1 but within range of an MF coast station with continuous DSC alerting. ([47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069))
- A3: outside A1 and A2 but within Inmarsat geostationary satellite coverage. ([47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069))
- A4: everywhere else, in practice the polar regions. ([47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069))

### 3A1

Which of the following regions lie outside Sea Areas A1, A2, and A3?

A. Sea Areas only apply to Inmarsat footprint areas.  
B. Sea Area A3-I Inmarsat coverage and Sea Area A3-S HF SITOR (NBDP) coverage.  
**C. Sea Area A4**  
D. There are no additional Sea Areas.  

Sea area A4 is defined as everything outside sea areas A1, A2 and A3. In practice that means the polar regions, beyond the coverage of geostationary satellites.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “Sea area A4. An area outside sea areas A1, A2 and A3.”

### 3A2

What sea area is defined as being within range of a shore-based MF station that provides for continuous DSC alerting?

A. Coastal waters  
B. Sea area A3  
C. Sea area A1  
**D. Sea area A2**  

Sea area A2 is the area, excluding A1, within radiotelephone coverage of at least one MF coast station with continuous DSC alerting.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “Sea area A2. An area, excluding sea area A1, within the radiotelephone coverage of at least one MF coast station in which continuous DSC alerting is available”

### 3A3

If a vessel is engaged in local trade and at no point in its voyage travels outside the range of a VHF shore station with continuous DSC alerting then the vessel is operating in what area?

**A. Sea area A1**  
B. Coastal and international zones  
C. Inland and coastal waters  
D. Sea areas A1 and A2  

A ship that always stays within range of a VHF coast station with continuous DSC alerting is operating in sea area A1.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “Sea area A1. An area within the radiotelephone coverage of at least one VHF coast station in which continuous DSC alerting is available”

### 3A4

What is defined as an area, excluding sea areas A1 and A2, within the coverage of an Inmarsat geostationary satellite in which continuous alerting is available?

A. Ocean Area Regions AOR-E, AOR-W, POR or IOR  
**B. Sea Area A3**  
C. Sea Area A4  
D. Coastal and Inland Waters  

Sea area A3 is the area, excluding A1 and A2, within coverage of an Inmarsat geostationary satellite with continuous alerting. AOR-E, AOR-W, POR and IOR are names of Inmarsat ocean regions, not sea areas.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “Sea area A3. An area, excluding sea areas A1 and A2, within the coverage of an INMARSAT geostationary satellite in which continuous alerting is available.”

### 3A5

SITOR (NBDP) equipment is a partial or alternate carriage requirement under GMDSS for vessels operating in which sea area(s)?

A. A1  
**B. A3 and A4**  
C. A1 and A2  
D. A1, A2, A3 and A4  

Ships equipped for sea area A3 by the MF/HF option, and ships equipped for A4, must carry an MF/HF installation that works on DSC, radiotelephony and narrow-band direct printing (SITOR/NBDP). In A3 the Inmarsat option can be used instead, which is why NBDP is a partial or alternative requirement. Ships that stay in A1 or A2 don't need it.

- [47 CFR §80.1091](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1091): “An MF/HF radio installation capable of transmitting and receiving on all distress and safety frequencies in the bands between 1605-27500 kHz using DSC, radiotelephony, and narrow-band direct-printing telegraphy”
- [47 CFR §80.1093](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1093): “ships engaged on voyages in all sea areas must be provided with the radio installations and equipment required by § 80.1091(b)”

### 3A6

What is defined as the area within the radiotelephone coverage area of at least one VHF coast station in which continuous DSC alerting is available as defined by the IMO regulation for GMDSS?

A. Ocean Area Regions AOR-E, AOR-W, POR or IOR  
B. Sea Area A2  
**C. Sea Area A1**  
D. Coastal and Inland Waters  

That is the definition of sea area A1: within the radiotelephone coverage of at least one VHF coast station with continuous DSC alerting.

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “Sea area A1. An area within the radiotelephone coverage of at least one VHF coast station in which continuous DSC alerting is available as defined by the International Maritime Organization.”

## Topic 4. Functional Requirements

Part 80 lists the functions every GMDSS ship must be able to perform at sea, whatever equipment it uses to do them.

- Ships must be able to send ship-to-shore distress alerts by at least two separate and independent means, each using a different radio service (with limited exceptions). ([47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081))
- They must also handle SAR coordinating and on-scene communications, locating signals and maritime safety information. ([47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081))
- General radiocommunications and bridge-to-bridge communications are GMDSS functions too. ([47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081))
- Equipment must cover these functions throughout the intended voyage. ([47 CFR §80.1083](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1083))

### 4A1

Which of the following is a functional or carriage requirement for compulsory vessels?

A. A compulsory vessel must carry at least two (2) FCC licensed GMDSS Radio Operators in all sea areas as well as a GMDSS Maintainer in sea areas A3 & A4.  
**B. A compulsory vessel must satisfy certain equipment carriage requirements based on the intended sea area of operation.**  
C. A compulsory vessel must be able to transmit and respond to Distress alerts and carry only one (1) FCC licensed GMDSS Radio Operator in sea areas A1 & A2.  
D. None of these answers are correct.  

GMDSS equipment requirements depend on the sea areas the ship will pass through on its intended voyage. The operator choices are wrong: every compulsory ship needs at least two GMDSS radio operators, in every sea area, and no rule requires a maintainer specifically for A3 and A4.

- [47 CFR §80.1083](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1083): “as appropriate for the sea area of areas through which it will pass during its intended voyage”
- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “Ships must carry at least two persons holding GMDSS Radio Operator's Licenses”

### 4A2

Which GMDSS communication functions must all compulsory vessels be capable of performing to meet International Maritime Organization requirements?

A. Distress alerting and receipt of Maritime Safety Information via Inmarsat for all vessels intending to operate in Sea Area A4.  
B. Distress alerting and receipt of MSI in Sea Areas A1, A2, A3, and A4 regardless of the vessel's intended area of operation.  
**C. Distress alerting, general communications and receipt of Maritime Safety Information in the vessel's intended area of operation.**  
D. General communications via Inmarsat and receipt of Maritime Safety Information via Enhanced Group Calling in Sea Area A4.  

Ships must be able to perform the GMDSS functions (distress alerting, maritime safety information, general communications and so on) throughout their intended voyage, with equipment chosen for the sea areas they'll actually be in. They don't need equipment for sea areas they won't enter.

- [47 CFR §80.1083](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1083): “capable of complying with the functional requirements prescribed by § 80.1081 throughout its intended voyage”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving maritime safety information”

### 4A3

GMDSS-equipped ships will be required to perform which of the following communications functions?

A. Distress alerting, MSI, SAR and On-scene communications & receipt of satellite alerts from other vessels.  
B. SAR and On-scene communications, Bridge-to-Bridge and general radio communications, MSI and relay of satellite alerts from other vessels.  
C. Bridge-to-Bridge and general radio communications, RDF of EPIRB homing signals, Distress alerting and MSI.  
**D. Transmit distress alerts, SAR and On-scene communications, MSI, Bridge-to-Bridge and general radio communications.**  

The GMDSS functional requirements are distress alerting, search and rescue coordinating communications, on-scene communications, locating signals, maritime safety information, general radiocommunications and bridge-to-bridge communications. Choice D lists only items from that list. The others include things that aren't GMDSS functions, such as relaying satellite alerts or direction-finding EPIRB signals.

- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving search and rescue co-ordinating communications;”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving on-scene communications;”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving bridge-to-bridge communications.”

### 4A4

What equipment can be used to receive Maritime Safety Information?

**A. NAVTEX, EGC receiver or HF SITOR (NBDP).**  
B. EGC receiver, Inmarsat-F77 or VHF WX channels.  
C. HF SITOR (NBDP), Inmarsat-F77 or NAVTEX.  
D. All of these answers are correct.  

Maritime safety information reaches ships by NAVTEX, by the Inmarsat enhanced group call system (SafetyNET), or by HF direct-printing telegraphy (NBDP) broadcasts. Inmarsat-F77 and VHF weather channels aren't GMDSS MSI systems.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “A receiver capable of receiving international NAVTEX service broadcasts”
- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “a radio facility for reception of maritime safety information by the INMARSAT enhanced group calling system”

### 4A5

Which of the following are required GMDSS functions?

A. Bridge-to-Bridge communications, reception of weather map facsimile broadcasts, SAR communications.  
B. Reception of weather map facsimile broadcasts, receiving company email, On-scene communications.  
C. Reception of VHF weather channels, On-scene communications, general communications.  
**D. Bridge-to-Bridge communications, general communications, SAR communications.**  

Bridge-to-bridge communications, general radiocommunications and search and rescue communications are all GMDSS functional requirements. Weather-fax reception, company email and VHF weather channels are not.

- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving search and rescue co-ordinating communications;”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving general radiocommunications to and from shore-based radio sytsems or networks; and”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving bridge-to-bridge communications.”

### 4A6

Which of the following are required GMDSS functions for vessels?

**A. Transmit and receive locating signals, general communications and SAR communications.**  
B. Transmit and receive general communications, transmit Distress Alerts by at least one means, MSI.  
C. Transmit and receive locating signals, send MSI to other ships via EGC, Bridge-to-Bridge communications.  
D. Transmit and receive SAR communications, transmit Distress Alerts by at least one means, Bridge-to-Bridge communications.  

Transmitting and receiving locating signals, general radiocommunications and SAR communications are all GMDSS functional requirements. The other choices go wrong: ships must be able to alert by at least two separate means (not one), and ships receive MSI rather than sending it by EGC.

- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “Of transmitting and receiving signals for locating;”
- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “by at least two separate and independent means, each using a different radiocommunication service”

## Topic 5. Carriage Requirements

What a ship must carry depends on the sea areas of its intended voyage, building up from a common core (VHF DSC, NAVTEX, EPIRB, SART) to MF, then Inmarsat or HF, and HF for A4.

- A1 ships need a way to start a ship-to-shore distress alert from where the ship is navigated. ([47 CFR §80.1087](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1087))
- A2 ships add MF: DSC on 2187.5 kHz and voice on 2182 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.1089](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1089))
- A3 ships choose between an Inmarsat option and an MF/HF option. ([47 CFR §80.1091](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1091))
- Passenger ships and cargo ships of 500 GT and up carry three survival craft VHF radios and a SART on each side. ([47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095))

### 5A1

Which statement is true regarding a vessel equipped with GMDSS equipment that will remain in Sea Area A1 at all times?

**A. The vessel must be provided with a radio installation capable of initiating the transmission of ship-to-shore Distress alerting from the position from which the ship is normally navigated.**  
B. VHF DSC alerting may be the sole means of Distress alerting.  
C. HF or MF DSC may satisfy the equipment requirement.  
D. HF SSB with 2182 kHz automatic alarm generator may satisfy the equipment requirement.  

A ship that stays in sea area A1 must have a radio installation that can start a ship-to-shore distress alert from the position where the ship is normally navigated. That is the requirement itself, so A is the answer. The rule lets it be met in several ways (VHF DSC, the 406 MHz EPIRB, MF DSC within MF DSC coast coverage, HF DSC or Inmarsat), so C describes allowed options rather than the requirement. B is wrong because every GMDSS ship also carries a 406 MHz satellite EPIRB, so VHF DSC is never its only alerting means. D describes the old 2182 kHz alarm system.

> Heads up: Choice C is partly true: the rule does allow MF or HF DSC to meet the A1 alerting requirement. The exam keys A because it states the requirement itself.

- [47 CFR §80.1087](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1087): “must be provided with a radio installation capable of initiating the transmission of ship-to-shore distress alerts from the position from which the ship is normally navigated”
- [47 CFR §80.1087](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1087): “On MF using DSC if the ship is engaged on voyages within coverage of MF coast stations equipped with DSC; or”
- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “A satellite emergency position-indicating radio beacon (satellite EPIRB)”

### 5A2

What statement is true regarding the additional equipment carriage requirement imposed for the survival craft of vessels over 500 gross tons?

A. Additional carriage of two radio equipped lifeboats aft.  
**B. A second radar transponder is required.**  
C. Four additional portable VHF radios are required.  
D. The ability to communicate in all modes with any shore station.  

Cargo ships of 300 to 500 gross tons need at least one search and rescue locating device (radar transponder or AIS-SART). Every passenger ship and every cargo ship of 500 gross tons and upwards needs one on each side, so a second radar transponder is the extra requirement.

- [47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095): “At least one radar transponder or AIS-SART (collectively, “search and rescue locating devices”) must be carried on each side of every passenger ship and every cargo ship of 500 tons gross tonnage and upwards.”
- [47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095): “At least one search and rescue locating device must be carried on every cargo ship of 300 tons gross tonnage and upwards but less than 500 tons gross tonnage.”

### 5A3

Vessels operating in which sea area(s) are required to carry either Inmarsat or HF equipment or a combination thereof under GMDSS?

A. All sea areas  
B. A4  
**C. A3**  
D. A1  

Ships equipped for sea area A3 choose between two options: an Inmarsat ship earth station plus MF, or an MF/HF installation with HF DSC. Either way, they carry Inmarsat or HF equipment, or both. A4 ships need HF because Inmarsat's geostationary satellites don't cover the polar regions.

- [47 CFR §80.1091](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1091): “Ships fitting in accordance to this section have the option to comply with either the requirements of paragraph (a) or (b) of this section.”
- [47 CFR §80.1091](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1091): “An INMARSAT ship earth station capable of:”
- [47 CFR §80.1091](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1091): “An MF/HF radio installation capable of transmitting and receiving on all distress and safety frequencies”

### 5A4

Within a single sea area, what is the primary reason GMDSS imposes carriage requirements for different radio subsystems?

A. Redundancy in duplicating all operational functions in the event of a system failure.  
B. Different subsystems are required to meet the specific equipment carriage requirements of national authorities.  
C. GMDSS vessels must be equipped to communicate in all modes with coast radio stations.  
**D. The combined capabilities of redundant subsystems mitigate the risk of a single point of failure.**  

The GMDSS deliberately combines different subsystems, so a ship can send a distress alert by at least two separate and independent means, each using a different radio service. If one fails, another still works. It isn't full duplication of every function, and it isn't about matching national rules.

- [47 CFR §80.1081](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1081): “by at least two separate and independent means, each using a different radiocommunication service”

### 5A5

If operating within Ocean Area A1, and outside of NAVTEX coverage, a GMDSS-equipped vessel must carry:

**A. Equipment capable of reception of Maritime Safety Information by the Inmarsat enhanced group call system, or HF SITOR (NBDP).**  
B. A GPS receiver.  
C. Equipment capable of maintaining a continuous DSC watch on 2187.5 kHz.  
D. An Inmarsat-F77 terminal.  

Every GMDSS ship needs NAVTEX. A ship that sails in an area with Inmarsat coverage but no international NAVTEX service must also be able to receive maritime safety information by the Inmarsat enhanced group call system (SafetyNET). The exception is a ship that can receive an HF direct-printing (NBDP) MSI service where one is provided.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “If the ship is engaged on voyages in any area of INMARSAT coverage in which an international NAVTEX service is not provided, a radio facility for reception of maritime safety information by the INMARSAT enhanced group calling system”

### 5A6

What is the equipment carriage requirement for survival craft under GMDSS?

A. At least three SCT units and two SARTs on every cargo ship between 300-500 gross tons and the same on all passenger ships regardless of tonnage.  
**B. At least three SCT units and two SARTs on every passenger ship and cargo ships of 500 gross tons and upwards.**  
C. At least two radar transponders must be carried on every cargo ship of 300-500 gross tons and two radar transponders (one for each side) of every passenger ship regardless of tonnage.  
D. All cargo vessels above 300 gross tons and every passenger ship regardless of tonnage must carry three SCT units and two SARTs.  

Every passenger ship and every cargo ship of 500 gross tons and upwards must carry at least three two-way VHF survival craft radiotelephones (SCTs) and two search and rescue locating devices (SARTs), one on each side. Cargo ships of 300 to 500 gross tons need only two SCTs and one SART.

- [47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095): “At least three two-way VHF radiotelephone apparatus must be provided on every passenger ship and on every cargo ship of 500 tons gross tonnage and upwards.”
- [47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095): “must be carried on each side of every passenger ship and every cargo ship of 500 tons gross tonnage and upwards”

## Topic 6. Maintenance Options

GMDSS equipment must stay available, using one or more of three maintenance methods depending on the sea areas.

- The three methods are duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance and at-sea maintenance. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))
- A1 and A2 ships need one method; A3 and A4 ships need at least two. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))
- Whatever the method, a ship can't leave port unless it can perform all its distress and safety functions. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))

### 6A1

Which of the following statements concerning maintenance requirements is FALSE?

A. Compulsory vessels sailing in Sea Areas A1 and A2 must provide any one of the three maintenance options which are: duplication of equipment, shore-based, or at-sea maintenance capability.  
B. Compulsory vessels sailing in Sea Areas A3 and A4 must provide any two of the three maintenance options which: are duplication of equipment, shore-based, or at-sea maintenance capability.  
C. Equipment warranties do not satisfy GMDSS maintenance requirements.  
**D. If shore-based maintenance is used, maintenance services do not have to be completed or performance verified unless the vessel will be sailing to a non-US port.**  

Statement D is false. With shore-based maintenance, repairs must be completed and performance verified before the ship leaves the first port of call after a failure, and every GMDSS unit must be tested before departure from every port, foreign or not. The other statements are true: A1/A2 ships need one maintenance method and A3/A4 ships need two.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “Maintenance services must be completed and performance verified and noted in the ship's record before departure from the first port of call entered after any failure occurs.”
- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “On ships engaged on voyages in sea areas A3 and A4, the availability must be ensured by using a combination of at least two of the following methods”

### 6A2

Which of the following statements concerning GMDSS maintenance requirements is true?

**A. The options are duplication of equipment, at-sea maintenance, and shore-based maintenance.**  
B. Compulsory vessels between 300-500 gross tons are required only to provide one maintenance option, while compulsory vessels larger than 500 gross tons and all passenger vessels are required to provide any two of the three maintenance options.  
C. The "at-sea" maintenance may be waived if the compulsory vessel carries at least three licensed GMDSS Radio Operators.  
D. Compulsory vessels operating in Sea Area A4 are required to carry at least one licensed GMDSS Radio Maintainer.  

The three GMDSS maintenance methods are duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance and at-sea electronic maintenance capability. How many a ship needs depends on its sea areas (one for A1/A2, two for A3/A4), not on its tonnage.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “the availability must be ensured by duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance, or at-sea electronic maintenance capability, or a combination of these”

### 6A3

Which of the following is a GMDSS requirement for all vessels over 300 gross tons operating within range of a MF-DSC equipped shore station?

A. Ship's Master or radio officer must be on watch at all times.  
**B. Only one of the three maintenance options is required.**  
C. MF communications must be handled by the holder of a General Radiotelephone Operator’s License.  
D. Only FCC required spare parts and a maintenance kit for repairs are required.  

A ship that stays within range of MF DSC shore stations is in sea areas A1 and A2, where any one of the three maintenance methods (duplication, shore-based or at-sea) is enough.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “On ships engaged on voyages in sea areas A1 and A2, the availability must be ensured by duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance, or at-sea electronic maintenance capability, or a combination of these.”

### 6A4

What statement is correct regarding the maintenance requirements for A3 ships under GMDSS?

A. If the vessel selects at-sea maintenance no additional parts and spares are required.  
B. On-board maintenance provided by a person holding a GMDSS Maintainer's license will fully meet the requirements.  
**C. Redundancy of functions of certain equipment and on-board maintenance provided by a person holding a GMDSS Maintainer's license will partially meet this requirement.**  
D. Shoreside maintenance and scheduled tests and inspections will not partially meet this requirement.  

_No explanation published._

### 6A5

A ship operating in sea area A-3 must have the following provisions for maintenance:

A. Duplication of Equipment.  
B. Shore Maintenance.  
**C. Any two of these are required.**  
D. At Sea Maintenance.  

Ships in sea areas A3 and A4 must use at least two of the three maintenance methods: duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance, or at-sea maintenance.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “On ships engaged on voyages in sea areas A3 and A4, the availability must be ensured by using a combination of at least two of the following methods: duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance, or at-sea electronic maintenance capability.”

### 6A6

A ship operating in sea area A-1 must have the following provisions for maintenance:

A. Shore maintenance.  
B. Duplication of equipment.  
C. At Sea Maintenance.  
**D. Any one of these is sufficient.**  

Ships in sea areas A1 and A2 need only one of the three maintenance methods: duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance or at-sea maintenance.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “On ships engaged on voyages in sea areas A1 and A2, the availability must be ensured by duplication of equipment, shore-based maintenance, or at-sea electronic maintenance capability, or a combination of these.”

## Topic 7. Radio Spectrum

The radio spectrum is divided into bands by decades of frequency. GMDSS uses MF and HF for long range, VHF for short range, UHF for satellite links (406 MHz EPIRBs and Inmarsat around 1.5–1.6 GHz), and SHF for 9 GHz radar and SARTs.

- MF is 300–3000 kHz, HF 3–30 MHz, VHF 30–300 MHz, UHF 300–3000 MHz and SHF 3–30 GHz. ([47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101))
- Inmarsat ship earth stations transmit on 1626.5–1645.5 MHz, in the UHF band. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))

### 7B1

What is the frequency range for Medium Frequency?

A. 10-30 MHz  
B. 1,000-10,000 kHz  
**C. 300-3,000 kHz**  
D. 30-300 kHz  

Medium frequency (MF) is 300 to 3,000 kHz. That band includes the GMDSS MF frequencies such as 2182 and 2187.5 kHz.

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

### 7B2

What is the frequency range for High Frequency?

A. 300-3,000 kHz  
B. 30-300 MHz  
C. 10-30 MHz  
**D. 3-30 MHz**  

High frequency (HF) is 3 to 30 MHz.

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

### 7B3

What is the frequency range for Very High Frequency?

**A. 30-300 MHz**  
B. 3-30 MHz  
C. 300-3,000 kHz  
D. 10-30 MHz  

Very high frequency (VHF) is 30 to 300 MHz. Marine VHF channels are around 156–162 MHz.

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

### 7B4

What is the frequency range for Ultra High Frequency?

A. 3-30 MHz  
**B. 300-3,000 MHz**  
C. 30-300 MHz  
D. 10-30 MHz  

Ultra high frequency (UHF) is 300 to 3,000 MHz.

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

### 7B5

What is the frequency range for Super High Frequency?

A. 30-300 GHz  
**B. 3-30 GHz**  
C. 300-3,000 MHz  
D. 30-300 MHz  

Super high frequency (SHF) is 3 to 30 GHz. That includes the 9 GHz (X-band) radars that trigger SARTs.

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

### 7B6

What is the primary frequency range for terrestrial communications using skywave propagation?

A. 300-3,000 kHz  
B. 30-300 MHz  
**C. 3-30 MHz**  
D. 10-30 MHz  

Long-distance terrestrial communication by skywave (signals reflected from the ionosphere) is done mainly in the HF band, 3 to 30 MHz. MF works mostly by ground wave by day, and VHF and above generally pass through the ionosphere.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §2.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.101): “6 MF 300 to 3 000 kHz Hectometric waves. 7 HF 3 to 30 MHz Decametric waves. 8 VHF 30 to 300 MHz Metric waves. 9 UHF 300 to 3 000 MHz Decimetric waves. 10 SHF 3 to 30 GHz Centimetric waves.”

## Topic 8. Frequency Bands

Each band behaves differently: VHF is short range, MF reaches a hundred miles or so by ground wave, HF reaches far by skywave but fades and picks up static, and satellites avoid the ionosphere altogether.

- VHF coast stations reach about 20–30 miles. ([IMO](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf))
- MF coast stations reach about 100 miles. ([IMO](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf))

### 8B1

Which of the following systems is most susceptible to fading or static interference?

A. Inmarsat  
**B. HF SITOR (NBDP)**  
C. DSC on channel 70  
D. VHF ARQ  

HF signals travel by skywave via the ionosphere, so HF systems such as SITOR (NBDP) are the most affected by fading and atmospheric static. Inmarsat (satellite) and VHF are largely free of these effects.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 8B2

Which system is most likely to be affected by atmospheric disturbances?

A. VHF DSC  
B. Inmarsat  
**C. MF/HF radiotelephony**  
D. SafetyNETTM  

MF/HF radiotelephony is the most affected by atmospheric disturbances: lightning static and changing ionospheric conditions affect these bands far more than VHF or satellite systems.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 8B3

Which of the following systems is least susceptible to fading or static interference?

A. HF SITOR (NBDP)  
B. MF-HF DSC Controller  
C. VHF ARQ  
**D. Inmarsat**  

Inmarsat uses geostationary satellites at microwave frequencies, so it doesn't depend on the ionosphere and isn't affected by fading and static the way MF/HF systems are.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1069](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1069): “within the coverage of an INMARSAT geostationary satellite”

### 8B4

Which of the following systems is least susceptible to atmospheric disturbances?

**A. Inmarsat**  
B. NAVTEX  
C. MF SITOR (NBDP)  
D. HF SITOR (NBDP)  

Inmarsat is the least affected by atmospheric disturbances: its satellite links don't rely on ionospheric propagation. NAVTEX and MF/HF SITOR do.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 8B5

Which of the following frequency bands would most likely provide reliable communications between two stations that are 100 miles (160 km) apart?

A. The Low Frequency (LF) band.  
B. The High Frequency (HF) band.  
C. The Very High Frequency (VHF) band.  
**D. The Medium Frequency (MF) band.**  

For about 100 miles, MF is the reliable choice: MF ground wave covers roughly that range, which is why MF coast stations define sea area A2 (about 100 miles). VHF reaches only about 20–30 miles, and HF skywave is suited to longer distances.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “Area A2 Beyond area Al, but within range of MF coastal stations (about l00 miles)”
- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “Area A1 Within range of VHF coast stations (about 20-30 miles)”

### 8B6

Which system has the least effective radius of operation?

**A. VHF DSC**  
B. HF SITOR (NBDP)  
C. MF SITOR (NBDP)  
D. NAVTEX  

VHF DSC has the shortest range of these systems: roughly line of sight, about 20–30 miles to a coast station. NAVTEX and MF/HF SITOR reach hundreds or thousands of miles.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “Area A1 Within range of VHF coast stations (about 20-30 miles)”

## Topic 9. Modulation, Demodulation, AM, FM

Modulation puts information onto a carrier; demodulation takes it off again. Emission codes say how: the first letter gives the modulation, the digit the kind of signal, and the last letter the type of information.

- A is double sideband AM, H single sideband with full carrier, J single sideband with suppressed carrier, F frequency modulation. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))
- E means telephony (voice). ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))
- MF/HF voice is J3E; VHF voice is F3E. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))

### 9B1

What statement best describes modulation?

**A. Imposing intelligence onto a radio carrier signal.**  
B. Changing mark-space to 1 and 0.  
C. Adjusting the frequency to the optimum band for long distance communications.  
D. Converting the carrier from a low frequency to a higher frequency.  

Modulation means impressing the information (voice or data) onto a radio-frequency carrier so it can be transmitted.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 9B2

What statement best describes demodulation?

A. Detuning the receiver to remove interfering signals.  
**B. Extracting intelligence from the radio carrier signal.**  
C. Removing atmospheric noise from the signal.  
D. Separating the TELEX signals from the voice signals.  

Demodulation is the reverse of modulation: the receiver extracts the information from the received carrier.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 9B3

Which statement best describes amplitude modulation?

A. The character data from the terminal is changed to audio tones.  
B. The frequency is varied in synchronization with the modulating signal.  
**C. The amplitudes in the sideband(s) changes but the radio carrier frequency remains constant.**  
D. The amplitude of the carrier is changed but there is still only a single frequency being transmitted.  

In amplitude modulation the carrier frequency stays fixed while the information appears as sidebands whose amplitudes vary with the signal. The frequency doesn't change (that's FM), and AM produces sidebands, so it's not a single frequency.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 9B4

What is the emission designation for MF-HF voice signals?

A. F1B  
B. J2B  
C. F3E  
**D. J3E**  

MF/HF voice uses single sideband with suppressed carrier: J3E. In emission codes, J means single sideband with suppressed carrier, 3 a single analogue channel, and E telephony.

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “(6) Telephony (including sound broadcasting) E”

### 9B5

Which statement best describes frequency modulation?

**A. The information signal changes the radio carrier frequency but the amplitude remains constant.**  
B. Both the amplitude and frequency are changed by the modulating signal.  
C. Frequency modulation is subject to interference by atmospheric noise.  
D. High level mixing of the final amplifier signal and the information signal.  

In FM the information changes the carrier's frequency while its amplitude stays constant. That's also why FM is less affected by amplitude noise.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “Emission in which the main carrier is angle-modulated: —Frequency modulation F”

### 9B6

What is the emission designation for VHF-FM?

A. F3C  
**B. F3E**  
C. J2B  
D. H3E  

VHF marine voice is F3E: F for frequency modulation, 3 for a single analogue channel, and E for telephony.

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “Emission in which the main carrier is angle-modulated: —Frequency modulation F”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “A single channel containing quantized or digital information with the use of a modulating sub-carrier, excluding time-division multiplex 2 (4) A single channel containing analogue information 3”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “(6) Telephony (including sound broadcasting) E”

## Topic 10. Carrier and Sidebands

An AM signal is a carrier plus sidebands. Single sideband keeps one sideband, with the carrier suppressed (J) or full (H). J2B sends data as two alternating audio tones through an SSB transmitter.

- J3E is single sideband with suppressed carrier. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))
- J2B feeds FSK audio tones into an SSB transmitter. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en))
- Distress voice frequencies include 2182, 4125, 6215, 8291, 12290 and 16420 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))

### 10B1

Which of the following statements describes the carrier?

A. The carrier consists of at least 3 separate but closely spaced frequencies.  
B. The carrier is used to modulate the information signal.  
C. There are always sidebands on either side of the carrier.  
**D. The carrier is a Radio Frequency (RF) signal that is modulated to carry intelligence.**  

The carrier is the radio-frequency signal that's modulated to carry the information.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 10B2

On what frequencies would the J3E emission be used for GMDSS Distress communications?

**A. 2182.0 kHz, 16420.0 kHz or 8291.0 kHz.**  
B. 2182.0, 2187.5 kHz or 8414.5 kHz.  
C. 8414.5 kHz, 8291.0 kHz or 8376.5 kHz.  
D. 4125.0 kHz, 16420.0 kHz or 4177.5 kHz.  

J3E (SSB voice) is used on the radiotelephone distress frequencies: 2182 kHz, and HF frequencies such as 8291 and 16420 kHz. 2187.5, 8414.5 and 4207.5 kHz are DSC frequencies, and 8376.5 and 4177.5 kHz are NBDP.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”

### 10B3

How many sidebands are present in the J3E mode?

A. Two sidebands and a carrier.  
**B. One upper sideband.**  
C. One lower sideband.  
D. Two carriers and one sideband.  

J3E is single sideband with suppressed carrier. Marine SSB voice uses the upper sideband, so there's one upper sideband and no carrier.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”

### 10B4

What is the signal transmitted in H3E mode?

A. Two sidebands, upper and lower.  
B. A reduced carrier and the lower sideband.  
**C. A full carrier and the upper sideband.**  
D. A full carrier and both upper and lower sidebands.  

H3E is single sideband with full carrier: the full carrier plus one sideband (the upper sideband in marine use).

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”

### 10B5

What is the signal transmitted in J2B mode?

A. A full carrier and one sideband.  
B. A full carrier and two sidebands.  
**C. An upper sideband of 2 alternating tones.**  
D. An upper sideband of a single tone switched on and off.  

J2B is single sideband with suppressed carrier, carrying digital information on a modulating sub-carrier: the FSK audio tones that make the telex or DSC signal go out as an upper sideband signal of two alternating tones.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “When frequency-shift keying is effected by applying audio signals to the input of single-sideband transmitters (J2B), the centre of the audio-frequency spectrum offered to the transmitter is 1 700 Hz.”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “A single channel containing quantized or digital information with the use of a modulating sub-carrier, excluding time-division multiplex 2 (4) A single channel containing analogue information 3”

### 10B6

Which of the following statements is true?

A. An RF carrier is always required to carry the information.  
B. There is only a single tone used in J2B mode.  
C. There are a carrier and two sidebands in H3E mode.  
**D. There are two tones used in J2B mode.**  

J2B uses two alternating tones (frequency-shift keying) fed into an SSB transmitter. The other statements are false: J3E has no carrier, J2B isn't a single tone, and H3E has one sideband, not two.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “When frequency-shift keying is effected by applying audio signals to the input of single-sideband transmitters (J2B), the centre of the audio-frequency spectrum offered to the transmitter is 1 700 Hz.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “F1B or J2B 170 Hz and modulation rate of 100”

## Topic 11. Channel Spacing

Channel spacing follows bandwidth: MF/HF voice channels are 3 kHz apart and NBDP telex channels 0.5 kHz apart.

- HF voice channels step by 3 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371))
- NBDP channels step by 0.5 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361))
- NBDP (F1B/J2B) uses a 170 Hz frequency shift. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en))
- It's sent at 100 baud. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en))

### 11B1

What is the channel spacing for MF-HF voice frequencies?

A. 500 Hz  
B. 2.8 kHz  
**C. 3.0 kHz**  
D. 3.5 kHz  

MF/HF voice (SSB) channels are spaced 3 kHz apart. You can see this in the HF duplex channel tables, where channels step by 3 kHz.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”

### 11B2

What is the channel spacing for authorized F1B/J2B frequencies?

A. 170 Hz  
B. 300 Hz  
C. 3.0 kHz  
**D. 500 Hz**  

NBDP (F1B/J2B) channels are spaced 500 Hz (0.5 kHz) apart, as the NBDP channel tables show.

- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “1 4210.5 4172.5”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 11B3

You look up a frequency table and all the listings end in either .0 or .5 kHz. What kind of emission is used with these frequencies?

**A. F1B/J2B SITOR (NBDP)**  
B. J3E SSB Voice  
C. F3E FM Voice  
D. G3E FM Voice  

Frequencies ending in .0 or .5 kHz step in 500 Hz increments, the spacing used for NBDP (F1B/J2B, SITOR) channels. SSB voice channels are spaced 3 kHz apart, and F3E/G3E is VHF FM.

- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “Ch. no. Paired frequencies for NBDP and data transmissions (kHz) 4 MHz 6 MHz 8 MHz 12 MHz 16 MHz 18/19 MHz 22 MHz 25/26 MHz Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship Coast Ship 1 4210.5 4172.5”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 11B4

How many SITOR (NBDP) signals can occupy the space of one voice signal?

A. 10  
**B. 6**  
C. 4  
D. 2  

A voice channel is 3 kHz wide and NBDP channels are 0.5 kHz apart, so six NBDP channels fit in the space of one voice channel (3 ÷ 0.5 = 6).

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 11B5

Which emission mode occupies the most bandwidth?

A. J2B  
**B. F3E**  
C. J3E  
D. F1B  

F3E (VHF FM voice) uses the most bandwidth: marine VHF channels are 25 kHz apart, against 3 kHz for SSB voice and 0.5 kHz for J2B/F1B telex.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 11B6

Which mode occupies the least bandwidth?

A. H3E  
B. A3E  
**C. J2B**  
D. F3E  

J2B (narrow-band telex or DSC) uses the least bandwidth: a 170 Hz frequency shift at 100 bit/s, much narrower than any voice mode.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “F1B or J2B 170 Hz and modulation rate of 100”

## Topic 12. Antennas

The standard GMDSS MF/HF antenna is a vertical whip, which radiates equally in all directions. An antenna coupler matches its fixed length to each operating frequency.

- The MF antenna must be as non-directional and efficient as practicable. ([47 CFR §80.863](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.863))

### 12B1

A vertical (whip) antenna has a radiation pattern best described by?

A. A figure eight  
**B. A circle**  
C. A cardioid  
D. An ellipse  

A vertical whip radiates equally in all horizontal directions, so its horizontal radiation pattern is a circle (omnidirectional).

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 12B2

Why is an antenna coupler/tuner required for MF-HF transmissions?

A. The antenna coupler/tuner calculates the proper spectrum band for the operator to use.  
B. To ensure transmissions are restricted to legal marine frequencies.  
**C. The length of the physically-fixed antenna must be electrically matched to the intended frequency of operation.**  
D. The antenna coupler/tuner indicates whether the ionosphere is ready to reflect a transmission properly.  

A ship's MF/HF whip has a fixed physical length but must work across a wide range of frequencies, so the coupler electrically matches the antenna to each operating frequency.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 12B3

A vertical quarter wave antenna with a good ground connection will:

A. Radiate directionally due to being grounded.  
B. Not function due to being grounded.  
C. Only be used in Satellite communications.  
**D. Radiate omni-directionally.**  

A vertical quarter-wave antenna over a good ground (or seawater) radiates equally in all horizontal directions (omnidirectionally). The ground connection is part of how it works.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.863](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.863): “An antenna system must be installed which is as nondirectional and as efficient as is practicable”

### 12B4

What is the most common type of antenna for GMDSS MF-HF?

**A. Vertically polarized whip antenna**  
B. Horizontally polarized long wire antenna.  
C. Horizontally polarized vertical whip antenna.  
D. Satellite radome with vertical polarization.  

The usual GMDSS MF/HF antenna is a vertically polarized whip: it's omnidirectional and efficient for ground waves over seawater.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 12B5

What is the purpose or function of the antenna coupler/tuner?

A. Electrically matching the antenna system to the transmit frequency.  
B. Changing the overall wavelength of the antenna system (in addition to the fixed length whip.)  
C. Enabling maximum transmitted signal on the band chosen by the operator.  
**D. All of these answers are functions of the antenna coupler/tuner.**  

The antenna coupler matches the antenna electrically to the transmit frequency, in effect changing its electrical length, so the transmitter can deliver maximum signal on the band the operator chooses. All three describe what it does.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 12B6

What advantage does a vertical whip have over a long wire?

**A. It radiates equally well in all directions.**  
B. It radiates directionally for better propagation.  
C. It radiates a strong signal vertically.  
D. It radiates more signal fore and aft.  

A vertical whip radiates equally well in all horizontal directions, whereas a long wire is somewhat directional.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


## Topic 13. Inspections and Exemptions

GMDSS stations are inspected every year, and exemptions are possible only in limited cases. Foreign governments may also inspect a visiting ship's station in some circumstances.

- Required equipment is inspected at least every 12 months by an FCC-licensed GMDSS Radio Maintainer. ([47 CFR §80.1067](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1067))
- Partial or conditional exemptions are possible, for example for a single voyage outside the ship's sea areas. ([47 CFR §80.1071](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1071))
- A foreign government may inspect if the license can't be produced or irregularities are seen. ([47 CFR §80.79](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.79))

### 13C1

How often must a compulsory vessel's GMDSS radio station be inspected?

**A. Annually, by the FCC or designated authority.**  
B. Annually, by the U.S. Coast Guard.  
C. Annually, by the FCC, and every six months if the vessel sails outside of Sea Areas A1 and A2.  
D. The FCC's annual inspection may be waived if and only if monthly inspections are performed by the vessel's on-board GMDSS Radio Maintainer.  

A GMDSS ship's required equipment must be inspected at least once every 12 months. Under Part 80 the inspection is done on the FCC's behalf by an FCC-licensed technician holding a GMDSS Radio Maintainer's License, who issues the Safety Certificate. It isn't a Coast Guard inspection, and there's no six-month rule or waiver for on-board maintainers.

- [47 CFR §80.1067](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1067): “Ships must have the required equipment inspected at least once every 12 months by an FCC-licensed technician holding a GMDSS Radio Maintainer's License.”

### 13C2

What periodic inspection is required in order to remain in compliance with the regulations regarding GMDSS ship radio station inspections?

A. U.S. Coast Guard annual inspection.  
**B. An inspection at least once every 12 months by the FCC or a holder of a GMDSS Maintainers license.**  
C. FCC inspection every five years.  
D. Periodic inspections not required if on board maintainers perform routine preventive maintenance.  

The required equipment must be inspected at least once every 12 months by an FCC-licensed technician holding a GMDSS Radio Maintainer's License.

- [47 CFR §80.1067](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1067): “Ships must have the required equipment inspected at least once every 12 months by an FCC-licensed technician holding a GMDSS Radio Maintainer's License.”

### 13C3

Which statement is FALSE regarding a GMDSS-equipped ship?

A. A conditional or partial exemption may be granted, in exceptional circumstances, for a single voyage outside the sea area for which the vessel is equipped.  
B. The regulations apply to all passenger ships regardless of size and cargo ships of 300 gross tons and upwards.  
**C. Ships must have the required equipment inspected at least once every five years.**  
D. Ships must carry at least two persons holding a GMDSS Radio Operator's license for Distress and Safety radio-communications purposes.  

Statement C is false: the required equipment must be inspected at least once every 12 months, not every five years. The others are true: single-voyage exemptions are allowed in exceptional circumstances, the rules cover all passenger ships and cargo ships of 300 gross tons and up, and two licensed GMDSS radio operators are required.

- [47 CFR §80.1067](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1067): “Ships must have the required equipment inspected at least once every 12 months”
- [47 CFR §80.1071](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1071): “In exceptional circumstances, for a single voyage outside the sea area or sea areas for which the ship is equipped.”

### 13C4

Which statement is FALSE regarding a GMDSS equipped ship?

A. Ships must have the required equipment inspected at least once every 12 months.  
B. The regulations apply to all passenger ships regardless of size and cargo ships of 300 gross tons and upwards.  
C. Ships must carry at least two persons holding a GMDSS Radio Operator's license for Distress and Safety radio-communications purposes.  
**D. A conditional or partial exemption may not be granted, in exceptional circumstances, for a single voyage outside the sea area for which the ship is equipped.**  

Statement D is false: a partial or conditional exemption can be granted, in exceptional circumstances, for a single voyage outside the sea areas the ship is equipped for. The other statements are true.

- [47 CFR §80.1071](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1071): “partial or conditional exemptions may be granted to individual ships”
- [47 CFR §80.1071](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1071): “In exceptional circumstances, for a single voyage outside the sea area or sea areas for which the ship is equipped.”

### 13C5

During an annual GMDSS station inspection:

**A. All required documents and publications might have to be produced and GMDSS operators may be required to demonstrate equipment competencies.**  
B. Licensed GMDSS operators may not be required to demonstrate equipment competencies but all required equipment must be fully operational.  
C. All required equipment must be fully operational and any required publications that are not current must be on order.  
D. GMDSS operators may be required to demonstrate equipment competencies and any of required equipment that is not fully operational can be repaired at the next port of call as long as there is functional duplication.  

An inspector checks the station as a whole: required documents and publications may have to be shown, operators may be asked to demonstrate the equipment, and the ship has to be able to perform all its distress and safety functions. Equipment that isn't working can't simply wait for the next port, and outdated publications don't count just because new ones are on order.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “a ship must not depart from any port unless and until the ship is capable of performing all distress and safety functions”

### 13C6

Which situation is least likely to result in an inspection of the radio installation by foreign governments or administrations?

A. When the ship's station license cannot be produced without delay.  
**B. When a ship visits a port for the first time.**  
C. When operational irregularities are observed.  
D. When compulsory equipment is found to be inoperative.  

A foreign government may ask to see the ship station license. It may inspect the radio installation when the license can't be produced without delay or when irregularities are observed, such as required equipment found not working. Simply visiting a port for the first time isn't a reason for an inspection.

- [47 CFR §80.79](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.79): “When the license cannot be produced without delay or when irregularities are observed, Governments or administrations may inspect the radio installations”

## Topic 14. Required Documents and Publications

Ships must carry certain licenses, certificates, rules and ITU service publications, plus the manufacturers' manuals for their equipment.

- Manufacturers' operating and maintenance manuals for all required equipment must be on board. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))
- Cargo ships of 300 tons and up must carry the station license, operator licenses, logs and the Part 80 rules. ([47 CFR §80.401](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401))
- They must also carry the ITU lists of ship and coast stations and the ITU manual for the maritime mobile services. ([47 CFR §80.401](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401))
- Any one of the GMDSS Master Plan, NGA Pub. 117, the Admiralty List of Radio Signals or IMO Circ. 7 satisfies the requirement. ([47 CFR §80.401](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401))

### 14C1

Which of the following references should be consulted for information on the proper setup and use of GMDSS equipment?

A. ITU List of Equipment Operations.  
B. 47 CFR Part 80 Subpart W.  
C. Instructions are available through the Maritime Safety Information (MSI) system.  
**D. The manufacturer's operating manuals.**  

The manufacturer's operating manuals explain how to set up and use each piece of GMDSS equipment, and Part 80 requires them to be on board. Part 80 Subpart W sets the requirements but doesn't explain how to operate a particular radio.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “all manufacturers' instruction manuals and maintenance manuals for each piece of equipment required and installed must be available on-board ship”

### 14C2

Where can GMDSS regulations pertaining specifically to U.S.-flag vessels be found?

**A. These are located in 47 CFR Part 80.**  
B. These are located in CCIR #476.  
C. These are located in FCC Part 83.  
D. These are published only by the U.S. Coast Guard.  

The FCC's GMDSS rules for U.S. ships are in 47 CFR Part 80, Subpart W. CCIR Recommendation 476 is an old technical standard for NBDP (SITOR) equipment, not a set of regulations, and the FCC's shipboard rules are in Part 80, not Part 83. The Coast Guard doesn't issue the FCC's radio rules.

- [47 CFR §80.1065](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1065): “The regulations contained within this subpart apply to all passenger ships regardless of size and cargo ships of 300 tons gross tonnage and upwards.”

### 14C3

What publications should the GMDSS Radio Operator consult to review the proper procedures to be followed in Distress situations under GMDSS?

A. The manufacturer’s technical manuals.  
**B. 47 CFR Part 80 Subpart W.**  
C. The manufacturer's operator manuals.  
D. 47 CFR Part 90 Subpart V.  

The GMDSS distress, urgency and safety procedures for U.S. ships are in 47 CFR Part 80 Subpart W, which also defines what counts as distress traffic. Manufacturers' manuals explain the equipment, not the procedures, and Part 90 covers land mobile radio.

- [47 CFR §80.1109](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1109): “Distress traffic consists of all messages relating to the immediate assistance required by the ship in distress, including search and rescue communications and on-scene communications.”

### 14C4

Which of the following documents or publications are required by the FCC for GMDSS vessels on international voyages (other than the Great Lakes)?

A. IMO master plan of shore-based facilities (or substitute), station logs, appropriate operator licenses, Inmarsat handbook for GMDSS.  
B. NGA Pub. 117 (or substitute), station logs, appropriate operator licenses, IAMSAR manual volume III.  
**C. Part 80 FCC rules, station logs, station licenses, ITU publications, ITU manual for Maritime Mobile stations.**  
D. Part 80 FCC rules, station logs, station licenses, ITU publications, IMO manual for Maritime Mobile stations.  

The FCC's station-documents table requires cargo ships of 300 tons and up to carry the station license, station logs, operator authorizations, the Part 80 rules, ITU service publications (including the lists of ship and coast stations), and the ITU Manual for Use by the Maritime Mobile and Maritime Mobile-Satellite Services. D is wrong because the manual is an ITU publication, not an IMO one. A and B each include one acceptable item (the GMDSS Master Plan or NGA Pub. 117, either of which satisfies the rule), but they also list an Inmarsat handbook or IAMSAR Volume III, which aren't in the FCC's table.

- [47 CFR §80.401, table of required station documents](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “Cargo ships (300 tons and up): Station License R(1); Appropriate Operator Authorization R; Station Logs R;”
- [47 CFR §80.401, table of required station documents](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “Part 80; FCC Rules and Regulations R;”
- [47 CFR §80.401, table of required station documents](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “Manual for Use by Maritime Mobile (M/M) Service and M/M Satellite Service R”
- [47 CFR §80.401](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “The requirements for having the GMDSS Master Plan, NGA Publication 117, Admiralty List of Radio Signals or IMO Circ. 7 are satisfied by having any one of those four documents.”

### 14C5

Which of the following documents or publications are required by Part 80 of the FCC rules for vessels on international voyages (other than the Great Lakes)?

A. Appropriate Safety Convention Certificate, ITU Master Plan of GMDSS Coast stations, and ITU manual for Maritime Mobile stations.  
B. ITU Master Plan of GMDSS Coast stations, ITU manual for Maritime Mobile stations, ITU List IV & List V.  
**C. ITU List IV & List V, Appropriate Safety Convention Certificate & ITU manual for Maritime Mobile stations.**  
D. ITU List IV & List V, Appropriate Safety Convention Certificate & IMO manual for Maritime Mobile stations.  

The FCC's station-documents table requires these ships to carry the ITU List of Coast Stations (List IV), the List of Ship Stations (List V), the appropriate Safety Convention certificate, and the ITU Manual for Use by the Maritime Mobile and Maritime Mobile-Satellite Services. The "ITU Master Plan" doesn't exist (the GMDSS Master Plan is an IMO publication), and the manual is from the ITU, not the IMO.

- [47 CFR §80.401, table of required station documents](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “Appropriate Safety Convention Certificate R;”
- [47 CFR §80.401, table of required station documents](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401): “List of Ship Stations R(6); Manual for Use by Maritime Mobile (M/M) Service and M/M Satellite Service R; List of Coast Stations R(5);”

### 14C6

Which of the following references should be consulted to identify the name of a vessel based on its Maritime Mobile Service Identity?

A. ITU list of Coast Stations.  
B. ITU List of Radio-determination and Ship Stations.  
C. ITU Master Plan of Shore-Based Facilities.  
**D. ITU List of Ship Stations and Maritime Mobile Service Identity Assignments.**  

The ITU's List of Ship Stations and Maritime Mobile Service Identity Assignments (List V) contains the ship station details, including MMSIs, that administrations notify to the ITU, so you can look up a vessel from its MMSI.
