- [ITU, List of Ship Stations and Maritime Mobile Service Identity Assignments (List V)](https://www.itu.int/en/ITU-R/terrestrial/mars/Pages/shipstationslist.aspx): “The List of Ship Stations and Maritime Mobile Service Identity Assignments (List V) is a service publication containing data collected from Administrations by the Radiocommunication Bureau”

## Topic 15. Maintenance

GMDSS operators do routine checks; anything that may affect the station's proper operation is a maintainer's job. A ship can't sail until it can perform all its distress and safety functions.

- Adjustments or servicing that may affect the station must be done by or under a qualified GMDSS maintainer. ([47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074))
- The maintainer may also be one of the designated operators. ([47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074))
- With shore-based maintenance, each GMDSS unit is tested and logged before leaving every port. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))
- Spares and test equipment follow the manufacturer's recommendations. ([47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105))

### 15C1

Which of the following maintenance functions is NOT the responsibility of the GMDSS Radio Operator?

A. Visual inspection of equipment, including the antenna and associated components.  
B. Perform on-the-air verification checks.  
**C. Aligning the power output stage for maximum power.**  
D. Perform scheduled testing of the battery's charged condition.  

A GMDSS Radio Operator's License covers operating the station and basic equipment adjustments. Any adjustment or servicing that may affect the station's proper operation, such as aligning a transmitter's output stage, must be done by or under a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer. Visual inspections, on-air checks and battery tests are operator tasks.

- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “including basic equipment adjustments as denoted in the knowledge requirements”
- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “all adjustments of radio installations, servicing, or maintenance of such installations that may affect the proper operation of the GMDSS station must be performed by, or under the immediate supervision and responsibility of, a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer”

### 15C2

When may a compulsory vessel NOT be allowed to leave port?

A. When the vessel is in an over-carriage condition.  
B. When the vessel has arranged for both duplication of equipment and shore-based maintenance.  
C. When the vessel is carrying only two licensed GMDSS Radio Operators and is capable of performing all required functions.  
**D. When the vessel has replaced a required piece of GMDSS-related equipment, but its performance has not been verified or logged.**  

A ship may not leave port until it can perform all its distress and safety functions. Ships using shore-based maintenance must also test each GMDSS unit, verify its performance and note the results before leaving every port. Replacement equipment whose performance hasn't been verified or logged doesn't meet that standard.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “a ship must not depart from any port unless and until the ship is capable of performing all distress and safety functions”
- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “Each GMDSS equipment must be tested and performance verified and the results noted in the ship's record before departure from every port.”

### 15C3

Which statement is true regarding the maintenance of GMDSS equipment at sea?

**A. The GMDSS maintainer may also be the person designated to have primary responsibility for radiocommunications during Distress incidents if licensed as an operator.**  
B. Ships must carry at least two persons who qualify as a GMDSS maintainer for the maintenance and repair of equipment if the at-sea maintenance option is selected.  
C. All at-sea maintenance and repairs must be performed by, or under the supervision of a person holding a GMDSS Maintainer license or GMDSS Operator license.  
D. The GMDSS maintainer may not be the person responsible for ensuring that the watches are properly maintained and that the proper guard channels and the vessel's position are entered into the DSC equipment.  

The person qualified as the GMDSS radio maintainer may also be one of the designated GMDSS radio operators, as long as they're licensed as an operator. The other statements are wrong: one maintainer (not two) is enough for at-sea maintenance, and maintenance must be done by or under a maintainer, not an operator.

- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “This person may be, but need not be, the person designated as GMDSS radio operator as specified in § 80.1073.”

### 15C4

Which of the following service or maintenance functions may NOT be performed by the holder of a GMDSS Radio Operator License?

A. Reset tripped circuit breakers or replace defective fuses.  
**B. Any adjustments or maintenance that may affect the proper operation of the station.**  
C. Routine battery maintenance if used as part of the GMDSS station.  
D. Replacement of consumable items such as paper, ribbons, etc.  

Adjustments or maintenance that may affect the proper operation of the GMDSS station must be done by, or under the immediate supervision of, a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer. Resetting breakers, replacing fuses, routine battery care and replacing paper are routine operator tasks.

- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “all adjustments of radio installations, servicing, or maintenance of such installations that may affect the proper operation of the GMDSS station must be performed by, or under the immediate supervision and responsibility of, a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer”

### 15C5

What are the conditions, under GMDSS, whereby a ship is NOT allowed to depart from any port?

A. The vessel is carrying more than the required number of qualified GMDSS radio operators.  
**B. The vessel is not capable of performing all required Distress and Safety functions.**  
C. The vessel has a temporary waiver of its radio license and Safety Certificate.  
D. The vessel is not carrying a GMDSS radio maintainer, but has provided for shoreside maintenance plus duplication of equipment if required.  

Whatever maintenance methods a ship uses, it may not leave any port until it can perform all the GMDSS distress and safety functions. Carrying extra operators, or using shore-based maintenance plus duplication instead of carrying a maintainer, is allowed.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “Irrespective of the maintenance methods used, a ship must not depart from any port unless and until the ship is capable of performing all distress and safety functions as specified in § 80.1081.”

### 15C6

What determines the spares and maintenance materials requirements for GMDSS equipment?

A. 47 CFR Part 80 Subpart W.  
B. IMO Circular "Equipment Spares".  
**C. The recommendations of the manufacturer.**  
D. The GMDSS Maintainer's requirements.  

The tools, spare parts and test equipment a ship carries should be those the manufacturer recommends for the maintenance methods the ship uses.

- [47 CFR §80.1105](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1105): “Adequate tools, spare parts, and test equipment appropriate to the methods used by the ship as recommended by the manufacturer should be provided.”

## Topic 16. License and Personnel Requirements

Every GMDSS ship needs at least two licensed GMDSS radio operators, with defined duties, and a licensed maintainer if it uses at-sea maintenance.

- At least two people with GMDSS Radio Operator's Licenses must be carried. ([47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073))
- One operator has primary responsibility during distress incidents; a second is the backup. ([47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073))
- The GMDSS Maintainer (DM) and Operator/Maintainer (DB) licenses qualify a person as a maintainer. ([47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074))
- A ship using at-sea maintenance carries at least one qualified maintainer. ([47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074))

### 16C1

Which FCC license meets the requirement to serve as a GMDSS operator?

A. General Radiotelephone Operator’s License.  
**B. GMDSS Radio Operator’s License.**  
C. Marine Radio Operator’s Permit.  
D. GMDSS Radio Maintainer’s License.  

Part 80 requires GMDSS ships to carry at least two people holding GMDSS Radio Operator's Licenses. A GROL or Marine Radio Operator Permit doesn't qualify, and a Maintainer's license alone is for maintenance, not operating.

- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “Ships must carry at least two persons holding GMDSS Radio Operator's Licenses”

### 16C2

Which of the following statements concerning GMDSS Radio Operator requirements is FALSE?

A. Each compulsory vessel must carry at least two licensed GMDSS Radio Operators at all times while at sea.  
B. Each compulsory vessel must carry at least two licensed GMDSS Radio Operators at all times while at sea and may elect to carry a GMDSS Radio Maintainer as well.  
**C. All communications involving Safety of life at sea must be logged as long as the compulsory vessel was not involved in such communications.**  
D. While at sea, adjustments to, and the maintaining of, GMDSS equipment may be performed by the GMDSS Radio Operator as long as the work is supervised by an on-board licensed GMDSS Radio Maintainer.  

Statement C is false. A record must be kept of all incidents connected with the radio service that appear important to safety of life at sea; there's no exception for communications your ship wasn't involved in. The others are true: two licensed operators are required, a maintainer may also be carried, and work may be done by an operator under a maintainer's immediate supervision.

- [47 CFR §80.1075](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1075): “A record must be kept, as required by the Radio Regulations and § 80.409 (a), (b) and (e), of all incidents connected with the radiocommunication service which appear to be of importance to safety of life at sea.”

### 16C3

Which FCC license meets the requirements to perform or supervise the performance of at-sea adjustments, servicing, or maintenance which may affect the proper operation of the GMDSS station?

A. General Radiotelephone Operator’s License with Shipboard RADAR endorsement.  
B. Marine Radio Operator’s Permit or GMDSS Maintainer’s license.  
C. GMDSS Radio Operator’s license or Marine Radio Operator’s Permit.  
**D. GMDSS Operator’s/Maintainer’s license or GMDSS Maintainer’s license.**  

At-sea adjustments, servicing or maintenance that may affect the station must be done by or under a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer. The licenses that qualify are the GMDSS Maintainer's License (DM) and the GMDSS Operator's/Maintainer's License (DB).

- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “(1) DM: GMDSS Maintainer's License;”
- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “(2) DB: GMDSS Operator's/Maintainer's License.”

### 16C4

Which statement is FALSE regarding the radio operator requirements for a GMDSS-equipped ship station?

**A. Maintaining a record of all incidents connected with the radio-communications service that appear to be of importance to Safety of life at sea is not required.**  
B. One of the qualified GMDSS radio operators must be designated to have primary responsibility for radiocommunications during Distress incidents.  
C. A qualified GMDSS radio operator, and a qualified backup, must be designated to perform Distress, Urgency and Safety communications.  
D. While at sea, all adjustments or radio installations, servicing or maintenance of such installations that may affect the proper operation of the GMDSS station must be performed by, or under the supervision of, a qualified GMDSS radio maintainer.  

Statement A is false: a record must be kept of all incidents connected with the radio service that appear to be important to safety of life at sea. The others are true: a primary and a backup GMDSS operator must be designated, and maintenance at sea must be done by or under a qualified maintainer.

- [47 CFR §80.1075](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1075): “A record must be kept, as required by the Radio Regulations and § 80.409 (a), (b) and (e), of all incidents connected with the radiocommunication service which appear to be of importance to safety of life at sea.”
- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “A qualified GMDSS radio operator must be designated to have primary responsibility for radiocommunications during distress incidents”

### 16C5

Which of the following are personnel, functional, or equipment FCC requirements of the GMDSS?

A. One FCC licensed GMDSS radio operator in sea areas A1 & A2, two FCC licensed GMDSS radio operators in sea areas A3 & A4 and equipment carriage based on intended sea area of operations.  
B. Distress alerting and response, two USCG STCW GMDSS watchstanders, equipment carriage based on intended sea area of operations.  
C. Equipment carriage reduced for sea areas A3 & A4, Distress alerting and response and two FCC licensed GMDSS radio operators.  
**D. Equipment carriage based on intended sea area of operations, distress alerting and response and two FCC licensed GMDSS radio operators.**  

The FCC's GMDSS rules require equipment based on the intended sea areas, the ability to send and respond to distress alerts, and at least two licensed GMDSS radio operators. The operator requirement is the same in every sea area, and equipment requirements grow, not shrink, in A3 and A4.

- [47 CFR §80.1083](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1083): “as appropriate for the sea area of areas through which it will pass during its intended voyage”
- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “Ships must carry at least two persons holding GMDSS Radio Operator's Licenses”

### 16C6

How many GMDSS radio maintainers must be carried aboard a compulsory vessel if the At-Sea maintenance method is used?

**A. One regardless of sea area of operation.**  
B. Two in Sea Areas A3 and A4.  
C. Two in Sea Area A1.  
D. None of these answers are correct.  

A ship that chooses at-sea maintenance must carry at least one qualified GMDSS radio maintainer, whatever its sea areas.

- [47 CFR §80.1074](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1074): “must carry at least one person who qualifies as a GMDSS radio maintainer”

## Topic 17. Reserve Source of Energy

The Reserve Source of Energy (RSE), usually batteries, keeps the radios working for distress and safety communications when both the main and emergency power sources fail.

- The RSE must run the radios if both the main and emergency sources of electrical power fail. ([47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099))
- It must be independent of the propulsion and the ship's electrical system. ([47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099))
- It doesn't have to power separate MF and HF installations at the same time. ([47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099))
- Battery charge is checked at least every 30 days with the equipment on and the charger off. ([47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099))

### 17C1

Which statement is FALSE regarding the GMDSS requirement for ship sources of energy?

**A. The reserve sources of energy need to supply independent MF and HF radio installations at the same time.**  
B. At all times while the vessel is at sea, a sufficient supply of electrical energy to operate the radio installations and charge any batteries which may be part of the reserve source of energy is required.  
C. An uninterruptible power supply or other means of ensuring a continuous supply of electrical power to all GMDSS equipment that could be affected by normal variations and interruptions of ship's power is required.  
D. If a vessel’s position is constantly required for the proper performance of a GMDSS station, provisions must be made to ensure position information is uninterrupted if the ship’s source of main or emergency energy fails.  

Statement A is false: the reserve source of energy does not need to supply independent HF and MF installations at the same time. The others are true: there must always be enough power to run the radios and charge the reserve batteries, a UPS must protect GMDSS equipment, and position input must survive a main or emergency power failure.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “The reserve sources of energy need not supply independent HF and MF radio installations at the same time.”
- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “An uninterruptible power supply or other means of ensuring a continuous supply of electrical power, within equipment tolerances, shall be provided to all GMDSS equipment”

### 17C2

How often should the charge condition of RSE GMDSS batteries be checked?

A. Every day with the battery charger on trickle charge.  
**B. At least every 30 days with the battery charger turned off.**  
C. At least every 30 days with the battery charger on full manual charge.  
D. Weekly with the battery charger on full manual charge.  

Reserve battery charge should be checked at least every 30 days, with the equipment on and the battery charger off, and the result logged.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “Battery charge levels should be checked at intervals of 30 days or less with equipment turned ON and the battery charger turned OFF.”

### 17C3

Which term describes the source of energy required to supply the GMDSS console with power if the ship’s source of main or emergency energy fails?

A. Emergency power  
B. Ship's emergency diesel generator  
**C. Reserve Source of Energy**  
D. Ship's standby generator  

The Reserve Source of Energy (RSE) powers the radio installations for distress and safety communications if both the ship's main and emergency sources of electrical power fail. The emergency or standby generators are the sources it backs up.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “A reserve source of energy to supply radio installations must be provided on every ship for the purpose of conducting distress and safety radiocommunications, in the event of failure of the ship's main and emergency sources of electrical power.”

### 17C4

What characteristics describe the GMDSS Reserve Source of Energy (RSE)?

A. Supplies independent HF and MF installations at the same time.  
B. Cannot be independent of the propelling power of the ship.  
C. Must be incorporated into the ship's electrical system.  
**D. Must be independent of the ship's electrical system when the RSE is needed to supply power to the GMDSS equipment.**  

The reserve source of energy must be independent of the ship's propelling power and of the ship's electrical system, so it still works when ship's power is lost. It doesn't have to supply separate MF and HF installations at the same time.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “The reserve sources of energy must be independent of the propelling power of the ship and the ship's electrical system.”

### 17C5

What is the requirement for emergency and reserve power in GMDSS radio installations?

**A. Compulsory ships must have emergency and reserve power sources for radio communications.**  
B. An emergency power source for radio communications is not required if a vessel has proper reserve power (batteries).  
C. A reserve power source is not required for radio communications.  
D. Only one of the above is required if a vessel is equipped with a second 406 EPIRB as a backup means of sending a Distress alert.  

GMDSS ships normally have an emergency source of electrical power (a SOLAS requirement) and, in addition, a reserve source of energy that runs the radios if both the main and emergency sources fail. One doesn't replace the other, and a second EPIRB doesn't substitute for either.

> Heads up: Part 80 allows for some ships built before February 1, 1995, and cargo ships under 500 gross tons, that have no fully compliant emergency source. If so, their reserve source must last six hours instead of one.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “A reserve source of energy to supply radio installations must be provided on every ship”
- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “in the event of failure of the ship's main and emergency sources of electrical power”
- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “Six hours, on ships constructed before February 1, 1995, and on cargo ships of less than 500 tons gross tonnage, if the emergency source of electrical power is not provided or does not comply fully”

### 17C6

Which of the following terms is defined as a back-up power source that provides power to radio installations for the purpose of conducting Distress and Safety communications when the vessel's main and emergency generators cannot?

A. Emergency Diesel Generator (EDG)  
**B. Reserve Source of Energy (RSE)**  
C. Reserve Source of Diesel Power (RSDP)  
D. Emergency Back-up Generator (EBG)  

The Reserve Source of Energy (RSE) is the back-up that runs the radio installations for distress and safety communications when the ship's main and emergency power sources fail. The other names are made up.

- [47 CFR §80.1099](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1099): “A reserve source of energy to supply radio installations must be provided on every ship for the purpose of conducting distress and safety radiocommunications, in the event of failure of the ship's main and emergency sources of electrical power.”

## Topic 18. Equipment Testing

At sea, GMDSS equipment is checked every day, by a test or by normal use, with extra weekly checks. Test transmissions must follow the FCC's testing procedure.

- Satisfactory daily tests of GMDSS equipment are logged every day. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- Normal communications can count as the daily check of the radiotelephone equipment. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- Each week, DSC is verified by a real or test call, and survival craft radios and transponders are tested. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- A test must not interfere with traffic in progress. ([47 CFR §80.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.101))

### 18C1

Under GMDSS, a compulsory VHF-DSC radiotelephone installation must be tested at what minimum intervals at sea?

A. Annually, by a representative of the FCC.  
B. At the annual SOLAS inspection.  
C. Monthly  
**D. Daily**  

GMDSS equipment, including the VHF-DSC installation, must be tested daily at sea, and the satisfactory tests must be logged every day. A daily log entry on the condition of the required radiotelephone equipment is also needed.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Daily entries of satisfactory tests to ensure the continued proper functioning of GMDSS equipment shall be made.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “A daily statement about the condition of the required radiotelephone equipment, as determined by either normal communication or test communication;”

### 18C2

Which statement concerning the testing of a compulsory radiotelephone station is FALSE?

**A. Calling the USCG on VHF CH-16 or 2182.0 kHz is the most effective method.**  
B. Tests may be accomplished by using the equipment for normal business.  
C. A daily test is necessary unless the equipment was used for routine traffic.  
D. The test may not interfere with communications in progress and must wait or be suspended if a request to do so is made.  

Statement A is false. Coast Guard stations may be called on 2182 kHz or Channel 16 for tests only in limited cases, such as by FCC inspectors or technicians, so it isn't the right way to do a routine test. The others are true: normal traffic can count as the daily check, and a test must not interfere with traffic and must stop if a station says "wait".

- [47 CFR §80.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.101): “U.S. Coast Guard stations may be contacted on 2182 kHz or 156.800 MHz for test purposes only when tests are being conducted by Commission employees”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “as determined by either normal communication or test communication”
- [47 CFR §80.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.101): “The operator must not interfere with transmissions in progress.”

### 18C3

While underway, how frequently is the DSC controller required to be tested?

A. Once a week  
**B. Once a day**  
C. Twice a week  
D. Once a month  

GMDSS equipment, including the DSC controller, must be tested daily at sea, with a log entry each day. On top of that, a weekly log entry must confirm that the DSC equipment was verified by an actual call or a test call.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Daily entries of satisfactory tests to ensure the continued proper functioning of GMDSS equipment shall be made.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “The proper functioning of digital selective calling (DSC) equipment has been verified by actual communications or a test call;”

### 18C4

At sea, all required equipment (other than Survival Craft Equipment) must be proven operational by:

A. Testing at least every 48 hours.  
B. Weekly testing of all S.C.E. and other compulsory equipment.  
**C. Daily testing or daily successful use of the equipment.**  
D. Daily testing of the S.C.E. and weekly tests of the other equipment.  

At sea, the required equipment must be shown to work every day, either by a test or by successful use in normal communications. Survival craft equipment is checked on a different schedule (weekly log entries and tests at least every 12 months).

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “A daily statement about the condition of the required radiotelephone equipment, as determined by either normal communication or test communication;”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Daily entries of satisfactory tests to ensure the continued proper functioning of GMDSS equipment shall be made.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “The portable survival craft radio gear and radar transponders have been tested”
- [47 CFR §80.1095](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1095): “Survival craft equipment must be tested at intervals not to exceed twelve months.”

### 18C5

The best way to test the MF-HF SITOR (NBDP) system is:

A. Make a radiotelephone call to a coast station.  
B. Initiate an ARQ call to demonstrate that the transmitter and antenna are working.  
**C. Initiate an ARQ call to a Coast Station and wait for the automatic exchange of answerbacks.**  
D. Initiate an FEC call to demonstrate that the transmitter and antenna are working.  

In ARQ mode the two stations automatically exchange answerbacks when the link is made. Calling a coast station and getting its answerback proves the whole NBDP path works: transmitter, antenna, receiver and the link protocol. An FEC broadcast or a voice call can't confirm that.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 18C6

The best way to test the Inmarsat-C terminal is:

A. Send a message to a shore terminal and wait for confirmation.  
B. Send a message to another ship terminal.  
C. If the “Send” light flashes, proper operation has been confirmed.  
**D. Compose and send a brief message to your own Inmarsat-C terminal.**  

Sending a short message to your own Inmarsat-C terminal tests the full path: your terminal transmits, the message goes through the satellite and land earth station, and it comes back to your terminal. A flashing light proves nothing, and messaging another ship or a shore terminal depends on someone else's equipment.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


## Topic 19. Watchkeeping

GMDSS ships keep automatic DSC watches on the frequencies that match their equipment, plus watches for maritime safety information and, when practicable, Channel 16.

- Ships with VHF DSC watch Channel 70; ships with MF watch 2187.5 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123))
- MF/HF ships also watch 8414.5 kHz and at least one other HF DSC frequency suited to time and position. ([47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123))
- Ships keep a Channel 16 listening watch when practicable. ([47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123))
- Received distress alerts must be reported to the master as soon as possible. ([47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121))

### 19C1

A vessel certified for service in Sea Area A3 is required to maintain a watch on:

A. VHF Channel 70, MF Frequency 2182.0 kHz, HF on 8414.5 kHz and one other HF DSC frequency.  
B. MF Frequency 2187.5 kHz, HF on 8414.5 kHz and one other HF DSC frequency, HF on 4125.0 kHz.  
**C. VHF Channel 70, MF Frequency 2187.5 kHz, HF on 8414.5 kHz and one other HF DSC frequency.**  
D. VHF Channel 16, VHF Channel 70, MF Frequency 2187.5 kHz, HF on 8414.5 MHz and HF 4177.5 MHz.  

A ship using the MF/HF option for sea area A3 must keep a continuous DSC watch on VHF Channel 70, on 2187.5 kHz, on 8414.5 kHz, and on at least one other HF DSC distress frequency (4207.5, 6312, 12577 or 16804.5 kHz). 2182 kHz is the MF radiotelephony (voice) distress frequency, not a DSC watch frequency.

> Heads up: A3 ships equipped with the Inmarsat option instead keep watch on Channel 70, 2187.5 kHz and Inmarsat shore-to-ship alerts, not HF DSC. The exam key assumes the MF/HF option.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On VHF DSC channel 70”
- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On the distress and safety DSC frequencies 2187.5 kHz and 8414.5 kHz also on at least one of the distress and safety DSC frequencies 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 12577 kHz, or 16804.5 kHz”
- [47 CFR §80.1089](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1089): “2182 kHz using radiotelephony”

### 19C2

A vessel certified for service in Sea Area A-2 is required to maintain watch on:

A. 2174.5 kHz  
B. 2182.0 kHz  
C. 2738.0 kHz  
**D. 2187.5 kHz**  

A ship equipped for sea area A2 has an MF DSC installation and must keep a continuous DSC watch on 2187.5 kHz, the MF DSC distress and safety frequency. 2182 kHz is the MF voice distress frequency.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On the distress and safety DSC frequency 2187.5 kHz, if the ship is fitted with an MF radio installation”
- [47 CFR §80.1089](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1089): “2182 kHz using radiotelephony”

### 19C3

What are the mandatory DSC watchkeeping bands/channels?

**A. 8 MHz HF DSC, 1 other HF DSC, 2 MHz MF DSC and VHF Ch-70.**  
B. 2 MHz MF DSC, 8 MHz DSC, VHF Ch-16 and 1 other HF DSC.  
C. VHF Ch-70, 2 MHz MF DSC, 6 MHz DSC and 1 other HF DSC.  
D. VHF Ch-70, 2 MHZ MF DSC, 4 MHZ DSC and 8 MHz DSC.  

The mandatory DSC watches are VHF Channel 70, 2187.5 kHz (MF), 8414.5 kHz (8 MHz HF), and at least one other HF DSC frequency chosen for time of day and position. Channel 16 is a voice watch, not DSC.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On VHF DSC channel 70”
- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On the distress and safety DSC frequencies 2187.5 kHz and 8414.5 kHz also on at least one of the distress and safety DSC frequencies 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 12577 kHz, or 16804.5 kHz appropriate to the time of day and the geographical position of the ship”
- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “a continuous listening watch on VHF Channel 16”

### 19C4

Proper watchkeeping includes the following:

A. Monitoring all required frequencies in the proper mode, setting the DSC scanner to 2 MHz, 4 MHZ and 8 MHz for ships in the vicinity, notifying the Master of any Distress alerts.  
**B. After silencing an alarm all displays and/or printouts are read, monitoring all required frequencies in the proper mode, notifying the Master of any Distress alerts.**  
C. Notifying the Master of any Distress alerts, setting the DSC scanner to 2 MHz, 4 MHZ and 8 MHz for ships in the vicinity, monitoring all required frequencies in the proper mode.  
D. Setting the DSC scanner only to the mandatory 2 MHz & 8 MHz, maintain continuous watch on 2182.0 kHz or 4125.0 kHz, notify the Master of any Distress traffic heard.  

Good watchkeeping means keeping the required watches in the right mode, reading the display or printout after silencing an alarm so you know what was received, and telling the master about any distress alert. The HF DSC scanner must include 2 MHz, 8 MHz and at least one other HF band chosen for time and position, not a fixed 2/4/8 MHz set.

- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “Ship or ship earth stations that receive a distress alert must, as soon as possible, inform the master or person responsible for the ship of the contents of the distress alert.”
- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “also on at least one of the distress and safety DSC frequencies 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 12577 kHz, or 16804.5 kHz appropriate to the time of day and the geographical position of the ship”

### 19C5

Proper watchkeeping includes the following:

A. Understanding normal operational indicators, setting the DSC scanner frequencies to minimize alarms, maintaining a proper log.  
**B. Maintaining a proper GMDSS radio station log, understanding normal operational indicators, responding to and comprehending alarms.**  
C. Responding to and comprehending alarms, logging out of Inmarsat-C terminals while at sea, maintaining a proper GMDSS radio station log.  
D. Maintaining a proper GMDSS radio station log, setting the DSC scanner frequencies to minimize alarms, logging out of Inmarsat-C terminals while at sea.  

Proper watchkeeping includes keeping the GMDSS radio log, knowing what the equipment's normal indicators look like, and responding to and understanding alarms. Setting the scanner to avoid alarms, or logging out of Inmarsat-C at sea, would defeat the watch: logged out, the terminal can't receive shore-to-ship distress alerts.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “For satellite shore-to-ship distress alert, if the ship is fitted with an INMARSAT ship earth station”

### 19C6

Which is true concerning a required watch on VHF Ch-16?

A. When a vessel is in an A1 sea area and subject to the Bridge-to-Bridge act and in a VTS system, a watch is required on Ch-16 in addition to both Ch-13 and the VTS channel.  
B. It is not compulsory at all times while at sea until further notice, unless the vessel is in a VTS system.  
**C. When a vessel is in an A1 sea area and subject to the Bridge-to-Bridge act and in a VTS system, a watch is not required on Ch-16 provided the vessel monitors both Ch-13 and the VTS channel.**  
D. It is not always compulsory in sea areas A2, A3 and A4.  

Every ship at sea must, when practicable, keep a continuous watch on Channel 16. The exception: a ship subject to the Bridge-to-Bridge Act and participating in a Vessel Traffic Service doesn't need the Channel 16 watch if it monitors both the bridge-to-bridge channel (13) and its VTS channel.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “Every ship while at sea must maintain, when practicable, a continuous listening watch on VHF Channel 16.”
- [47 CFR §80.148](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.148): “For vessels subject to the Bridge-to-Bridge Act and participating in a Vessel Traffic Service (VTS) system when the watch is maintained on both the bridge-to-bridge frequency and a separately assigned VTS frequency.”

## Topic 20. Logkeeping

The GMDSS radio log records distress, urgency and certain safety traffic, tests, equipment problems and inspections. It is kept at the operating position and retained for set periods.

- The log summarizes distress and urgency communications affecting your own ship. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- Only safety communications on other than VHF need to be logged. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- Entries stay on board in their original form for at least 30 days. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))
- The licensee keeps logs two years, or three if they relate to distress. ([47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409))

### 20C1

Which of the following statements meets requirements for 47 CFR 80 Subpart-W?

A. GMDSS Radio Logs may not be retained aboard compulsory vessels in an electronic file (e.g., word processing) format.  
**B. GMDSS Radio Logs must contain entries of all Distress and Urgency communications affecting your own ship.**  
C. GMDSS Radio Logs must be retained aboard compulsory vessels for a period of at least 90 days in their original form.  
D. Entries in the GMDSS Radio Log are only required for communications within the vessel's intended Sea Area of operation.  

The GMDSS radio log must summarize all distress and urgency communications affecting your own ship. The other statements are wrong: logs may be kept electronically, entries must stay on board for at least 30 days, and entries aren't limited to the vessel's sea area.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “A summary of all distress and urgency communications affecting the station's own ship”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “The log may be kept electronically or in writing.”

### 20C2

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

A. Key letters or abbreviations may be used in GMDSS Radio Logbooks if their meaning is explained.  
B. Urgency communications may need to be entered in the GMDSS radio log.  
**C. Distress communications heard do not require entries if the vessel did not participate in SAR activity.**  
D. Log entries of VHF Safety broadcasts are not required.  

Statement C is false. A record must be kept of incidents important to safety of life at sea, whether or not your ship took part in the search. The others are true: abbreviations are allowed if explained, urgency traffic affecting your ship is logged, and only safety communications on other than VHF have to be logged.

- [47 CFR §80.1075](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1075): “of all incidents connected with the radiocommunication service which appear to be of importance to safety of life at sea”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Key letters or abbreviations may be used if their proper meaning or explanation is contained elsewhere in the same log.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “A summary of safety communications on other than VHF channels affecting the station's own ship.”

### 20C3

Where should the GMDSS radio log be kept on board ship?

A. Captain's office  
B. Sea cabin  
C. Anywhere on board the vessel.  
**D. At the GMDSS operating position.**  

The GMDSS radio log must be kept at the principal radiotelephone operating location (the GMDSS operating position) while the ship is being navigated.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “must be kept at the principal radiotelephone operating location while the vessel is being navigated”

### 20C4

How long must the radio log be retained on board before sending it to the shoreside licensee?

**A. At least 30 days after the last entry.**  
B. At least one year after the last entry.  
C. At least two years after the last entry.  
D. At least 90 days after the last entry.  

All log entries in their original form must stay on board for at least 30 days from the date of entry.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “All entries in their original form must be retained on board the vessel for at least 30 days from the date of entry.”

### 20C5

Which statement concerning radio log archival by the station licensee is FALSE?

A. Retain for two years if there are no Distress entries.  
B. Logs related to an investigation may not be destroyed without specific authorization.  
C. Retain for three years if there are Distress entries.  
**D. Retain for one year unless there are Distress or Urgency entries.**  

Statement D is false. The licensee keeps logs for two years, three years if they relate to a distress situation, and logs related to an FCC investigation can't be destroyed without written authorization. There's no one-year retention period.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Logs must be retained by the licensee for a period of two years from the date of entry”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “Logs relating to a distress situation or disaster must be retained for three years from the date of entry.”

### 20C6

Which of the following logkeeping statements is FALSE?

**A. Entries of all company communications using GMDSS satellite equipment are required.**  
B. Entries relating to pre-voyage, pre-departure and daily tests are required.  
C. A summary of all Distress communications heard and Urgency communications affecting the station’s own ship. Also, all Safety communications (other than VHF) affecting the station’s own ship must be logged.  
D. Entries related to failures of compulsory equipment are required.  

Statement A is false. The log rules cover distress, urgency and certain safety communications, tests, equipment failures and inspections, but not routine company traffic. The other statements reflect logging requirements: pre-departure and daily test entries, equipment failures, and summaries of distress and urgency communications.

> Heads up: Today's rule words the distress entry as communications affecting your own ship, plus relays and messages from search and rescue authorities. A separate rule requires a record of any incident important to safety of life at sea.

- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “An entry that pre-departure equipment checks were satisfactory and that required publications are on hand.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “An entry describing any malfunctioning GMDSS equipment and another entry when the equipment is restored to normal operation.”
- [47 CFR §80.409](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.409): “A summary of all distress and urgency communications affecting the station's own ship, all distress alerts relayed by the station's own ship, and all distress call acknowledgements and other communications received from search and rescue authorities.”

## Topic 21. Call Signs and SELCALs

Stations identify themselves in different ways depending on the system: call signs for voice, selective call numbers (SELCALs) for narrow-band direct printing, and MMSIs for DSC.

- In NBDP, ship stations use 5-digit selective call numbers and coast stations 4-digit identification numbers. ([47 CFR §80.121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.121))
- NBDP equipment also handles 9-digit identities. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.625-4](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.625/en))
- NMN is the Coast Guard communications station at Chesapeake, Virginia. ([National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/marine/hfvprod))

### 21D1

A typical call sign for a large container ship under U. S. flag would be:

**A. KBZY**  
B. WBX1469  
C. NADN  
D. KPH  

_No explanation published._

### 21D2

What would the number 1090 indicate?

A. A ship DSC MMSI number.  
**B. A coast station SITOR (NBDP) SELCAL number.**  
C. A coast station DSC MMSI number.  
D. A ship station SITOR (NBDP) SELCAL number.  

In narrow-band direct printing (SITOR), coast stations are identified by 4-digit numbers and ship stations by 5-digit selective call numbers (SELCALs). 1090 has four digits, so it's a coast station SELCAL. DSC uses 9-digit MMSIs, not 4-digit numbers.

- [47 CFR §80.121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.121): “the coast station must use the ship station selective calling number (5 digits) and its assigned coast station identification number (4 digits)”

### 21D3

Which one of the following is a ship station SELCAL?

A. 1104  
B. 1502352  
**C. 11243**  
D. 0230364973  

Ship station SELCALs for narrow-band direct printing are 5 digits; coast stations use 4 digits. 11243 is the only 5-digit number. The 7- and 10-digit numbers aren't SELCALs.

- [47 CFR §80.121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.121): “the coast station must use the ship station selective calling number (5 digits) and its assigned coast station identification number (4 digits)”

### 21D4

Which of the following is the call sign for a U.S.C.G. coast station?

A. NERK  
B. KPH  
C. WCC  
**D. NMN**  

NMN is the call sign of the U.S. Coast Guard communications station at Chesapeake, Virginia, which broadcasts weather on HF.

- [National Weather Service, Marine products via U.S. Coast Guard HF voice](https://www.weather.gov/marine/hfvprod): “U.S. Coast Guard Chesapeake, VA (NMN)”

### 21D5

What type of station would be assigned the call sign WAB2174?

**A. Tugboat**  
B. Container ship  
C. Large Passenger ship  
D. Bulk Tanker  

_No explanation published._

### 21D6

What number will a ship station use to identify itself using SITOR (NBDP)?

A. Four-digit SELCAL.  
**B. Five-digit SELCAL or 9-digit SELCAL number identical to MMSI.**  
C. 9-digit Inmarsat-F77 I. D. number.  
D. 9-digit Inmarsat-FBB I.D. number.  

A ship identifies itself on SITOR (NBDP) with its 5-digit selective call number, or with a 9-digit identity (equal to its MMSI) on equipment that uses the 7-signal call format. NBDP equipment works with 4-, 5- or 9-digit identities: 4 digits are for coast stations. Inmarsat terminal numbers aren't used on HF NBDP.

- [47 CFR §80.121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.121): “the coast station must use the ship station selective calling number (5 digits) and its assigned coast station identification number (4 digits)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.625-4, Direct-printing telegraph equipment employing automatic identification in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.625/en): “by the number of digits (4, 5 or 9) supplied to the equipment of a calling station to identify the station to be called”

## Topic 22. MMSI- MID and Ship Station I.D. Numbers

Every ship MMSI begins with its Maritime Identification Digits (MID), three digits that identify the country whose administration has jurisdiction over the ship.

- A ship MMSI is nine digits, the first three being the MID. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))
- The MID shows which administration has jurisdiction over the ship. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))
- U.S. MIDs include 338 and 366–369; 303 is Alaska. ([ITU](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs))

### 22D1

What is the MID?

A. Mobile Identification Number  
B. Marine Indemnity Directory  
C. Mobile Interference Digits  
**D. Maritime Identification Digits**  

MID stands for Maritime Identification Digits: the digits in an MMSI that show which country's administration the station belongs to.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “MID Maritime identification digit”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “The MID denotes the administration having jurisdiction over the ship station so identified.”

### 22D2

How many digits are in the MID (Maritime Identification Digits)?

**A. 3**  
B. 7  
C. 9  
D. 10  

The MID is three digits. In a ship MMSI they are the first three of the nine digits.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”

### 22D3

What does the MID (Maritime Identification Digits) signify?

A. Port of registry  
**B. Nationality**  
C. Gross tonnage  
D. Passenger vessel  

The MID identifies the administration (country) with jurisdiction over the station, that is, its nationality. It doesn't say anything about port of registry, tonnage or vessel type.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “The MID denotes the administration having jurisdiction over the ship station so identified.”

### 22D4

Which of the following numbers indicates a U.S. flag ship station?

A. 036627934  
B. 243537672  
**C. 338426791**  
D. 003382315  

A ship MMSI is nine digits starting with the MID. 338 is a U.S. MID, so 338426791 is a U.S. ship. 243 is Hungary, and numbers that start with 0 belong to groups (0 + MID) or coast stations (00 + MID), not individual ships.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”
- [ITU, Table of Maritime Identification Digits](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs): “338 | United States of America |”
- [ITU, Table of Maritime Identification Digits](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs): “243 | Hungary |”

### 22D5

Which of the following MMSI numbers indicates a U.S. flag ship station?

A. 430326190  
B. 033609991  
**C. 303236424**  
D. 257326819  

303 is the MID assigned to Alaska (United States), so 303236424 is a U.S. ship station. 257 is Norway, and a number starting with 0 is a group or coast station identity.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”
- [ITU, Table of Maritime Identification Digits](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs): “303 | United States of America - Alaska (State of) |”
- [ITU, Table of Maritime Identification Digits](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs): “257 258 259 | Norway |”

### 22D6

Which of the following numbers indicates a ship station MMSI?

A. 003372694  
B. 030356328  
C. 3384672  
**D. 623944326**  

A ship station MMSI has nine digits and begins with the MID, so it doesn't start with zero. 623944326 is the only choice in that form (judged by format). Numbers starting with 00 are coast stations, numbers starting with a single 0 are group calls, and 3384672 has only seven digits.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”

## Topic 23. MMSI; Group and Coast Station I.D. Numbers

Where the zeros fall in an MMSI tells you what kind of station it is: no leading zero for a ship, one zero for a group, two zeros for a coast station.

- Group call identities start with a single zero followed by the MID. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))
- Coast station identities start with two zeros followed by the MID. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))
- Administrations may optionally use the sixth digit to show the kind of station (for example 00MID1XXX for coast stations, 00MID2XXX for port stations); any 00 + MID number is a coast station identity. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))

### 23D1

A DSC call is received from a station with a MMSI number of 003669991. What type of station made the call?

A. A vessel operating in Sea Area A3.  
B. A group ship station  
**C. A U.S. coast station**  
D. An Intercoastal vessel  

Coast station MMSIs start with two zeros followed by the MID. 003669991 is 00 + 366, a U.S. MID, so the call came from a U.S. coast station.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”
- [ITU, Table of Maritime Identification Digits](https://www.itu.int/gladapp/Allocation/MIDs): “366 367 368 369 | United States of America |”

### 23D2

A valid MMSI number for a DSC call to a specific group of vessels is:

A. 003664523  
B. 338462941  
C. 003036483  
**D. 030327931**  

A group MMSI starts with a single zero followed by the MID. 030327931 fits (0 + 303). Numbers starting with 00 are coast stations, and 338462941 is an individual ship.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”

### 23D3

A MMSI 030346239 indicates what?

**A. Group MMSI**  
B. Inmarsat-C I.D. number  
C. Coast station  
D. Ship station  

A single leading zero followed by the MID (here 303) marks a group call identity, used to call several ships at once. Coast stations start with two zeros, and ship MMSIs start with the MID.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”

### 23D4

Which of the following statements concerning MMSI is true?

A. Coast station MMSI numbers have 9 digits starting with 4.  
**B. All MMSI numbers are 9 digits and contain an MID.**  
C. Ship station MMSI numbers can be 7 digits or 9 digits depending on the Inmarsat terminal.  
D. Group MMSI numbers must begin with 2 zeros.  

Every MMSI, whether ship, group or coast station, has nine digits and includes the MID: first for ships, after one zero for groups, after two zeros for coast stations. Coast stations begin with 00, not 4, and groups begin with a single zero.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”

### 23D5

Which of the following statements concerning MMSI is FALSE?

A. All Coast Station MMSI must begin with 2 zeros.  
**B. All Coast Station MMSI must begin with the MID then 2 zeros.**  
C. A group call must begin with a single zero followed by the MID.  
D. The first 3 digits of a ship MMSI comprise the MID.  

Statement B is false: coast station MMSIs begin with two zeros and then the MID, not the MID followed by zeros. The others are true: coast stations start with 00, group calls start with one zero and then the MID, and a ship MMSI starts with its MID.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”

### 23D6

Which of the following statements concerning MMSI is true?

A. All ship station MMSI must begin with a single zero and include the MID.  
B. All group station MMSI must begin with the MID.  
**C. None of these answers are correct.**  
D. All Coast Station MMSI must be 9 digits and begin with the MID and then two zeros.  

Each statement gets a format wrong: ship MMSIs start with the MID (not zero), group MMSIs start with zero then the MID, and coast station MMSIs start with two zeros then the MID. So none is correct.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “assigned a 9-digit unique ship station identity in the format M1I2D3X4X5X6X7X8X9 where in the first three digits represent the maritime identification digits (MID)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “Group ship station call identities for calling simultaneously more than one ship are formed as follows: 01M2I3D4X5X6X7X8X9 where the first figure is zero”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”

## Topic 24. IMN for F77 Terminals & AIS MMSI for SART

Some 9-digit identities aren't MMSIs at all. AIS locating devices use special prefixes, and Inmarsat Fleet terminals have their own numbers.

- AIS-SARTs start with 970, man-overboard devices with 972 and EPIRB-AIS with 974. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en))
- Inmarsat Fleet (F77) numbers are 9 digits starting with 76. ([ITU Operational Bulletin notice](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc))
- FleetBroadband numbers start with 77 or 78. ([ITU Operational Bulletin notice](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc))

### 24D1

Which of the following would indicate a standard AIS SART?

A. A 9-digit number beginning with 3.  
**B. A 9-digit number always starting with 970.**  
C. A 9 digit number beginning with 974.  
D. A 9-digit number always starting with 4.  

AIS search and rescue transmitters (AIS-SARTs) use 9-digit identities beginning with 970. 972 is for man-overboard devices and 974 for EPIRB-AIS.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “970 to indicate AIS SART, 972 to indicate man overboard device (MOB), 974 to indicate EPIRB-AIS”

### 24D2

Which of the following would indicate a MOB AIS SART?

A. 003662517  
B. 436982011  
**C. 972350058**  
D. 970125648  

Man-overboard (MOB) AIS devices use identities beginning with 972. 970 is an AIS-SART, and the others are coast station or other numbers.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “970 to indicate AIS SART, 972 to indicate man overboard device (MOB), 974 to indicate EPIRB-AIS”

### 24D3

Which of the following would indicate an F77 terminal?

A. 303261598  
B. 430364290  
C. 338472564  
**D. 764832922**  

Inmarsat Fleet (F77) terminals have 9-digit numbers starting with 76. 764832922 fits. 4-prefixed numbers are Inmarsat-C, and 3xx numbers look like U.S. ship MMSIs.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”

### 24D4

Which of the following would indicate an F77 terminal?

**A. 765044177**  
B. 0036648202  
C. 436682433  
D. 367224126  

Inmarsat Fleet (F77) terminals have 9-digit numbers starting with 76, so 765044177 is the F77 number. 436682433 fits the Inmarsat-C pattern (4, then a MID, then five digits), 367224126 is in U.S. ship MMSI form, and 0036648202 has ten digits, so it isn't a valid MMSI.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “4 N/A MIDX1X2X3X4X5 Inmarsat C Maritime”

### 24D5

Which of the following would indicate an EPIRB AIS SART?

A. 970240099  
B. 430363275  
C. 369632824  
**D. 974350188**  

EPIRBs with an AIS locating signal use identities beginning with 974. 970 is an AIS-SART.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “970 to indicate AIS SART, 972 to indicate man overboard device (MOB), 974 to indicate EPIRB-AIS”

### 24D6

Which of the following would indicate an F77 terminal?

**A. A 9-digit number always starting with 76.**  
B. A 12-digit number starting with the MID.  
C. A 9-digit number ending with the MID.  
D. A 9-digit number always starting with 4.  

Inmarsat Fleet (F77) terminals have 9-digit numbers that start with 76. A 9-digit number starting with 4 is Inmarsat-C.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”

## Topic 25. Inmarsat Mobile Numbers for “C” Terminals

Inmarsat-C terminals have their own Inmarsat mobile numbers (IMNs), separate from the ship's MMSI, and they always start with 4.

- Inmarsat-C terminal numbers start with 4. ([ITU Operational Bulletin notice](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc))
- Inmarsat-C doesn't carry telephone calls; it's a data and telex service. ([ITU Operational Bulletin notice](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc))

### 25D1

You receive a TELEX with the sender’s I.D. of 433863491. What type of terminal sent this message to your vessel?

**A. Inmarsat-C**  
B. Land TELEX terminal  
C. Inmarsat-F77  
D. Inmarsat-FBB  

Inmarsat-C terminals have mobile numbers starting with 4, so 433863491 came from an Inmarsat-C terminal. Telex is also how Inmarsat-C messages are exchanged. F77 numbers start with 76 and FleetBroadband with 77.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “77 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat BGAN Inmarsat Fleet broadband”

### 25D2

You receive a TELEX with the sender’s I.D. of 436640927. What type of terminal sent this message to your vessel?

A. F77 Fleet  
**B. Inmarsat-C**  
C. Land TELEX terminal  
D. Inmarsat-FBB  

An Inmarsat mobile number starting with 4 belongs to an Inmarsat-C terminal. F77 numbers start with 76 and FleetBroadband with 77.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”

### 25D3

Which of the following would indicate an Inmarsat-C terminal?

A. 003698202  
B. 325467263  
**C. 436782433**  
D. 764835982  

Inmarsat-C mobile numbers start with 4, so 436782433 is the Inmarsat-C terminal. 764835982 is an F77 number, and numbers starting with 0 or 3 look like MMSIs.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”

### 25D4

Which of the following would indicate an Inmarsat-C terminal?

A. 600935021  
B. 366294824  
C. 764881432  
**D. 430346275**  

Inmarsat-C mobile numbers start with 4: 430346275. 764881432 is an F77 (76) number, and 366294824 looks like a U.S. ship MMSI.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”

### 25D5

Which of the following would indicate an Inmarsat-C terminal?

**A. 436782011**  
B. 003662517  
C. 325463325  
D. 764848791  

Inmarsat-C mobile numbers start with 4: 436782011. 764848791 is an F77 number and 003662517 is a coast station MMSI.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat numbering using country code +870 (2008)](https://www.itu.int/dms_pub/itu-t/oth/02/02/T02020000650001MSWE.doc): “76 9 digits 9 digits Inmarsat mini-M GAN Inmarsat Fleet”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “0102M3I4D5X6X7X8X9 where the digits 3, 4 and 5 represent the MID”

### 25D6

Which of the following would indicate an Inmarsat-C terminal?

A. A 9-digit number beginning with the MID.  
**B. A 9-digit number always starting with 4.**  
C. A 9 digit number always starting with 970.  
D. A 9-digit number always starting with 3.  

Inmarsat-C mobile numbers are 9 digits starting with 4. A number starting with 970 is an AIS-SART, and one starting with 3 or with a MID looks like a ship MMSI.

- [ITU Operational Bulletin notice, Inmarsat national numbering plan (2005)](https://www.itu.int/itudoc/itu-t/number/gn/imt/82457_ww2.doc): “Inmarsat C terminals have "4" as the initial digit”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.585-10, Assignment and use of identities in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.585/en): “970 to indicate AIS SART, 972 to indicate man overboard device (MOB), 974 to indicate EPIRB-AIS”

## Topic 26. DSC Format and Info Sent

A DSC call is a short burst of digital data: a dot pattern and phasing to wake up receivers, a format specifier, addresses, messages, and closing characters that let the receiver check for errors.

- DSC characters use a ten-bit error-detecting code. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en))
- On MF/HF, DSC is sent as J2B (FSK tones through an SSB transmitter) or F1B. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en))
- A distress alert carries the MMSI, position and time, and the nature of distress if entered. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))
- Each call ends with an End Of Sequence character and an Error Check Character. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en))

### 26D1

What sequence best describes an MF-HF DSC Distress Alert transmission?

A. Vessel name, Distress priority code, vessel position & time, nature of Distress,  
B. A string of dots to stop the DSC scanner, vessel MMSI, vessel position & time, nature of Distress, frequency for follow-on communications.  
C. Vessel MMSI, frequency & emission for follow-on communications, vessel position & time, Distress priority code.  
**D. A string of dots to stop the DSC scanner, Distress priority code, vessel MMSI, vessel position & time, emission for follow-on communications, DSC frequency.**  

An MF/HF DSC distress alert starts with a dot pattern that lets scanning receivers stop and synchronize, then the distress format specifier (the priority), the ship's MMSI, the nature of distress, the position and the time it was valid, and the type of follow-on communication, ending with end-of-sequence and error-check characters. It is sent on a DSC distress frequency. The vessel's name is never sent, and the follow-on frequency isn't chosen in the alert.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “to allow for scanning methods to monitor several HF and MF frequencies by ship stations, the phasing sequence should be preceded by a dot pattern”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “For a “distress” alert the priority is defined by the format specifier”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “The DSC distress alert provides for alerting, maritime mobile service identity (MMSI), ship’s position including time, and if entered nature of distress”

### 26D2

MF-HF DSC alerts are transmitted using what emission type?

**A. J2B to ensure compatibility with DSC receivers.**  
B. J3E to ensure correct voice follow-on frequencies are used.  
C. F1B to ensure correct SITOR (NBDP) follow-on frequencies are used.  
D. A3E to achieve the best transmission range.  

MF/HF DSC is frequency-shift keying at 100 bit/s. When it's produced by feeding audio tones into an SSB transmitter, as most GMDSS consoles do, the emission is J2B. J3E is SSB voice, A3E is AM voice, and F1B is used for NBDP telex.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “When frequency-shift keying is effected by applying audio signals to the input of single-sideband transmitters (J2B)”

### 26D3

When sending a DSC call:

A. Vessel’s position will automatically be sent with DSC calls specifying an alternate frequency.  
**B. Vessel's position will automatically be sent if the vessel is sending a “Distress Hot Key” alert.**  
C. Vessel's MMSI will indicate its ocean region and vessel position.  
D. Vessel’s MMSI and position will automatically be sent for all types of DSC calls.  

A distress alert, including one sent with the distress button, automatically includes the ship's position and time if the DSC unit has them (from a connected or built-in navigation receiver). Other DSC calls don't include the position automatically, though an MF/HF call can carry it in some cases, such as in reply to a position request. The MMSI says nothing about ocean region or location.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “The position and the time should be included automatically by the ship’s navigational equipment if this information is not included it should be inserted manually.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “For MF/HF calls, message 2 may contain the ship’s position”

### 26D4

DSC transmissions are encoded:

A. Using J3E mode for proper follow-on communications.  
B. Using F1B mode to ensure proper reception.  
**C. Using a ten-bit error detecting code.**  
D. Using J2B mode for correct transmission.  

DSC uses characters made from a ten-bit error-detecting code: seven information bits plus three check bits. J3E, F1B and J2B are emission types, not codes.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “The system is a synchronous system using characters composed from a ten-bit error-detecting code”

### 26D5

DSC transmissions are received:

A. Using voice or TELEX modes as appropriate.  
B. Using J3E or H3E modes as appropriate.  
**C. Using digital decoding by the DSC controller.**  
D. Using F1B and/or J2B decoding by the transceiver.  

DSC calls are digital: the DSC controller decodes the received data and shows the call on its display. They aren't listened to as voice or read as telex.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “The system is a synchronous system using characters composed from a ten-bit error-detecting code”

### 26D6

Properly formatted MF-HF DSC transmissions can request which of the following emissions for follow on communications?

A. J3E/H3E telex emissions  
B. F1B/J2B voice emissions  
C. J3E/F3E voice emissions  
**D. J3E/H3E voice emissions**  

For voice follow-on communications on MF/HF, a DSC call requests single-sideband radiotelephony (the J3E telephony code). D is the only choice that pairs SSB voice emissions; H3E (SSB with full carrier) is also SSB voice. F1B and J2B are telex/data emissions, and F3E is VHF FM voice.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “type of subsequent distress communication (telephony)”

## Topic 27. DSC Operations

Operators are responsible for keeping DSC equipment ready: position updated, guard channels selected, and the right actions when alerts come in. Ships normally leave acknowledging and relaying to coast stations.

- Position must be updated automatically, or by hand at least every four hours underway. ([47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073))
- MF/HF distress alerts use a multi-frequency attempt by default; a single frequency can be chosen. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))
- On HF, relay to a coast station only if no coast station acknowledgement and no distress traffic have been heard. ([47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121))

### 27D1

Which of the following statements concerning DSC equipment is true?

A. The GMDSS Radio Operator is not responsible for properly selecting HF DSC guard channels because they are done automatically by the watch receiver.  
B. All equipment must be type accepted by Inmarsat.  
**C. The vessel's navigational position must be updated, either automatically or manually, no less often than every four (4) hours.**  
D. The GMDSS Radio Operator is responsible for properly selecting VHF & MF DSC guard channels but the HF frequencies are done automatically by the watch receiver.  

The ship's position must be entered into all DSC equipment, either automatically from a navigation receiver or by hand at least every four hours while underway. Operators are responsible for selecting the HF DSC guard channels; the receiver doesn't do it for them.

- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “either automatically through a connected or integral navigation receiver, or manually at least every four hours when the ship is underway”
- [47 CFR §80.1073](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1073): “Responsible for selecting HF DSC guard channels”

### 27D2

What is the action that a GMDSS Radio Operator should take when a DSC Distress alert is received requesting J3E?

A. No action is necessary, as the DSC control unit will automatically switch to the SITOR (NBDP) follow-on communications frequency.  
B. The Operator should immediately set continuous watch on VHF channel 70.  
C. The Operator should immediately set continuous watch on the SITOR (NBDP) frequency that is associated with frequency band on which the Distress alert was received.
