**D. The Operator should immediately set continuous watch on the radiotelephone frequency that is associated with frequency band on which the Distress alert was received.**  

On receiving a DSC distress alert, ships must set watch on the radiotelephone distress and safety traffic frequency associated with the DSC frequency the alert came in on (for example 2182 kHz for 2187.5 kHz). If the alert requested J3E, that's voice, not NBDP.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “ship stations must set watch on the radiotelephone distress and safety traffic frequency associated with the distress and safety calling frequency on which the distress alert was received”

### 27D3

What does the DSC control unit do if the GMDSS Radio Operator fails to insert updated information when initiating a DSC Distress alert?

**A. It will initiate the DSC Distress alert and default information will automatically be transmitted.**  
B. It will abort the transmission and set off an audible alarm that must be manually reset.  
C. It will initiate the DSC Distress alert but, as no information will be transmitted, rescue personnel will not be able to identify the vessel, its position, or its situation.  
D. It will initiate the DSC Distress alert, but any station receiving it will have to establish contact with the distressed vessel to determine its identity, position, and situation.  

If the operator doesn't enter details, the distress alert still goes out with default information: "undesignated" for the nature of distress, radiotelephony as the follow-on mode, and the MMSI and position the unit already has. It isn't aborted.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “Use of the button without any previous operator actions to compose the alert should initiate the default distress alert attempt.”

### 27D4

A DSC Distress alert:

A. Must always be sent on VHF Ch-70.  
**B. Will always be sent on one or more of the DSC Distress frequencies.**  
C. Must always be sent on MF 2 MHz plus one other HF DSC frequency.  
D. Must always be sent on VHF Ch-70, then 2 MHz MF then 8 MHz HF.  

A DSC distress alert always goes out on one or more of the seven DSC distress frequencies: Channel 70 on VHF, 2187.5 kHz on MF, and five HF frequencies. Which ones depends on the equipment and the operator's choice; there's no fixed sequence.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

### 27D5

In all cases, the transmit frequency of a MF/HF console DSC Distress alert:

A. Will always go out first on 2187.5 kHz to reach nearby vessels.  
**B. Depends upon operator DSC Call set up entries or manufacturer’s defaults.**  
C. Will go out on 8 MHz and 2 MHz and one other DSC Distress frequency.  
D. Will always go out first on 8414.5 kHz to ensure receipt by a coast station.  

Where an MF/HF console sends a distress alert depends on what the operator set up, or on the manufacturer's defaults if nothing was set. By default it's a multi-frequency attempt, but the operator can choose a single frequency.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “For MF/HF, a distress alert attempt should use multi-frequency call attempt (see § A3-6) by default, however, a single-frequency call attempt may be used when the operator wants to do so.”

### 27D6

DSC relays of Distress alerts to a coast station:

A. Should be done for all Distress alerts received aboard the ship.  
B. Should be transmitted to all ships involved in Distress traffic.  
**C. Should be avoided; unless a DSC acknowledgement has not been heard.**  
D. Are the best means to provide for a retransmission of Distress communications.  

Ships should normally not relay DSC distress alerts, because unnecessary relays overload the system. On VHF and MF, a ship listens on the voice channel, acknowledges by voice if appropriate, and informs the coast station or RCC. On HF, if no coast station acknowledgement and no distress traffic have been heard, the ship relays the alert to the coast station, not to all ships.

- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “For VHF and MF, ships in receipt of a distress alert shall not transmit a distress alert relay, but should listen on the distress traffic channel for 5 minutes and, if appropriate, acknowledge the alert by radiotelephony to the ship in distress and inform the coast station and/or Rescue Coordination Center.”
- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “If no distress communications are heard and if the call is not acknowledged by a coast station, the ship shall transmit a distress relay on HF to the coast radio station and inform the Rescue Coordination Center.”

## Topic 28. DSC Frequencies

There are seven DSC distress frequencies: one VHF, one MF and five HF. Which ones a ship watches depends on its equipment.

- The DSC distress frequencies are 2187.5, 4207.5, 6312, 8414.5, 12577 and 16804.5 kHz and Channel 70. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))
- MF/HF ships watch 2187.5 and 8414.5 kHz plus at least one other HF DSC frequency. ([47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123))
- 2182 kHz is the MF voice distress frequency, not a DSC frequency. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))

### 28D1

Which of the following is NOT a DSC watch frequency?

A. 2187.5 kHz  
**B. 2182.0 kHz**  
C. 6312.0 kHz  
D. 12577.0 kHz  

2182 kHz is the MF radiotelephony (voice) distress frequency, not a DSC frequency. 2187.5, 6312 and 12577 kHz are DSC distress frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”

### 28D2

Which of the following channels and modes should be used when initiating a Distress alert transmission?

A. Ch-6 DSC  
B. Ch-6 Radiotelephony  
**C. Ch-70 DSC**  
D. Ch-13 Radiotelephony and Ch-16 DSC  

VHF distress alerts are sent by DSC on Channel 70. Channel 16 is for the voice distress traffic that follows; Channels 6 and 13 are intership safety and bridge-to-bridge voice.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “DSC on the frequency 156.525 MHz (channel 70), and it must be able to initiate the transmission of distress alerts on channel 70”

### 28D3

How many total frequencies are available for DSC Distress alerting?

A. One  
B. Two  
C. Five  
**D. Seven**  

There are seven DSC distress frequencies: VHF Channel 70, 2187.5 kHz on MF, and five on HF (4207.5, 6312, 8414.5, 12577 and 16804.5 kHz).

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

### 28D4

Which of the following watches must a compulsory Sea Area A1 vessel maintain?

**A. A continuous DSC watch on Ch-70.**  
B. A continuous DSC watch on 8414.5 kHz plus one other HF DSC frequency.  
C. A continuous DSC watch on 2187.5 kHz.  
D. A continuous DSC watch on Ch-16.  

Every GMDSS ship fitted with VHF DSC, as all must be, keeps a continuous DSC watch on Channel 70. The 2187.5 kHz and HF DSC watches apply to ships with MF or MF/HF installations, and Channel 16 is a voice watch.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On VHF DSC channel 70, if the ship is fitted with a VHF radio installation in accordance with § 80.1085(a)(2)”

### 28D5

Which of the following are the MF/HF DSC Distress watch frequencies

A. 2177.5, 4210.0, 6314.0, 8416.5 12579.0, 16806.5  
B. 2182.0, 4125.0, 6215.0, 8291.0, 12290.0, 16420.0  
C. 2174.5, 4177.5, 6268.0, 8376.5, 12520.0, 16695.0  
**D. 2187.5, 4207.5, 6312.0, 8414.5, 12577.0, 16804.5**  

The MF/HF DSC distress frequencies are 2187.5, 4207.5, 6312, 8414.5, 12577 and 16804.5 kHz. The other lists aren't DSC frequencies: B is the voice distress frequencies, C the NBDP distress traffic frequencies, and A is mostly the HF NBDP MSI broadcast frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

### 28D6

How many HF DSC Distress watch channels must be guarded by a compulsory vessel underway.

**A. 2**  
B. 3  
C. 4  
D. 6  

A ship with MF/HF DSC watches two HF DSC frequencies: 8414.5 kHz and at least one other (4207.5, 6312, 12577 or 16804.5 kHz) chosen for time of day and position. (2187.5 kHz is also watched, but that's MF.)

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On the distress and safety DSC frequencies 2187.5 kHz and 8414.5 kHz also on at least one of the distress and safety DSC frequencies 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 12577 kHz, or 16804.5 kHz”

## Topic 29. Sending a Distress Alert

Sending a distress alert is designed to be quick: one protected button sends a default alert, and a fuller alert can be composed if time allows.

- A distress alert must identify the ship and its position. ([47 CFR §80.1113](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1113))
- The distress button with no setup sends the default alert: undesignated distress, telephony follow-on. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en))
- Unacknowledged alerts repeat after a random 3½–4½ minutes. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))
- An Inmarsat distress alert just needs the distress button or a short code. ([IMO](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf))

### 29E1

What is usually the first step for a GMDSS Radio Operator to take when initiating a Distress priority message via Inmarsat?

**A. By pressing a "Distress Button" or "Distress Hot Key(s)" on the equipment.**  
B. By dialing the correct code on the telephone remote unit.  
C. By contacting the LES operator and announcing a Distress condition is in existence.  
D. By contacting the LES operator using the radiotelephone Distress procedure "Mayday"... etc.  

Sending an Inmarsat distress alert is designed to be simple: press the dedicated distress button (or distress hot keys). That gives the alert priority access and routes it automatically to a rescue coordination centre, with the ship's identity and position. You don't need to talk to the land earth station operator first.

- [IMO, Shipping Emergencies: Search and Rescue and the GMDSS (1999)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/GMDSSandSAR1999.pdf): “It only involves pressing a special distress button or using an abbreviated dialling code.”

### 29E2

Which of the following statements is true regarding Distress alerting under GMDSS?

A. Ship to shore Distress alerts are used to alert other ships in port of navigational hazards.  
**B. The Distress alert should identify the station in Distress and its position & time of position update. Also, the alert may include the nature of the Distress, the type of assistance required, or the course and speed of the mobile unit.**  
C. Ship-to-ship Distress alerts are used to alert other ships in the vicinity of navigational hazards and bad weather.  
D. The vessel nearest to the emergency must notify the Coast Guard before leaving the vicinity.  

A distress alert must identify the station in distress and its position, and may also give the nature of the distress, the assistance needed, course and speed, and the time the information was recorded. Ship-to-shore and ship-to-ship alerts are for ships in distress, not for hazards or bad weather.

- [47 CFR §80.1113](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1113): “The distress alert must identify the station in distress and its position. The distress alert may also contain information regarding the nature of the distress, the type of assistance required, the course and speed of the mobile unit, the time that this information was recorded”

### 29E3

If a GMDSS Radio Operator initiates a DSC Distress transmission but does not insert a message, what happens?

A. The transmission is aborted and an alarm sounds to indicate this data must be provided by the operator.  
B. The transmission is not initiated and "ERROR" is indicated on the display readout.  
**C. The transmission will be made with "default" information provided automatically.**  
D. The receiving station will poll the DSC unit of the vessel in Distress to download the necessary information.  

Pressing the distress button without composing a message sends the default distress alert: nature of distress "undesignated", radiotelephony for follow-on traffic, plus the MMSI and the position the unit has. It isn't blocked or aborted.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “The “default distress alert attempt” consists of “undesignated” for the nature of distress, radiotelephony for the communication mode”

### 29E4

Repetition of a DSC Distress alert is normally automatic if not acknowledged after a delay of:

A. 2 - 5 minutes  
B. 10-15 minutes  
C. 1 - 2 minutes  
**D. 3.5 - 4.5 minutes**  

An unacknowledged DSC distress alert attempt is repeated automatically after a random delay of 3½ to 4½ minutes, which keeps repeats from colliding with incoming acknowledgements.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “this call attempt may be transmitted on the same frequency again after a random delay of between 3 ½ and 4 ½ min from the beginning of the initial call”

### 29E5

For a MF/HF DSC Distress alert which statement is FALSE?

**A. It always requests J3E Voice follow-on communications so other vessels can hear the Mayday.**  
B. It will send the minimal necessary information using the "Distress Button" or "Distress Hot Key."  
C. It will send a more detailed Distress format if time permits and operator data entries are correctly performed.  
D. It does not contain all the information normally of interest in On-scene Distress communications.  

Statement A is false: a DSC distress alert doesn't always request J3E voice. Telephony is the default, but the operator can choose telex (NBDP) as the follow-on mode. The other statements are true: the distress button sends minimal default information, a fuller alert can be composed if time permits, and the alert doesn't carry everything needed on scene.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “on MF and HF the communication mode (should be telephone)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “if telex mode was indicated in the DSC distress alert”

### 29E6

Which statement regarding an MF/HF DSC Distress alert is true:

A. Follow on communications should be presumed to take place on the SITOR (NBDP) frequency associated with the specific DSC frequency used.  
**B. Follow on communications should be presumed to take place on the voice frequency associated with the specific DSC frequency used.**  
C. An alternate emission and frequency may be specified for follow-up communications by the vessel in the original Distress alert.  
D. Both the nature of Distress and the alternate emission and frequency must be specified for follow-up communications in the original Distress alert.  

Unless the alert says otherwise, follow-on distress traffic takes place on the radiotelephone frequency associated with the DSC frequency used (for example 2182 kHz for 2187.5 kHz, 8291 kHz for 8414.5 kHz). Receiving ships set watch there. A distress alert can't name an arbitrary follow-on frequency.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “ship stations must set watch on the radiotelephone distress and safety traffic frequency associated with the distress and safety calling frequency on which the distress alert was received”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”

## Topic 30. Follow-On Voice Transmission

After a DSC distress alert, the distress traffic follows by voice on the radiotelephone frequency associated with the DSC frequency used, starting with a MAYDAY call.

- Voice follow-on frequencies: Ch 16, 2182 kHz, and 4125, 6215, 8291, 12290 or 16420 kHz on HF. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))
- After a multi-frequency alert, use 8291 kHz. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))
- The call is MAYDAY three times, THIS IS, and the call sign or name three times. ([47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314))
- The message gives position, nature of distress, assistance wanted, and anything else that helps the rescue. ([47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314))

### 30E1

If a VHF-DSC Distress alert is transmitted what channel is used for follow-on voice transmission?

A. Ch-12  
B. Ch-70  
C. Ch-13  
**D. Ch-16**  

After a VHF DSC distress alert on Channel 70, distress traffic follows by voice on Channel 16 (156.800 MHz).

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “the distress traffic channel in the same band, i.e. 2 182 kHz on MF, channel 16 on VHF”

### 30E2

If a MF-DSC Distress alert is transmitted what frequency is used for follow-on voice transmission?

**A. 2182.0 kHz**  
B. 2760.0 kHz  
C. 2187.5 kHz  
D. 2174.5 kHz  

After an MF DSC distress alert on 2187.5 kHz, distress traffic follows by voice on 2182 kHz.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “the distress traffic channel in the same band, i.e. 2 182 kHz on MF, channel 16 on VHF”

### 30E3

If a HF-DSC Distress alert is transmitted what frequency is used for follow-on voice transmission?

A. 8376.5 kHz  
**B. 8291.0 kHz**  
C. 8401.5 kHz  
D. 8201.0 kHz  

The HF radiotelephone distress traffic frequencies are 4125, 6215, 8291, 12290 and 16420 kHz. In the 8 MHz band that's 8291 kHz, which is also the frequency to use after a multi-frequency alert. 8376.5 kHz is the 8 MHz NBDP (telex) distress frequency.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Where multiple frequency call attempts are transmitted the corresponding distress traffic frequency should be 8 291 kHz.”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “NBDP 2174.5 kHz, 4177.5 kHz, 6268 kHz, 8376.5 kHz, 12520 kHz, and 16695 kHz.”

### 30E4

What is the proper format for a Distress follow on voice transmission? (3x is three times),

A. All Ships 3x, this is Ship's Name/Call Sign 3x, MMSI, Ship's position, nature of distress and assistance requested.  
B. Mayday 3x, this is Ship's Name/Call Sign once, MMSI, Ship's position, nature of distress and assistance requested.  
**C. Mayday 3x, this is Ship's Name/Call Sign 3x, MMSI, Ship's position, nature of distress and assistance requested.**  
D. All Stations 3x, this is Ship's Name/Call Sign 3x, MMSI, Ship's position, nature of distress and assistance requested.  

A radiotelephone distress call starts with MAYDAY spoken three times, then THIS IS and the ship's call sign or name spoken three times, followed by the position, nature of distress and assistance wanted. "All ships" or "All stations" isn't how a distress call begins.

- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “The distress signal MAYDAY spoken three times;”
- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “The call sign (or name, if no call sign assigned) of the mobile station in distress, spoken three times;”
- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “Particulars of the station's position;”

### 30E5

What information should be included in a Distress follow on voice transmission after a DSC Alert?

A. Ship's Name and Call Sign, MMSI number, DSC frequency used and any other information that might facilitate rescue.  
B. Ship's position, Ship’s IMN, the nature of distress and assistance requested.  
**C. Ship's Name and Call Sign, MMSI number & position, the nature of distress and assistance requested.**  
D. Ship’s Name and Call sign, repeat IMN, provide any other information that might facilitate rescue.  

The follow-on voice message identifies the ship (name, call sign and MMSI) and gives its position, the nature of the distress and the assistance needed. The DSC frequency used isn't needed, and the Inmarsat number (IMN) doesn't identify the ship on voice.

- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “The call sign (or name, if no call sign assigned) of the mobile station in distress, spoken three times;”
- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “The nature of the distress;”
- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “The kind of assistance desired; and”

### 30E6

What information is NOT vital in a Distress follow on voice transmission after a DSC Alert?

A. Ship's position, nature of distress and assistance requested.  
B. Ship's Name, Call Sign and MMSI number.  
C. Physical description of the vessel and number of POB.  
**D. Company emergency contact information.**  

Company emergency contact details don't help rescuers on scene. The vital items are the ship's identity, position, nature of distress and assistance required, and other details that help the rescue, such as a description of the vessel and the number of people on board.

- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “Any other information which might facilitate rescue, for example, the length, color, and type of vessel, or number of persons on board.”

## Topic 31. Response to a Distress Alert

When a distress alert comes in, inform the master, watch the associated voice frequency, and normally let a coast station acknowledge first.

- Tell the master about any distress alert as soon as possible. ([47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121))
- Set watch on the voice frequency associated with the DSC frequency the alert arrived on. ([47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123))
- Ships should delay acknowledging so a coast station can respond first. ([47 CFR §80.1117](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1117))
- On HF, ships don't acknowledge another ship's DSC alert. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))

### 31E1

Which statement is true regarding the receipt and acknowledgement of actual Distress follow-on communications by GMDSS ship stations?

A. Ship stations in receipt of Distress alert should not defer acknowledgement for a short interval, so that receipt may be acknowledged by the coast station.  
B. A Coast station has the sole obligation to respond. A ship station should wait for the Coast station MMSI DSC Acknowledgment before taking action. If a Coast station has no response in 15 minutes the ship should DSC acknowledge and inform the RCC.  
**C. A ship station that receives a Distress call from another vessel must, as soon as possible, inform the Master or person responsible for the ship of the contents of the Distress communications received.**  
D. Alerts concerning navigational hazards are second only to Safety traffic.  

A ship that receives a distress alert must tell the master (or person responsible) about its contents as soon as possible. A is wrong because ships should delay acknowledging so a coast station can respond first. B is wrong because there's no 15-minute rule, and a ship sends a DSC acknowledgement only on the advice of the RCC. D is wrong because navigational hazard warnings are themselves Safety traffic, the lowest of the three priorities (distress, urgency, safety).

- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “Ship or ship earth stations that receive a distress alert must, as soon as possible, inform the master or person responsible for the ship of the contents of the distress alert.”
- [47 CFR §80.1117](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1117): “Ships should delay any acknowledgement in order to give sufficient time for a coast station to acknowledge the call.”
- [47 CFR §80.1117](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1117): “Upon advice from the Rescue Coordination Center, the ship may transmit a DSC acknowledgement call to stop it from being repeated.”

### 31E2

What is meant by the acronym “EOS" in a DSC message?

A. Error Of Sequence  
B. End Of Signals  
C. Equal Operating Signals  
**D. End Of Sequence**  

EOS stands for End Of Sequence: the character sent near the end of every DSC call, showing whether an acknowledgement is required.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “EOS End of sequence”

### 31E3

What is the proper procedure to be followed upon receipt of a Distress alert transmitted by use of Digital Selective Calling techniques?

**A. Set watch on the radiotelephone Distress and Safety frequency associated with the Distress and Safety calling frequency on which the Distress alert was received.**  
B. Set watch on the DSC alerting frequency in the band of frequencies the alert was received.  
C. Set a continuous watch on VHF-FM Channel 13, 16 and DSC on Channel 70.  
D. Ship stations equipped with narrow-band direct-printing equipment should respond to the Distress alert as soon as practicable by this means.  

On receiving a DSC distress alert, set watch on the radiotelephone distress and safety traffic frequency associated with the DSC frequency on which it came in. That's where the distress traffic will follow. Ships with NBDP should watch the NBDP frequency only if the alert says NBDP will be used.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “ship stations must set watch on the radiotelephone distress and safety traffic frequency associated with the distress and safety calling frequency on which the distress alert was received”

### 31E4

What is meant by the acronym “ECC" in a DSC message?

A. Every Cipher Counted  
**B. Error Check Character**  
C. Error Cannot Confirm  
D. Even Characters Counted  

ECC stands for Error Check Character: the last character of a DSC call, used to check the whole sequence for errors the ten-bit code didn't catch.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “The error-check character (ECC) is the final character transmitted and it serves to check the entire sequence for the presence of errors”

### 31E5

What action should be taken on receipt of a DSC Distress alert?

A. Read the display screen and/or printout, silence the alarm, always call the Master to confirm the alert is real.  
**B. Silence the alarm, read the display screen and/or printout and listen for any follow-on transmissions.**  
C. Listen for any follow-on voice/TELEX transmission on the appropriate DSC frequency.  
D. Silence the alarm, read the display screen and/or printout, only call the Master if the alert is within 500 NM.  

When a DSC distress alert arrives, silence the alarm, read the display or printout, and then listen on the associated distress traffic frequency for the follow-on voice (or telex) traffic. Listening on the DSC frequency itself won't work: DSC is data.

> Heads up: Part 80 also requires telling the master about any distress alert received, as soon as possible, wherever it is. That's a separate duty from the steps in this answer.

- [47 CFR §80.1111](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1111): “the station receiving the distress alert must set watch on the associated distress traffic frequency for five minutes to determine if distress traffic takes place”
- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “must, as soon as possible, inform the master or person responsible for the ship of the contents of the distress alert”

### 31E6

What action should be taken if a Distress alert is received on the 12 MHz DSC frequency?

A. Use DSC to acknowledge/relay the alert using the 12 MHz DSC frequency.  
B. Do nothing. Ship is too far away to render assistance.  
**C. Set the transceiver to 12290.0 kHz simplex J3E emission.**  
D. Set the transceiver to 12520.0 kHz simplex F1B/J2B emission.  

An alert on the 12 MHz DSC frequency (12577 kHz) is followed by voice on the associated 12 MHz radiotelephone distress frequency, 12290 kHz, using J3E. Don't acknowledge by DSC: on HF that's for coast stations. 12520 kHz is the 12 MHz NBDP frequency.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Ships receiving a DSC distress alert on HF from another ship shall not acknowledge the alert”

## Topic 32. DSC Distress Relays

Relaying a distress alert is the exception, not the rule. Unnecessary relays clutter the distress channels.

- Relay for another ship if it can't alert for itself, or if the master decides more help is needed. ([47 CFR §80.1115](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1115))
- On VHF and MF, a ship that receives a DSC distress alert doesn't normally relay it: it listens on the voice channel for 5 minutes, acknowledges by voice if appropriate, and informs the coast station or RCC. An all-ships relay on these bands is allowed only if the ship in distress can't alert for itself and the master decides more help is needed. ([47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121))
- A relay from a coast station is acknowledged by voice, not DSC. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))

### 32E1

Your ship received a Distress relay from a coast station on DSC frequency 2187.5 kHz. What action should the watch officer take?

A. Retransmit the DSC call on 2187.5 kHz to other vessels in the vicinity to assist in SAR operations.  
**B. Monitor 2182.0 kHz to determine if there are any genuine Distress communications.**  
C. Transmit a voice “Mayday Relay” call on 2187.5 kHz to other vessels in the vicinity.  
D. Transmit a voice “Mayday Relay” call on 2182.0 kHz to other vessels in the vicinity.  

When a coast station relays a distress alert on 2187.5 kHz, don't retransmit it. Monitor 2182 kHz, the associated voice frequency, for the distress traffic, and acknowledge the relay by voice there if appropriate. DSC isn't used to answer a coast station's relay.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Ships receiving a distress alert relay transmitted by a coast station shall not use DSC to acknowledge the call, but should acknowledge the receipt of the call by radiotelephony on the distress traffic channel in the same band”

### 32E2

Your ship received a Distress relay from a coast station on DSC VHF channel 70. What action should the watch officer take?

A. Retransmit the DSC call on Ch-70 to other vessels in the vicinity.  
B. Monitor Ch-06 to determine if there are any genuine Distress communications.  
**C. Monitor Ch-16 to determine if there are any genuine Distress communications.**  
D. Transmit a voice “Mayday Relay” call on Ch-13.  

When a coast station relays a distress alert on Channel 70, monitor Channel 16, the associated voice channel, for the distress traffic, and acknowledge by voice there if appropriate. Don't retransmit the DSC call.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Ships receiving a distress alert relay transmitted by a coast station shall not use DSC to acknowledge the call, but should acknowledge the receipt of the call by radiotelephony on the distress traffic channel in the same band”

### 32E3

Under what condition would you not relay a DSC Distress alert?

A. If the mobile unit in Distress is incapable of further Distress alert communications.  
B. If no Coast Station/Mobile Unit acknowledgement of the alert is observed.  
C. No distress traffic has been heard and the DSC alert is unacknowledged via DSC.  
**D. A coast station DSC acknowledgment of the original Distress alert was received by your vessel.**  

If a coast station has already acknowledged the original distress alert, there's no need to relay it: shore authorities have it. A relay is for when the vessel in distress can't send the alert itself, or (on HF) when no coast station acknowledgement and no distress traffic have been heard, and then it goes to a coast station.

- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “If no distress communications are heard and if the call is not acknowledged by a coast station, the ship shall transmit a distress relay on HF to the coast radio station and inform the Rescue Coordination Center.”
- [47 CFR §80.1115](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1115): “When the mobile unit in distress is not itself in a position to transmit the distress alert; or”

### 32E4

The relay of DSC Distress alerts:

**A. Can quickly overburden the GMDSS systems in the vicinity with improperly transmitted or inappropriately relayed DSC calls.**  
B. Was not originally an intended function of the GMDSS system but now is the preferred method to notify an RCC.  
C. Remains the preferred method for passing Distress message traffic to an RCC or Coast Station.  
D. Should always be done immediately to ensure a Coast Station receives the DSC Distress Alert.  

Inappropriate DSC relays can quickly overload the distress channels and the GMDSS systems around an incident. That's why ships normally don't relay, and do so only when no acknowledgement or distress traffic has been heard.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1117](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1117): “In cases where no acknowledgement has been heard and no distress traffic has been heard, the ship should transmit a distress alert relay to the coast station.”
- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “For VHF and MF, ships in receipt of a distress alert shall not transmit a distress alert relay”

### 32E5

Transmission of a DSC Distress alert by a station on behalf of another vessel actually in Distress should NOT occur:

A. When the mobile unit actually in Distress is not itself in a position to transmit the Distress alert.  
B. When the Master or responsible person on the mobile unit not in Distress so decides.  
C. When the responsible person at the Coast Station determines further help is necessary.  
**D. When communications between the Distress vessel and a Coast station are already in progress.**  

A ship relays a distress alert on behalf of another when the vessel in distress can't send the alert itself, or when the master decides further help is needed. If the vessel in distress is already in communication with a coast station, a relay isn't needed.

- [47 CFR §80.1115](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1115): “When the mobile unit in distress is not itself in a position to transmit the distress alert; or”
- [47 CFR §80.1115](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1115): “When the master or person responsible for the mobile unit not in distress or the person responsible for the land station determines that further help is necessary.”

### 32E6

DSC Relays of DSC Distress alerts received from other ships should be done?

**A. Only when the original DSC call is not acknowledged, and no follow-on Distress traffic has been heard.**  
B. Only by Inmarsat-C TELEX with Distress priority conveying the follow-on Distress traffic that has been heard.  
C. Only by Inmarsat-FBB voice or TELEX with Distress priority if no follow-on Distress traffic has been heard.  
D. Preferably by MF/HF voice or TELEX directly to the RCC conveying the follow-on Distress traffic that has been heard.  

A ship should relay another ship's DSC distress alert only when it hasn't been acknowledged and no distress traffic has been heard, and then to a coast station, not to all ships. Under Part 80 that's the HF procedure. On VHF and MF, ships instead listen on the voice channel, acknowledge by voice if appropriate, and inform the coast station or RCC.

- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “If no distress communications are heard and if the call is not acknowledged by a coast station, the ship shall transmit a distress relay on HF to the coast radio station and inform the Rescue Coordination Center.”
- [47 CFR §80.1121](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1121): “For VHF and MF, ships in receipt of a distress alert shall not transmit a distress alert relay, but should listen on the distress traffic channel for 5 minutes and, if appropriate, acknowledge the alert by radiotelephony to the ship in distress and inform the coast station and/or Rescue Coordination Center.”

## Topic 33. Action-False Distress Alerts

A false distress alert must be cancelled straight away, by voice on the associated distress frequency (and, with current equipment, a DSC self-cancel first). For Inmarsat, send a cancellation through the same land earth station.

- VHF: reset, switch to Channel 16 and cancel by voice to all stations. ([47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335))
- MF: cancel by voice on 2182 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335))
- HF: cancel on the voice distress frequency in each band used. ([47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335))
- Current ITU procedure sends a DSC self-cancel first, if the equipment can. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en))

### 33E1

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on VHF?

**A. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on Ch-16.**  
B. Send a DSC cancellation message on Ch-70.  
C. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on Ch-13.  
D. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on Ch-22A.  

After a false VHF DSC distress alert, reset the equipment, switch to Channel 16 and make a voice broadcast to all stations giving the ship's name, call sign and MMSI and cancelling the alert.

> Heads up: Today's ITU procedure (Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11) adds a step: if the equipment can, first send a DSC "self-cancel" on the frequencies used, then make the voice cancellation. The FCC rule and the exam key cover only the voice cancellation.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Set to Channel 16; and”
- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Transmit a broadcast message to “All stations” giving the ship's name, call sign or registration number, and MMSI, and cancel the false distress alert.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Immediately cancel the distress alert by transmitting a DSC self-cancel on all the frequencies where the inadvertent DSC distress alert was transmitted, if the ship station is capable hereof.”

### 33E2

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on MF?

A. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on 2187.5 kHz.  
**B. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on 2182.0 kHz.**  
C. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on 2174.5 kHz.  
D. Send another DSC alert and follow on with voice on 2182.0 kHz.  

After a false MF DSC distress alert (2187.5 kHz), reset the equipment and cancel it by voice to all stations on 2182 kHz, the associated voice distress frequency. Sending another DSC alert would make things worse.

> Heads up: Today's ITU procedure (Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11) adds a step: if the equipment can, first send a DSC "self-cancel" on the frequencies used, then make the voice cancellation. The FCC rule and the exam key cover only the voice cancellation.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Tune for radiotelephony transmission on 2182 kHz; and”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Immediately cancel the distress alert by transmitting a DSC self-cancel on all the frequencies where the inadvertent DSC distress alert was transmitted, if the ship station is capable hereof.”

### 33E3

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on MF?

A. Send another DSC alert on 2187.5 kHz. and follow on with voice on 2187.5 kHz.  
B. No action is necessary.  
**C. Make a voice announcement to cancel the alert on 2182.0 kHz.**  
D. Send a DSC alert on all 7 DSC frequencies and follow on voice on 2174.5 kHz.  

A false MF DSC distress alert must be cancelled by voice on 2182 kHz, giving the ship's name, call sign and MMSI. Doing nothing isn't an option.

> Heads up: Today's ITU procedure (Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11) adds a step: if the equipment can, first send a DSC "self-cancel" on the frequencies used, then make the voice cancellation. The FCC rule and the exam key cover only the voice cancellation.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Tune for radiotelephony transmission on 2182 kHz; and”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Immediately cancel the distress alert by transmitting a DSC self-cancel on all the frequencies where the inadvertent DSC distress alert was transmitted, if the ship station is capable hereof.”

### 33E4

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on 8 MHz?

A. Make an "ALL STATIONS” call on all 5 H.F. TELEX channels canceling the alert.  
B. Make a "MAYDAY" call on 8414.5 kHz canceling the alert.  
C. Make an "Urgency" call on 8614.0 kHz canceling the alert.  
**D. Make an "ALL STATIONS" call on 8291.0 kHz canceling the alert.**  

A false HF DSC alert is cancelled by voice on the radiotelephone distress frequency of each band used. For the 8 MHz band that's 8291 kHz, with a broadcast to "All stations" giving the ship's details.

> Heads up: Today's ITU procedure (Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11) adds a step: if the equipment can, first send a DSC "self-cancel" on the frequencies used, then make the voice cancellation. The FCC rule and the exam key cover only the voice cancellation.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Tune for radiotelephony on the distress and safety frequency in each band in which a false distress alert was transmitted; and”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Immediately cancel the distress alert by transmitting a DSC self-cancel on all the frequencies where the inadvertent DSC distress alert was transmitted, if the ship station is capable hereof.”

### 33E5

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on 12577.0 kHz?

**A. Make an "ALL STATIONS” call on the associated 12 MHz J3E frequency canceling the alert.**  
B. No action is necessary.  
C. Make an "ALL STATIONS” call on all 5 H.F. TELEX frequencies canceling the alert.  
D. Send a message to the nearest RCC via Inmarsat canceling the alert.  

For a false alert on 12577 kHz (12 MHz DSC), make an "All stations" voice cancellation on the associated 12 MHz radiotelephone distress frequency, 12290 kHz (J3E).

> Heads up: Today's ITU procedure (Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11) adds a step: if the equipment can, first send a DSC "self-cancel" on the frequencies used, then make the voice cancellation. The FCC rule and the exam key cover only the voice cancellation.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Tune for radiotelephony on the distress and safety frequency in each band in which a false distress alert was transmitted; and”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Radiotelephony 2182 kHz, 4125 kHz, 6215 kHz, 8291 kHz, 12290 kHz, 16420 kHz, and 156.8 MHz.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “Immediately cancel the distress alert by transmitting a DSC self-cancel on all the frequencies where the inadvertent DSC distress alert was transmitted, if the ship station is capable hereof.”

### 33E6

What action should you take after sending a false or mistaken Distress alert on Inmarsat-C?

A. Press the "Distress Hot Keys” then press the "cancel" key.  
**B. Select "Transmit" or “SEND/REC” menu and send a cancellation message via the LES used for the Distress alert.**  
C. Do nothing until the RCC contacts your vessel to determine if the Distress alert was genuine.  
D. Select the cancel false Distress alert option in the Distress Setup Menu and re-transmit the call.  

A false Inmarsat distress alert is cancelled by sending a distress-priority message through the same land earth station that carried the alert, telling the rescue coordination centre it is cancelled and giving the ship's name, call sign and Inmarsat identity.

- [47 CFR §80.335](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.335): “Immediately notify the appropriate rescue coordination center that the alert is cancelled by sending a distress priority message by way of the same land earth station through which the false distress alert was sent.”

## Topic 34. Radio Silence – Resume Traffic

Radio silence protects distress traffic. It's imposed by the station controlling the traffic and lifted only when that station says distress traffic has ended.

- The RCC, the unit coordinating the search, or the coast station involved may impose silence. ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))
- SEELONCE MAYDAY imposes silence by voice. ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))
- Stations not involved may not transmit there until normal working is announced. ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))
- The end is announced with SEELONCE FEENEE (voice) or SILENCE FINI (NBDP). ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))

### 34E1

What is the fundamental purpose for imposing radio silence?

A. To ensure that interference to proprietary communications is minimized.  
B. To ensure that only voice communications can be effected on the Distress frequency or channel.  
C. To ensure that a Distressed vessel will have a "window" twice each hour for transmitting routine messages.  
**D. To mitigate the risk of interference on a frequency or channel being used for emergency communications.**  

Radio silence keeps other stations from interfering with distress traffic. Stations not involved must stay off the frequency being used for the emergency.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “may impose silence on stations which interfere with that traffic”

### 34E2

When can routine communications be resumed after radio silence has been imposed?

**A. Routine communications can resume after the Rescue Coordination Center transmits a message on the frequency or channel being used for emergency communications stating that such traffic has concluded.**  
B. After determining that the frequency or channel appears to be no longer in use.  
C. After determining that geographic distance from the Distress situation will prohibit any other signal from interfering with emergency communications.  
D. If, in the master's opinion, communications on that frequency will interfere with emergency communications.  

Normal traffic can resume only after the station controlling the distress traffic (normally the rescue coordination centre) broadcasts on that frequency that distress traffic has finished. Until then, stations not involved must not transmit there.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “When distress traffic has ceased on frequencies which have been used for distress traffic, the Rescue Coordination Center controlling a search and rescue operation must initiate a message for transmission on these frequencies indicating that distress traffic has finished.”
- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “Until they receive the message indicating that normal working may be resumed”

### 34E3

What is meant by the term "Seelonce Mayday”?

A. Stations remaining off the air to safeguard proprietary information.  
**B. Stations not directly involved with the on-going Distress communications may not transmit on the Distress frequency or channel.**  
C. Two three-minute silent periods, at 15 and 45 minutes after the hour that provide a transmitting "window" for distressed vessels to transmit Distress alerts using J3E.  
D. Communications on a Distress frequency or channel is banned for 24 hours following the cessation of the Distress traffic.  

SEELONCE MAYDAY is the radiotelephone signal used to impose silence. Stations that aren't taking part in the distress traffic must not transmit on that frequency until normal working is announced.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “In radiotelephony, the signal SEELONCE MAYDAY, pronounced as the French expression “silence, m'aider”;”
- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “are forbidden to transmit on the frequencies in which the distress traffic is taking place”

### 34E4

How is "radio silence" imposed?

A. By the Land Earth Station (LES) controlling the Distress communications on that frequency.  
B. By the nearest Public Correspondence Coast Station.  
**C. By the On-scene Coordinator (OSC) or the RCC chosen by the SAR Mission Coordinator.**  
D. By the vessel first responding to the Distress call.  

Silence is imposed by the station controlling the distress traffic: the rescue coordination centre, the unit coordinating the search and rescue operation (such as the on-scene coordinator), or the coast station involved. A land earth station or the first responding vessel doesn't do it.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “The Rescue Coordination Center coordinating distress traffic, the unit coordinating search and rescue operations, or the coast station involved may impose silence”

### 34E5

How are normal working conditions restored on a SITOR (NBDP) frequency on which radio silence had been imposed?

A. The LES that imposed the radio silence must transmit a SITOR (NBDP) message stating "SILENCE FINI".  
B. The Public Correspondence Station (PCS) that imposed the radio silence must transmit a narrow band direct printing message on the Distress frequency stating "SILENCE FINI".  
**C. The RCC or Coast station that imposed the radio silence must transmit a SITOR (NBDP) message stating "SILENCE FINI".**  
D. The High Seas Service (HSS) that imposed the radio silence must transmit a narrow band direct printing message on the Distress frequency stating "SILENCE FINI".  

On NBDP the end of silence is announced with a message ending SILENCE FINI, sent by the station controlling the distress traffic (the RCC, or the coast station acting for it).

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “The words SILENCE FINI.”

### 34E6

How are normal working conditions restored after radio silence has been imposed?

A. All of these answers are correct.  
B. The Land Earth Station (LES) that imposed the radio silence must transmit a voice message on the Distress frequency stating "SILENCE FINI".  
C. The Public Correspondence Station (PCS) that imposed the radio silence must transmit a voice message on the Distress frequency stating "SILENCE FINI".  
**D. The Rescue Coordination Center (RCC) that imposed the radio silence must transmit a voice message on the Distress frequency stating "SEELONCE FEENEE".**  

By voice, the end of radio silence is announced by the rescue coordination centre (or the station controlling the traffic) with a message on the distress frequency ending SEELONCE FEENEE.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “The words SEELONCE FEENEE pronounced as the French words “silence fini””

## Topic 35. Urgency Traffic

Urgency (PAN PAN) is for very urgent messages about the safety of a ship or person, short of the grave and imminent danger that calls for distress.

- The urgency signal is PAN PAN. ([47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131))
- It means a very urgent message about the safety of a mobile unit or person follows. ([47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131))
- By voice, the urgency signal is said three times before the message. ([47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131))
- Distress (MAYDAY) is for grave and imminent danger. ([47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314))

### 35E1

The Radiotelephone Urgency signal is:

A. Mayday  
B. Securite  
**C. Pan Pan**  
D. Seelonce Feenee  

The radiotelephone urgency signal is PAN PAN. MAYDAY is distress, SECURITE is safety, and SEELONCE FEENEE ends radio silence.

- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “The urgency signal consists of the words PAN PAN.”

### 35E2

Which of the following situations would normally use the Urgency priority?

A. A crewmember falling over the side.  
B. A serious medical situation involving a crewmember with potential loss of life.  
C. An important meteorological warning concerning hazardous weather.  
**D. A cargo shift or weather situation considered to be of greater hazard than would justify a Safety priority designation.**  

Urgency is for a very urgent message about the safety of a ship or person that falls short of grave and imminent danger, such as a dangerous cargo shift or weather threat beyond what a Safety message covers. Weather warnings use Safety. A man overboard is one of the DSC natures of distress, and a medical emergency where life is at risk may call for distress rather than urgency.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “The urgency call format and the urgency signal indicate that the calling station has a very urgent message to transmit concerning the safety of a mobile unit or a person.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “110 man overboard.”

### 35E3

Which of the following situations would NOT properly use the Urgency priority?

**A. Abandoning the vessel just before sinking.**  
B. Treatment of a crewmember breaking a leg in a cargo hold.  
C. Leaking oil from a minor tank fracture requiring a mandatory pollution report.  
D. An unexpected deviation in the forecast track line of a typhoon.  

Abandoning a sinking vessel is grave and imminent danger, so it calls for distress (MAYDAY), not urgency. Of the choices it's the one that clearly should not use the urgency priority. The other situations fall short of grave and imminent danger, so distress isn't warranted for any of them. (A warning about a typhoon's track would normally go out as a Safety message.)

- [47 CFR §80.314](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.314): “These distress signals indicate that a mobile station is threatened by grave and imminent danger and requests immediate assistance.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “108 abandoning ship;”
- [47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329): “Messages about meteorological warnings, of cyclones, dangerous ice, dangerous wrecks, or any other imminent danger to marine navigation must be preceded by the safety signal.”

### 35E4

Which of the following situations would normally use the Urgency priority?

A. A collision with the ship taking on water.  
**B. A serious medical situation involving a crewmember.**  
C. Important company communications related to an itinerary change.  
D. Scenarios concerning the Safety of navigation or important meteorological warnings.  

A serious medical situation involving a crewmember is a classic urgency case: a very urgent message about the safety of a person. Navigational or weather warnings use Safety, company messages are routine, and a collision with flooding is distress.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “The urgency call format and the urgency signal indicate that the calling station has a very urgent message to transmit concerning the safety of a mobile unit or a person.”

### 35E5

The Urgency Priority should be used for:

A. Messages concerning the Safety of Life At Sea (SOLAS).  
**B. Messages containing information concerning the Safety of a mobile unit or person.**  
C. Messages detailing important navigational warnings.  
D. Messages concerning On-scene communications.  

The urgency priority is for very urgent messages concerning the safety of a mobile unit (ship, aircraft or vehicle) or a person.

- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “The urgency call format and the urgency signal indicate that the calling station has a very urgent message to transmit concerning the safety of a mobile unit or a person.”

### 35E6

If the Watch Officer hears "PAN PAN" spoken 3 times it means:

A. A navigation or important meteorological warning should follow.  
B. The station is preparing to transmit a Safety message possibly concerning the safety of a mobile unit or person.  
**C. None of these answers is correct.**  
D. A mobile unit is in need of immediate assistance.  

PAN PAN spoken three times means an urgency message follows: a very urgent message about the safety of a ship or person. Choice A describes a Safety message, B wrongly calls it a safety message, and D describes distress. None matches.

- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “In radiotelephony, the urgency message must be preceded by the urgency signal, repeated three times, and the identification of the transmitting station.”
- [47 CFR §80.1131](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1131): “The urgency call format and the urgency signal indicate that the calling station has a very urgent message to transmit concerning the safety of a mobile unit or a person.”

## Topic 36. Safety Traffic

Safety (SECURITE) messages carry important navigational and meteorological warnings.

- The safety signal is SECURITE, spoken three times. ([47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329))
- It announces an important navigational or meteorological warning. ([47 CFR §80.1133](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1133))
- Dangers to navigation such as wrecks or ice are reported with the safety signal. ([47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329))

### 36E1

When the GMDSS Radio Operator on watch hears "Securite" spoken three times, he can expect to receive the following information:

A. The safety of vessel or person is in jeopardy.  
**B. A message concerning the Safety of navigation.**  
C. A vessel is in need of immediate assistance.  
D. A Coast Station sending an important traffic list.  

SECURITE spoken three times announces a safety message: an important navigational or meteorological warning.

- [47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329): “In radiotelephony, the safety signal consists of the word SECURITE, pronounced as in French, spoken three times and transmitted before the call.”
- [47 CFR §80.1133](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1133): “The safety call format or the safety signal indicates that the calling station has an important navigational or meteorological warning to transmit.”

### 36E2

Which of the following situations would normally use the Voice designation "Securite"?

A. Messages concerning the Safety of Life At Sea (SOLAS).  
B. Messages containing information concerning the Safety of a mobile unit or person.  
**C. Messages detailing important navigational warnings.**  
D. Messages concerning On-scene communications.  

SECURITE is used for important navigational and meteorological warnings. Messages about the safety of a ship or person use the urgency signal.

- [47 CFR §80.1133](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1133): “The safety call format or the safety signal indicates that the calling station has an important navigational or meteorological warning to transmit.”

### 36E3

Which of the following situations would normally use the Safety priority?

A. Treatment of a crewmember with a broken leg that is not life-threatening.  
B. Treatment of a crewmember with a serious cardiac emergency.  
C. A fire in the generator flat/spaces.  
**D. Loss of 5 containers with lashing gear over the side.**  

Containers lost overboard are a danger to navigation for other ships, so they're reported with a Safety (SECURITE) message. A broken leg or a cardiac emergency concerns the safety of a person, which is urgency (or distress if life is in grave and imminent danger).

- [47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329): “Messages about meteorological warnings, of cyclones, dangerous ice, dangerous wrecks, or any other imminent danger to marine navigation must be preceded by the safety signal.”

### 36E4

Which of the following situations would normally use the Safety priority?

**A. Important navigational or meteorological warnings.**  
B. A serious medical situation involving a crewmember.  
C. An unanticipated warning related to piracy or terrorism.  
D. Grounding in a way that could lead to imminent danger to the ship’s crew.  

The Safety priority is for important navigational or meteorological warnings. A serious medical case is urgency, and a grounding putting the crew in imminent danger is distress.

- [47 CFR §80.1133](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1133): “The safety call format or the safety signal indicates that the calling station has an important navigational or meteorological warning to transmit.”

### 36E5

The Radiotelephone Safety signal is:

A. "Safety Safety Safety"  
B. "Pan Pan" repeated 3 times  
C. "Securite Securite" repeated 3 times  
**D. "Securite" repeated 3 times**  

The radiotelephone safety signal is SECURITE, spoken three times.

- [47 CFR §80.329](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.329): “In radiotelephony, the safety signal consists of the word SECURITE, pronounced as in French, spoken three times and transmitted before the call.”

### 36E6

Which of the following situations would normally use the Safety priority?

**A. A scenario concerning an important navigational or meteorological warning.**  
B. A serious medical situation involving a crewmember.  
C. A crewmember falling over the side.  
D. Important company communications involving weather routing.  

The Safety priority is for important navigational or meteorological warnings. A serious medical case is urgency, a crewmember overboard is a distress situation (man overboard), and company weather routing is routine traffic.

- [47 CFR §80.1133](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1133): “The safety call format or the safety signal indicates that the calling station has an important navigational or meteorological warning to transmit.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.493-16, Digital selective-calling system for use in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.493/en): “110 man overboard.”

## Topic 37. Frequencies

Part 80 lists the GMDSS frequencies by purpose: alerting, on-scene communications, locating signals, MSI and general distress traffic.

- Alerting: 406 MHz EPIRBs, Inmarsat 1626.5–1645.5 MHz, Channel 70 and six MF/HF DSC frequencies. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))
- On-scene: Channel 16, 2182 kHz voice and 2174.5 kHz NBDP. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))
- There are seven DSC distress frequencies in all. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))

### 37E1

Which of the following frequencies and modes is allocated for Distress alerting in GMDSS?

**A. 406 MHz via EPIRB, 1626.5-1645.5 MHz via Inmarsat and Channel 70 DSC plus six (6) MF/HF DSC frequencies.**  
B. 1626.5-1645.5 MHz via Inmarsat, VHF CH-16 plus six (6) MF/HF DSC frequencies, 406 MHz via EPIRB.  
C. Channel 70 DSC plus six (6) MF/HF DSC frequencies, 7 voice follow-on and 6 telex follow-on frequencies.  
D. Mayday on VHF Channel 70 and the other six voice follow-on frequencies.  

The GMDSS alerting frequencies are 406 MHz for satellite EPIRBs, 1626.5–1645.5 MHz for Inmarsat ship earth stations, and DSC on Channel 70 plus six MF/HF DSC frequencies. Channel 16 is for voice traffic, not alerting.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Alerting: 406.0-406.1 EPIRBs 406.0-406.1 MHz (Earth-to-space).”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “INMARSAT Ship Earth Stations capable of voice and/or direct printing 1626.5-1645.5 MHz (Earth-to-space). VHF DSC Ch. 70 156.525 MHz.”
- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “MF/HF DSC 2 2187.5 kHz 3, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, and 16804.5 kHz.”

### 37E2

Which of the following frequencies is designated for On-scene Distress and Safety communications?

A. 4209.5 kHz  
**B. 2174.5 kHz**  
C. 518.0 kHz  
D. 490.0 kHz  

2174.5 kHz is the MF NBDP frequency for on-scene communications, alongside 2182 kHz and Channel 16 for voice. 518 and 490 kHz are NAVTEX broadcast frequencies, and 4209.5 kHz is an MSI warnings frequency.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “On-scene communications: VHF Ch.16 156.8 MHz. MF Radiotelephony 2182 kHz. NBDP 2174.5 kHz.”

### 37E3

Which channel is designated for GMDSS Digital Selective Calling?

A. Ch-06  
B. Ch-16  
**C. Ch-70**  
D. Ch-83  

Channel 70 (156.525 MHz) is the VHF channel for DSC. Channel 16 is voice distress and calling, Channel 6 intership safety, and 83 a working channel.

- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “DSC on the frequency 156.525 MHz (channel 70)”

### 37E4

How many MF frequencies are available for DSC Distress related calls?

A. Five  
B. Four  
C. Two  
**D. One**  

There is one MF DSC distress frequency: 2187.5 kHz.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

### 37E5

How many HF frequencies are available for DSC Distress related calls?

**A. Five**  
B. Four  
C. Two  
D. One  

There are five HF DSC distress frequencies: 4207.5, 6312, 8414.5, 12577 and 16804.5 kHz.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

### 37E6

How many frequencies are available under GMDSS for DSC Distress-related calls?

A. Six  
**B. Seven**  
C. Four  
D. Five  

There are seven DSC distress frequencies in all: one VHF (Channel 70), one MF (2187.5 kHz) and five HF.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “DSC 2187.5 kHz, 4207.5 kHz, 6312 kHz, 8414.5 kHz, 12577 kHz, 16804.5 kHz, and 156.525 MHz.”

## Topic 38. Other Procedures

Other distress procedures: who controls distress traffic, which NBDP mode to use, and what to take when abandoning ship.

- The RCC coordinates distress traffic or appoints another station to do it. ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))
- NBDP uses ARQ with coast stations, FEC for broadcasts. ([47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125))
- Keep a SART's antenna at least 1 m high for good detection range. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.628-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.628/en))

### 38E1

Which of the following steps should be taken, if possible, when the vessel must be abandoned because of a Distress situation?

A. Alert the U.S. Coast Guard by using the survival craft's portable Inmarsat unit.  
B. Program the SART and EPIRB to transmit the vessel's location and situation.  
C. No additional steps are needed as the SART and EPIRB will both automatically float free and operate properly.  
**D. Secure the EPIRB to the survival craft and mount the SART in a position to maximize its elevation.**  

When abandoning ship, take the EPIRB into the survival craft and keep it attached there, and mount the SART as high as possible. Its radar detection range drops sharply if it lies on the floor or in the water. EPIRBs and SARTs aren't "programmed" with the situation, and you can't rely on float-free alone.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.628-5, Technical characteristics for search and rescue radar transponders](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.628/en): “Tests have also shown the importance of maintaining a SART antenna height of at least 1 m.”
- [47 CFR §80.1085](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1085): “capable of being carried by one person into a survival craft”

### 38E2

Which action is the most appropriate action for a GMDSS radio Operator to take in a Distress situation where immediate help is needed, but the vessel is not sinking nor needs to be abandoned?

**A. Transmit Distress calls by HF/MF/VHF DSC or Inmarsat.**  
B. Switch off EPIRB and SART manually.  
C. Notify the RCC (Rescue Coordination Center) through VHF FM on channel 13.  
D. Transmit Distress calls by activating the radiotelegraph automatic alarm signal.  

If the ship or a person is in distress and needs immediate assistance, send a distress alert by DSC (VHF, MF or HF) or Inmarsat. Switching off the EPIRB and SART does nothing to call for help, Channel 13 is bridge-to-bridge rather than a way to reach the RCC, and the radiotelegraph auto alarm is obsolete.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.541-11, Operational procedures for the use of digital selective-calling equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.541/en): “A distress alert should be transmitted if, in the opinion of the Master, the ship or a person is in distress and requires immediate assistance.”
- [47 CFR §80.1111](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1111): “on the distress and safety frequencies in the MF, HF, and VHF bands using digital selective calling”

### 38E3

DSC is used primarily to:

A. Receive weather warnings, navigational notices and other Maritime Safety Information.  
**B. Transmit and receive Distress, Urgency and Safety alerts and routine calls to and from other ships and coast radio stations.**  
C. Provide routine communications with the ship owner.  
D. Report ship's position to search-and-rescue authorities via satellite.  

DSC is used to call stations automatically: distress, urgency and safety alerts and routine calls between ships and coast stations. MSI comes by NAVTEX and EGC, and DSC isn't a satellite position-reporting system.

- [47 CFR §80.5](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.5): “used to establish contact with a station or group of stations automatically by means of radio”

### 38E4

GMDSS vessels equipped for Sea Areas A2, A3 or A4 must maintain a continuous DSC watch on 2187.5 kHz.

A. Only in areas beyond Inmarsat coverage.  
B. Only outside of areas covered by VHF-DSC.  
**C. At all times when underway.**  
D. When directed to do so by a cognizant rescue authority.  

Ships fitted with MF DSC for sea areas A2, A3 or A4 must keep a continuous DSC watch on 2187.5 kHz whenever they're at sea.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “While at sea, all ships must maintain a continuous watch:”
- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On the distress and safety DSC frequency 2187.5 kHz”

### 38E5

Which statement is true regarding Distress communications under GMDSS?

A. Distress communications by (SITOR) NBDP should be in the BFEC mode when in two-way communications with the Coast Guard or other coast radio stations.  
B. The Rescue Coordination Center may not appoint another station to coordinate Distress traffic relating to the incident.  
**C. The Rescue Coordination Center (RCC) is responsible for controlling a search and rescue operation, will coordinate the Distress traffic relating to the incident and may appoint another station to manage the Distress traffic.**  
D. Initial Distress communications by (SITOR) NBDP should be in the ARQ mode to broadcast them to the Coast Guard, other coast radio stations and other ship stations.  

The rescue coordination centre controlling a search and rescue operation coordinates the related distress traffic and may appoint another station to do so. For NBDP, ARQ is for direct communication with coast stations and FEC for broadcasts, so A and D have the modes reversed.

- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “The Rescue Coordination Center responsible for controlling a search and rescue operation will also coordinate the distress traffic relating to the incident or may appoint another station to do so.”
- [47 CFR §80.1125](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1125): “Distress communications by direct-printing telegraphy should be in the ARQ mode when ships are communicating directly to the Coast Guard or other coast stations”

### 38E6

When operating in coastal waters (sea area A1), a GMDSS-equipped vessel must:

A. Maintain a continuous DSC watch on 8514.5 kHz.  
B. Maintain a continuous aural watch on 2182.0 kHz.  
C. Maintain a continuous DSC watch on VHF channel 16.  
**D. Maintain a continuous DSC watch on VHF channel 70.**  

Every GMDSS ship keeps a continuous DSC watch on VHF Channel 70. Channel 16 is a voice (listening) watch, and the MF/HF DSC watches apply to ships with MF or MF/HF installations.

- [47 CFR §80.1123](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1123): “On VHF DSC channel 70”

## Topic 39. SART- Activation and Survival Craft Operation

A search and rescue transponder (SART) sits in standby until a searching ship's 9 GHz (3 cm, X-band) radar triggers it, then replies so the survival craft shows on that radar. Height and upright mounting make a big difference to range.

- A SART works in the 9200–9500 MHz band and answers X-band radar only. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.628-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.628/en))
- It lets survivors know, by light or sound, when a radar has triggered it. ([IMO Resolution A.802(19)](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/AssemblyDocuments/A.802(19).pdf))
- Mount it at least 1 m high; lying flat in the boat cuts the range sharply. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.628-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.628/en))
- It lasts 96 hours in standby, then 8 hours of replies. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.628-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.628/en))

### 39F1

What indication is given to the personnel in a survival craft of the approach of SAR craft?

A. The Satellite EPIRB will change its strobe light pattern to indicate radar interrogation.  
B. The SART informs survivors when the SART switches to the "standby" mode.  
**C. The SART may provide a visual or audible indication of interrogation by a 3-CM radar.**  
D. The AIS SART will alarm to indicate that SAR craft with radars are getting close.  

A SART must have a visual or audible indicator (or both) that shows it's working and tells survivors when a searching ship's 3 cm (9 GHz, X-band) radar has triggered it. EPIRBs don't react to radar, and an AIS-SART doesn't detect radars.

- [IMO Resolution A.802(19), Performance standards for survival craft radar transponders](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/KnowledgeCentre/IndexofIMOResolutions/AssemblyDocuments/A.802(19).pdf): “be equipped with a means which is either visual or audible, or both visual and audible, to indicate correct operation and to alert survivors to the fact that a radar has triggered the SART”

### 39F2

Which of the following would aid the detection of a SART's signal?

A. The rescue personnel were monitoring the 3-CM radar and the SART was mounted improperly in the lifeboat.  
B. The SART was mounted improperly in the survival craft and rescue personnel were monitoring the 10-CM radar.  
C. The rescue personnel were monitoring the 10-CM radar and the SART was properly mounted in the lifeboat.  
**D. The SART was properly mounted in the lifeboat and rescue personnel were monitoring the 3-CM radar.**
