- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “In order to avoid erroneous reception and interference of transmissions from two stations having the same transmitter identification character, it is necessary to ensure that such stations have a large geographical separation.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Experience indicates that the required range of 250 to 400 nautical miles will normally be attained by transmitted power of no more than 1 kW during daylight with a 60% reduction during night conditions.”

### 51H2

When do NAVTEX broadcasts typically achieve maximum transmitting range?

A. Local noontime  
B. Afternoon  
C. Sunset  
**D. Middle of the night**  

NAVTEX uses medium frequency (518 kHz), which travels farthest at night, when the ionosphere stops absorbing MF signals. That's why stations reduce power at night to avoid interfering with each other.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Experience indicates that the required range of 250 to 400 nautical miles will normally be attained by transmitted power of no more than 1 kW during daylight with a 60% reduction during night conditions.”

### 51H3

What should a GMDSS Radio Operator do if a NAVTEX warning message is received but it contains too many errors to be usable?

**A. Do nothing. Vital NAVTEX messages will be repeated on the next scheduled broadcast.**  
B. Contact the NAVAREA coordinator and request a repeat broadcast.  
C. Initiate a request for Category A, B, L and D messages.  
D. Listen to appropriate VHF weather channel for repeat warnings.  

If a NAVTEX message is garbled, wait for the next scheduled broadcast. Vital and important warnings are repeated at every scheduled transmission while they remain valid. NAVTEX is receive-only, so there's no way to request a repeat.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Both VITAL and IMPORTANT messages should be repeated at each scheduled transmission time slot, if the situation is still valid.”

### 51H4

Which of these cannot happen when a paper model NAVTEX receiver runs out of paper?

A. The unit is unable to print messages and all subsequent MSI broadcasts may be missed until the paper is replaced.  
**B. The system will automatically change from receiving MSI by NAVTEX to receiving it by SafetyNETTM so that no messages will be lost.**  
C. It may give off either an audible and/or visual alarm.  
D. MSI messages may be missed because the unit cannot print them out.  

Running out of paper can't make the receiver switch to SafetyNET: NAVTEX and SafetyNET are separate systems. Without paper, the unit can't print, messages may be missed, and it may sound or show an alarm.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 51H5

Which of the following is the primary frequency that is used exclusively for NAVTEX broadcasts internationally?

A. 2187.5 kHz  
**B. 518 kHz**  
C. 4209.5 kHz  
D. VHF channel 16 when the vessel is sailing in Sea Area A1, and 2187.5 kHz when in Sea Area A2.  

518 kHz is the international NAVTEX frequency. 4209.5 kHz is used for some NAVTEX-type warnings, and 2187.5 kHz and Channel 16 aren't NAVTEX frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “International NAVTEX 518 kHz.”

### 51H6

What is the transmitting range of most NAVTEX stations?

A. Typically 50-100 nautical miles (90-180 km) from shore.  
B. Typically upwards of 1000 nautical miles (1800 km) during the daytime.  
**C. Typically 200-400 nautical miles (360-720 km).**  
D. It is limited to line-of-sight or about 30 nautical miles (54 km).  

NAVTEX stations typically cover a few hundred nautical miles: IMO guidance gives a required range of 250 to 400 nautical miles.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Experience indicates that the required range of 250 to 400 nautical miles will normally be attained by transmitted power of no more than 1 kW during daylight with a 60% reduction during night conditions.”

## Topic 52. Navtex-2: Programming

A NAVTEX receiver lets you choose which stations and message categories to receive, but the most important categories can't be switched off.

- Stations are selected by their one-letter identifier. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))
- Categories are selected by their subject letter. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))
- Navigational warnings, meteorological warnings and SAR information can't be deselected. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))

### 52H1

How is a NAVTEX receiver programmed to reject certain messages?

A. The transmitting station's two-digit identification can be entered to de-select reception of its broadcasts.  
**B. By choosing a message category's single letter (A-Z) identifier and then deselecting or deactivating.**  
C. By entering the SELCAL of the NAVTEX transmitting station.  
D. By pressing "00" in the transmitter's ID block.  

Each NAVTEX message type has a single letter (A to Z), its subject indicator, and you reject a type by deselecting its letter. Stations are identified by a single letter too, not a two-digit code or a SELCAL.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Some subject indicator characters can be used to reject messages concerning certain subjects which may not be required by the ship”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “The transmitter identification character (B1) is a single letter which is allocated to each transmitter. It is used to identify the broadcasts which are to be accepted by the receiver and those to be rejected, and also the time slot for the transmission.”

### 52H2

How can reception of certain NAVTEX broadcasts be prevented?

A. Stations are limited to daytime operation only.  
B. Coordinating reception with published broadcast schedules.  
**C. The receiver can be programmed to reject certain stations and message categories.**  
D. Automatic receiver desensitization during night hours.  

A NAVTEX receiver can be programmed to accept or reject broadcasts from particular stations (by their identification letter) and particular message categories (by their subject letter).

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “The transmitter identification character (B1) is a single letter which is allocated to each transmitter. It is used to identify the broadcasts which are to be accepted by the receiver and those to be rejected, and also the time slot for the transmission.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Some subject indicator characters can be used to reject messages concerning certain subjects which may not be required by the ship”

### 52H3

Which of the following statements is true?

A. No NAVTEX receiver can be programmed to reject category A, B, D and L messages since they are mandatory to be received via NAVTEX.  
B. Upon entering a new NAVTEX station's broadcast range, the GMDSS Radio Operator enters the station's SELCAL number.  
C. The GMDSS Radio Operator can select the "None" option in the message category menu.  
**D. A GMDSS Radio Operator may choose to program certain NAVTEX receivers to reject category A, B, D and L messages if they are being received by another MSI system.**  

_No explanation published._

### 52H4

What means are used to prevent the reception of unwanted broadcasts by vessels utilizing the NAVTEX system?

**A. Programming the receiver to reject certain stations and message categories.**  
B. Operating the receiver only during daytime hours.  
C. Coordinating reception with published broadcast schedules.  
D. Automatic receiver de-sensitization during night hours.  

Unwanted NAVTEX broadcasts are kept out by programming the receiver to reject certain stations and message categories. Receivers aren't run on schedules or desensitized at night.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “The transmitter identification character (B1) is a single letter which is allocated to each transmitter. It is used to identify the broadcasts which are to be accepted by the receiver and those to be rejected, and also the time slot for the transmission.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Some subject indicator characters can be used to reject messages concerning certain subjects which may not be required by the ship”

### 52H5

What statement is true regarding the control the operator can exercise over the NAVTEX receiver's operation?

A. The operator can set the unit to automatically reject any and all categories of messages if the ship desires to not receive them.  
B. Upon entering a coastal area for the first time, the operator enters code KK to indicate "ready to receive NAVTEX".  
C. To reduce the number of messages, the operator can select code 00 to indicate "not in coastal passage".  
**D. The operator can set most units to reject all messages except navigation, meteorological warnings, and search and rescue messages. If the unit will reject such messages it may be unsafe to do so.**  

Operators can deselect most message categories, but navigational warnings, meteorological warnings and search and rescue information (categories A, B and D, plus L) are mandatory and can't be deselected on current receivers. Rejecting them would be unsafe.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “which have been allocated for navigational warnings, meteorological warnings, search and rescue information, acts of piracy warnings, tsunamis and other natural phenomena, is mandatory and cannot be deselected on the NAVTEX receiver”

### 52H6

Which messages are mandatory to be received and should not typically be rejected or disabled by the operator of a NAVTEX receiver?

**A. Navigational warnings, meteorological warnings, SAR information.**  
B. Meteorological warnings, SAR information, Pilot Service Messages.  
C. Meteorological warnings, meteorological forecasts, navigational warnings.  
D. SAR information, navigational warnings, ice reports.  

Navigational warnings (A), meteorological warnings (B) and search and rescue information (D) are mandatory and can't be deselected on the NAVTEX receiver. Forecasts, pilot messages and ice reports can be.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “which have been allocated for navigational warnings, meteorological warnings, search and rescue information, acts of piracy warnings, tsunamis and other natural phenomena, is mandatory and cannot be deselected on the NAVTEX receiver”

## Topic 53. Navtex-3: Message Format

Every NAVTEX message starts with a four-character identity: station letter, subject letter and a two-digit number. It's broadcast in FEC mode.

- The identity is B1 (station), B2 (subject) and B3B4 (number). ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))
- Messages already received aren't printed again for 72 hours. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))
- Messages numbered 00 can't be rejected. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf))
- NAVTEX uses the collective B-mode (FEC) of direct printing. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.540-2](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.540/en))

### 53H1

The NAVTEX message header contains the following?

**A. The first letter (from A to Z) indicates the NAVTEX transmitting station.**  
B. A two-digit number (01-99) indicates the NAVTEX message category.  
C. Message numbers include a date/time group, along with the transmitting station's numerical ID.  
D. None of these answers is correct.  

The first character of a NAVTEX message identity (B1) is a single letter, A to X in practice, identifying the transmitting station. The second letter (B2) gives the message category, and the last two digits are the message number.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “The transmitter identification character (B1) is a single letter which is allocated to each transmitter. It is used to identify the broadcasts which are to be accepted by the receiver and those to be rejected, and also the time slot for the transmission.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “B1 Transmitter identification character B2 Subject indicator character B3B4 Message numbering characters”

### 53H2

If the Inmarsat-C terminal is inoperative but the vessel remains within NAVTEX coverage -- which of the following message categories should not be disabled by the GMDSS Radio Operator?

A. Navigational warnings, meteorological warnings and metrological forecasts.  
**B. Meteorological warnings, Search and Rescue information and Navigational warnings.**  
C. Search and Rescue information, navigational warnings and other electronic navaid messages.  
D. Search and Rescue information, Meteorological warnings and ice reports.  

Meteorological warnings, search and rescue information and navigational warnings (A, B and D) are mandatory NAVTEX categories and can't be deselected, whatever other MSI equipment the ship has.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “which have been allocated for navigational warnings, meteorological warnings, search and rescue information, acts of piracy warnings, tsunamis and other natural phenomena, is mandatory and cannot be deselected on the NAVTEX receiver”

### 53H3

How are NAVTEX broadcasts transmitted?

A. NAVTEX is transmitted by commercial coast radio stations following their traffic lists.  
B. NAVTEX is transmitted only when an Urgency or Distress broadcast is warranted.  
**C. Using FEC techniques.**  
D. No more often than every two hours and should immediately follow the radiotelephone silent periods.  

NAVTEX is sent in the collective B-mode of narrow-band direct printing, which is forward error correction (FEC): a one-way broadcast to many receivers.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.540-2, Operational and technical characteristics for an automated direct-printing telegraph system for promulgation of navigational and meteorological warnings and urgent information to ships](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.540/en): “The signals transmitted should be in conformity with the collective B-mode of the direct-printing system”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5, Direct-printing telegraph equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en): “Mode B, forward error correction (FEC)”

### 53H4

What determines whether a NAVTEX receiver prints a particular type of message content from a programmed NAVTEX station?

A. The serial number and type of message have already been received but additional printouts are generated to ensure receipt aboard the vessel.  
B. The subject indicator has been programmed for rejection by the operator but the message contains a priority override print command.  
C. The transmitting station ID covering your area has been programmed for rejection by the operator or has not been previously received.  
**D. The serial number and type of message has not been previously received or the subject indicator has not been programmed for rejection.**  

A NAVTEX receiver prints a message if it hasn't already received that message identity (stored for 72 hours) and the message's subject letter hasn't been deselected. Repeats of a message already received aren't printed again.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “When a message is received for the first time by a NAVTEX receiver, the message identity is recorded and stored in the memory for 72 hours.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Some subject indicator characters can be used to reject messages concerning certain subjects which may not be required by the ship”

### 53H5

Which information determines if a NAVTEX message is to be rejected?

**A. The second letter (from A to Z) in the header indicating the type of message.**  
B. Transmitter identity (numerals from 1 to 26 identifying transmitting station within the NAVAREA).  
C. The Answerback of the receiving station has not been entered in the NAVTEX receiver.  
D. Only messages having a serial number 00 are rejected.  

The second letter of the NAVTEX header (B2, the subject indicator) gives the message type, and the receiver uses it to accept or reject messages. Stations are identified by letters, not numbers, and messages numbered 00 can't be rejected.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Some subject indicator characters can be used to reject messages concerning certain subjects which may not be required by the ship”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Messages broadcast using NAVTEX number B3B4 = 00 cannot be rejected”

### 53H6

NAVTEX broadcasts are sent:

A. Immediately following traffic lists.  
**B. In categories of messages indicated by a single letter or identifier.**  
C. On request of maritime mobile stations.  
D. Regularly, after the radiotelephone silent periods.  

NAVTEX broadcasts are grouped into message categories, each identified by a single letter (the subject indicator). They're sent in each station's time slot, not on request or after traffic lists.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Information is grouped by subject in the NAVTEX broadcast and each subject group is allocated a B2 subject indicator character.”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1403/Rev.1, Revised NAVTEX Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/MSC.1-Circ.1403-Rev.1%20-%20REVISED%20NAVTEX%20MANUAL%20(Secretariat).pdf): “Each transmitter identification character is allocated a maximum transmission time of 10 minutes every 4 hours.”

## Topic 54. SafetyNETTM -1: Operations

SafetyNET is Inmarsat-C's satellite service for maritime safety information, received by the terminal's EGC receiver. It also covers coastal areas without NAVTEX.

- SafetyNET is a satellite direct-printing service for MSI. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- It's part of the Inmarsat-C enhanced group call (EGC) system. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- It also covers coastal areas not served by NAVTEX. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- Distress and urgency messages trigger an alarm that must be reset by hand. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))

### 54H1

Where NAVTEX cannot be feasibly established, what system can be implemented to provide an automated service in coastal waters to receive MSI?

A. AMVER  
B. VHF DSC  
C. ARQ SITOR (NBDP)  
**D. SafetyNETTM**  

SafetyNET, the Inmarsat-C enhanced group call MSI service, can broadcast MSI to coastal warning areas that the international NAVTEX service doesn't cover.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “it also provides the means of broadcasting MSI to coastal warning areas not covered by the International NAVTEX service”

### 54H2

What action should a GMDSS Radio Operator take when SafetyNETTM Distress or Urgency messages are received by the vessel's EGC receiver?

**A. Aural and/or visual alarms are activated and require manual deactivation.**  
B. No immediate action is required, as an audible tone will be generated at the beginning and end of the transmission and a paper printout of the message will be generated.  
C. No immediate action is required by the operator, since the transmission will be automatically acknowledged by the receiving vessel.  
D. A periodic alarm tone will be heard until the radio operator prints the message from the unit's memory.  

SafetyNET distress and urgency messages set off an audible and visual alarm at the navigating position. The alarm can't be disabled and must be reset manually, so the operator has to respond and read the message.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “It is not possible to disable this alarm and it is only possible to reset it manually”

### 54H3

What system can provide an automated service in coastal waters where it may not be feasible to establish the NAVTEX service or where shipping density is too low to warrant its implementation?

A. AMVER  
**B. SafetyNETTM**  
C. VHF DSC  
D. ARQ SITOR (NBDP)  

SafetyNET can broadcast MSI to coastal warning areas not covered by NAVTEX, for example where NAVTEX isn't feasible or traffic doesn't justify it.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “it also provides the means of broadcasting MSI to coastal warning areas not covered by the International NAVTEX service”

### 54H4

Aboard ship, SafetyNETTM messages can be received by which equipment/methods?

A. VHF DSC on the weather channels.  
B. NAVTEX Receiver on 518 kHz or the Tropical Navtex frequency.  
**C. EGC receiver of the vessel's Inmarsat-C SES.**  
D. HF SITOR (NBDP) MSI frequencies.  

SafetyNET messages are received by the EGC (enhanced group call) receiver of the ship's Inmarsat-C ship earth station. NAVTEX, HF NBDP and VHF DSC are separate systems.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “It has been developed as a safety service of the Inmarsat C Enhanced Group Call (EGC) system”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “Aboard ships, messages are received by type-approved Inmarsat ship earth station EGC terminals.”

### 54H5

SafetyNETTM messages can be received by which of the following shipboard equipment?

A. NAVTEX  
B. MF and HF SITOR (NBDP)  
**C. Inmarsat-C EGC receiver**  
D. Inmarsat F77 EGC receiver  

SafetyNET is an Inmarsat-C enhanced group call service, so it's received on an Inmarsat-C EGC receiver.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “It has been developed as a safety service of the Inmarsat C Enhanced Group Call (EGC) system”

### 54H6

Maritime Safety Information is promulgated via satellite through which system?

A. AMVER  
B. NAVTEX  
C. Inmarsat-M SES  
**D. SafetyNETTM**  

SafetyNET, Inmarsat's enhanced group call service, is the satellite MSI service among these choices (Iridium now offers a similar service). NAVTEX is terrestrial, AMVER is a ship reporting system, and Inmarsat-M is a voice terminal.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”
- [National Weather Service, METAREAs](https://www.weather.gov/marine/metarea): “The satellite broadcasts of MSI are handled by two companies, Inmarsat and Iridium”

## Topic 55. SafetyNETTM -2: Information

SafetyNET carries MSI (warnings, forecasts and SAR information) to NAVAREAs, coastal areas or user-defined areas. The terminal needs an accurate position to select messages for its area.

- SafetyNET carries navigational and meteorological warnings, forecasts and SAR information. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- If the position is stale or unknown, the terminal accepts messages for the whole ocean region. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))

### 55H1

SafetyNETTM promulgates what type of information?

A. Traffic Lists  
B. News advisories  
**C. MSI**  
D. MARAD  

SafetyNET broadcasts maritime safety information (MSI): navigational and meteorological warnings, forecasts, SAR information and other urgent safety messages.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”

### 55H2

What kind(s) of broadcasts are not available through SafetyNETTM?

A. MSI and messages to specific geographic areas.  
B. Storm warnings  
C. Distress and Urgency bulletins  
**D. Vessel traffic lists**  

SafetyNET carries MSI, storm warnings and distress and urgency broadcasts to geographic areas, but not vessel traffic lists, which are a commercial coast station service.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “The area may be fixed, as in the case of a NAVAREA/METAREA or coastal warning area, or a user-defined area (circular or rectangular). A user-defined area is used for messages, such as a local storm warning or a shore-to-ship distress alert, for which it is inappropriate to alert ships in an entire satellite Ocean Region or NAVAREA/METAREA.”

### 55H3

Which satellite system promulgates Maritime Safety Information?

**A. Inmarsat-C SafetyNETTM**  
B. AMVER  
C. NAVTEX  
D. Inmarsat-M SES  

Inmarsat-C SafetyNET is the satellite MSI service among these choices (Iridium now runs a similar service). NAVTEX is terrestrial MF, AMVER is a ship reporting system, and Inmarsat-M is a voice terminal.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”
- [National Weather Service, METAREAs](https://www.weather.gov/marine/metarea): “The satellite broadcasts of MSI are handled by two companies, Inmarsat and Iridium”

### 55H4

What information is promulgated by the international SafetyNETTM?

A. Traffic Lists  
**B. MSI**  
C. Priority Messages  
D. MARAD  

SafetyNET broadcasts maritime safety information (MSI).

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”

### 55H5

To receive all mandatory MSI using the SafetyNETTM system the vessel must:

A. Notify the NAVAREA coordinator you are using SafetyNETTM for the receipt of MSI (Maritime Safety Information).  
**B. Log-in and ensure the position is accurate to receive MSI for the NAVAREA the vessel is currently within.**  
C. Set the receiver to your destination Inmarsat Ocean Region.  
D. Notify the NAVAREA coordinator you are using SafetyNETTM for the receipt of MSI (Maritime Safety Information) and set the receiver to your destination Ocean Region.  

To receive the MSI meant for your area, the Inmarsat-C terminal must be working and logged in to the right ocean region, with an accurate position so it accepts broadcasts for the NAVAREA you're in. You don't notify the NAVAREA coordinator, and setting it to your destination region would miss current warnings.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “The position information in Inmarsat SESs is updated automatically from integrated navigational receivers”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “If the position has not been updated for more than 12 hours or is unknown, all SafetyNET messages within the entire satellite Ocean Region will be printed or stored in the Inmarsat SES.”

### 55H6

In using SafetyNETTM for the receipt of MSI (Maritime Safety Information):

A. Only unscheduled Urgency and Distress messages will be received if the Inmarsat-C SES is not logged in.  
B. Both scheduled MSI and unscheduled Urgency and Distress messages will be received if the Inmarsat-C SES is logged in.  
**C. All of these answers are correct.**  
D. The Inmarsat-C SES must have Enhanced Group Calling (EGC) capability to receive MSI.  

_No explanation published._

## Topic 56. Enhanced Group Calling (EGC)

Enhanced Group Call (EGC) is the Inmarsat-C broadcast system behind SafetyNET. It can address fixed areas such as NAVAREAs or any user-defined area.

- EGC can target fixed areas or user-defined circles and rectangles. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- Messages come from registered information providers via a land earth station. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))
- Some warnings are automatically repeated 6 minutes later. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))

### 56H1

Over what system are Enhanced Group Calls transmitted?

A. COSPAS satellite  
**B. Inmarsat satellite**  
C. HF SITOR (NBDP) shore stations  
D. NAVTEX shore stations  

Enhanced Group Calls are broadcast over Inmarsat satellites. EGC is part of the Inmarsat-C system.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “It has been developed as a safety service of the Inmarsat C Enhanced Group Call (EGC) system”

### 56H2

How is a MSI (Maritime Safety Information) broadcast received by an Inmarsat-C SES that is engaged in communications?

A. The broadcast message is missed and the Radio Operator must request a retransmission.  
B. The broadcast message is stored in the EGC memory and will automatically be printed at the conclusion of the ongoing traffic.  
**C. There is no loss of information since broadcasts of "vital" messages will be repeated.**  
D. The radio operator can request retransmission of messages missing from numeric serial number succession.  

An Inmarsat-C terminal that's busy may miss a broadcast, but vital messages are repeated: some are echoed a few minutes after the first broadcast, and warnings still in force are repeated at later scheduled broadcasts.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “will initiate an automatic repeated broadcast 6 minutes after the initial scheduled or unscheduled broadcast”
- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “MSI and SAR related information broadcasts will only be received when the terminal is idle.”

### 56H3

Which of the following provides a unique automated system capable of addressing messages to predetermined groups of ships or all vessels in both fixed and variable geographic areas?

A. NAVTEX  
B. AFRTS  
C. NAVAREAs  
**D. EGC**  

The EGC system can address messages to fixed areas (such as NAVAREAs) or to variable, user-defined areas. That's unique to EGC: NAVTEX broadcasts to fixed station areas only.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “The area may be fixed, as in the case of a NAVAREA/METAREA or coastal warning area, or a user-defined area (circular or rectangular). A user-defined area is used for messages, such as a local storm warning or a shore-to-ship distress alert, for which it is inappropriate to alert ships in an entire satellite Ocean Region or NAVAREA/METAREA.”

### 56H4

What system may be useful for messages, such as local storm warnings or a shore-to-ship Distress alert, for which it is inappropriate to alert all ships in the satellite coverage area?

**A. EGC**  
B. NAVTEX  
C. AMVER  
D. DSC  

EGC lets a message go to a user-defined area, which suits things like a local storm warning or a shore-to-ship distress relay, where alerting a whole ocean region would be inappropriate.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “The area may be fixed, as in the case of a NAVAREA/METAREA or coastal warning area, or a user-defined area (circular or rectangular). A user-defined area is used for messages, such as a local storm warning or a shore-to-ship distress alert, for which it is inappropriate to alert ships in an entire satellite Ocean Region or NAVAREA/METAREA.”

### 56H5

What services are available through Enhanced Group Calls?

A. Maritime Safety Information and vessel traffic lists.  
B. Hourly NOAA weather broadcasts from the NWS.  
C. Coastal weather broadcasts.  
**D. Maritime Safety Information and messages to pre-defined groups of subscribers.**  

EGC has two kinds of service: SafetyNET, which broadcasts maritime safety information, and a commercial service (FleetNET) that sends messages to predefined groups of subscribers, such as a company's fleet. Vessel traffic lists and NOAA hourly broadcasts aren't EGC services.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET is an international automatic direct-printing satellite-based service for the promulgation of Maritime Safety Information (MSI)”

### 56H6

What messages originate from registered information providers anywhere in the world and are broadcast to the appropriate ocean region via a LES?

**A. SafetyNETTM messages**  
B. AMVER broadcasts  
C. Urgency messages  
D. NAVTEX broadcasts  

SafetyNET messages come from registered information providers (such as NAVAREA coordinators and meteorological services) and are sent through an Inmarsat-C land earth station to the right ocean region.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “SafetyNET messages are submitted by registered information providers for promulgation to the appropriate satellite Ocean Region(s) via an Inmarsat C Land Earth Station (LES).”

## Topic 57. H.F. MSI

HF MSI broadcasts use NBDP (SITOR) in FEC mode on eight dedicated HF frequencies, for ships beyond NAVTEX range.

- The HF NBDP MSI frequencies are 4210, 6314, 8416.5, 12579, 16806.5, 19680.5, 22376 and 26100.5 kHz. ([47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077))
- Broadcasts use FEC (Mode B). ([Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en))
- Coast Guard stations at Boston (NMF) and Honolulu (NMO) broadcast HF SITOR. ([National Weather Service](https://www.weather.gov/marine/uscg_broadcasts))

### 57H1

Which HF SITOR (NBDP) mode would be selected to receive MSI broadcasts from high seas shore stations?

**A. FEC**  
B. AM  
C. RTTY  
D. ARQ  

HF MSI broadcasts use NBDP in FEC (forward error correction) mode, the broadcast mode. ARQ is for two-way links with a single station.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5, Direct-printing telegraph equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en): “Mode B, forward error correction (FEC)”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.625-4, Direct-printing telegraph equipment employing automatic identification in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.625/en): “that for the broadcast mode a forward error-correcting (FEC) method should be used”

### 57H2

The U.S. Coast Guard communications station providing HF MSI broadcast coverage for NAVAREA IV is:

A. NOJ (Kodiak)  
**B. NMF (Boston)**  
C. NMC (San Francisco)  
D. NMO (Honolulu)  

NAVAREA IV covers the western North Atlantic and Caribbean, and the Coast Guard's Boston station (NMF) broadcasts HF NBDP (SITOR) MSI for that area. Kodiak, San Francisco (Point Reyes) and Honolulu are Pacific stations.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the east coast boundary of French Guiana to 07-00N out to 035-00W, from there to 067-00N and the coastline of Greenland”
- [National Weather Service, Marine products via U.S. Coast Guard broadcasts](https://www.weather.gov/marine/uscg_broadcasts): “Boston (NMF) HF SITOR (NBDP Broadcast Schedule)”

### 57H3

The U.S. Coast Guard communications station providing HF MSI (Maritime Safety Information) broadcast coverage for NAVAREA XII is:

A. NMA (Miami)  
B. NMF (Boston)  
**C. NMO (Honolulu)**  
D. NMR (San Juan)  

NAVAREA XII covers the eastern North Pacific, and the Coast Guard's Honolulu station (NMO) broadcasts HF NBDP (SITOR) MSI there. Miami, Boston and San Juan are Atlantic stations.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the coastline at 03-24S to 120-00W, then to 00-00, then to 180-00, then to 50- 00N, and then following the International Date Line to 67-00N.”
- [National Weather Service, Marine products via U.S. Coast Guard broadcasts](https://www.weather.gov/marine/uscg_broadcasts): “Honolulu (NMO) HF SITOR (NBDP Broadcast Schedule)”

### 57H4

Frequencies for receiving HF MSI (Maritime Safety Information) are:

A. The same as used for NAVTEX  
B. The same as used for contact a Coast Radio Station using FEC  
C. Specified HF voice frequencies  
**D. Specified HF SITOR (NBDP) frequencies**  

HF MSI is broadcast on specific HF NBDP (SITOR) frequencies: 4210, 6314, 8416.5, 12579, 16806.5, 19680.5, 22376 and 26100.5 kHz. They aren't NAVTEX or voice frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “NBDP 4210 kHz, 6314 kHz, 8416.5 kHz, 12579 kHz, 16806.5 kHz, 19680.5 kHz, 22376 kHz, 26100.5 kHz.”

### 57H5

Which frequency/mode is authorized for use internationally for Maritime Safety Information transmissions?

**A. 4209.5 kHz using FEC mode**  
B. 4209.5 kHz using ARQ mode  
C. 4125.0 kHz using simplex mode  
D. 4125.0 kHz using FEC mode  

4209.5 kHz is an international MSI warnings frequency, used for NAVTEX-type broadcasts, which are sent in FEC mode. 4125 kHz is a voice distress frequency.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “Warnings 490 kHz, 4209.5 kHz.”
- [Recommendation ITU-R M.540-2, Operational and technical characteristics for an automated direct-printing telegraph system for promulgation of navigational and meteorological warnings and urgent information to ships](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.540/en): “The signals transmitted should be in conformity with the collective B-mode of the direct-printing system”

### 57H6

How many frequencies are assigned specifically for HF MSI broadcasts?

A. 6  
**B. 8**  
C. 5  
D. 7  

There are eight HF NBDP frequencies for MSI broadcasts: 4210, 6314, 8416.5, 12579, 16806.5, 19680.5, 22376 and 26100.5 kHz.

- [47 CFR §80.1077](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.1077): “NBDP 4210 kHz, 6314 kHz, 8416.5 kHz, 12579 kHz, 16806.5 kHz, 19680.5 kHz, 22376 kHz, 26100.5 kHz.”

## Topic 58. NAVAREAs

The world's oceans are divided into NAVAREAs for navigational warnings. The U.S. coordinates NAVAREA IV (western Atlantic) and XII (eastern Pacific), and the Arctic has NAVAREAs XVII to XXI.

- NAVAREA IV runs from French Guiana north to Greenland. ([IHO WWNWS16](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf))
- NAVAREA XII runs from about 3°S on the Pacific coast of South America out to the date line. ([IHO WWNWS16](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf))
- SafetyNET can address a whole NAVAREA. ([IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf))

### 58H1

Which NAVAREA is associated with the western North Atlantic and the Caribbean Sea?

A. NAVAREA X  
B. NAVAREA XI  
C. NAVAREA XII  
**D. NAVAREA IV**  

NAVAREA IV covers the western North Atlantic from French Guiana north to Greenland, including the Caribbean. The U.S. coordinates it.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the east coast boundary of French Guiana to 07-00N out to 035-00W, from there to 067-00N and the coastline of Greenland”
- [National Weather Service, METAREAs](https://www.weather.gov/marine/metarea): “is responsible for METAREAs IV (western north Atlantic) and XII (eastern north Pacific)”

### 58H2

Which sequence is associated with the 5 new Arctic Ocean NAVAREAs?

**A. NAVAREA XVIII, NAVAREA XX, NAVAREA XXI**  
B. NAVAREA III, NAVAREA VII, NAVAREA XV  
C. NAVAREA IV, NAVAREA XII, NAVAREA X  
D. NAVAREA XII, NAVAREA X, NAVAREA XI  

The Arctic NAVAREAs are XVII through XXI. Choice A lists three of them (XVIII, XX and XXI); the other choices name areas outside the Arctic.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “Until Inmarsat C or Mini-C terminals operating in arctic waters have been updated or replaced, C2 = 04 may be used for NAVAREA warnings to a rectangular area by NAVAREAs XVII, XVIII, XIX, XX and XXI.”

### 58H3

NAVAREAs referred to in NAVTEX are the same as used in:

A. GMDSS sea areas  
**B. Inmarsat SafetyNETTM**  
C. International Vessel Traffic Service  
D. Inmarsat ocean regions  

NAVAREAs are the same areas used by SafetyNET, which can address messages to a whole NAVAREA. They aren't the same as GMDSS sea areas or Inmarsat ocean regions.

- [IMO MSC.1/Circ.1364/Rev.2, International SafetyNET Services Manual](https://wwwcdn.imo.org/localresources/en/OurWork/Safety/Documents/Documents%20relevant%20to%20GMDSS/MSC.1-Circ.1364-Rev.2.pdf): “The area may be fixed, as in the case of a NAVAREA/METAREA or coastal warning area, or a user-defined area (circular or rectangular). A user-defined area is used for messages, such as a local storm warning or a shore-to-ship distress alert, for which it is inappropriate to alert ships in an entire satellite Ocean Region or NAVAREA/METAREA.”

### 58H4

A vessel operating in the Western Atlantic or along the East coast of North America and Central America from Canada to Venezuela, including the Caribbean and Panama, would be located in which NAVAREA?

A. X  
B. XI  
**C. IV**  
D. XIII  

The western Atlantic and the east coast of North and Central America, including the Caribbean, are in NAVAREA IV.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the east coast boundary of French Guiana to 07-00N out to 035-00W, from there to 067-00N and the coastline of Greenland”
- [National Weather Service, METAREAs](https://www.weather.gov/marine/metarea): “is responsible for METAREAs IV (western north Atlantic) and XII (eastern north Pacific)”

### 58H5

A vessel operating in the Eastern Pacific or along the West coast of North and Central America from Alaska to Ecuador, including Panama and Hawaii, would be operating in which NAVAREA?

A. X  
B. XI  
**C. XII**  
D. IV  

The eastern Pacific along the west coast of the Americas, including Hawaii, is NAVAREA XII. Its limits run from about 3°S on the South American coast out to 180° and north along the date line.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the coastline at 03-24S to 120-00W, then to 00-00, then to 180-00, then to 50- 00N, and then following the International Date Line to 67-00N.”
- [National Weather Service, METAREAs](https://www.weather.gov/marine/metarea): “is responsible for METAREAs IV (western north Atlantic) and XII (eastern north Pacific)”

### 58H6

A vessel on a voyage between Miami and Los Angeles via the Panama Canal would be operating in which NAVAREAS?

A. II and III  
B. IV and V  
C. V and VI  
**D. IV and XII**  

Miami is in NAVAREA IV (western Atlantic and Caribbean). Through the Panama Canal and up to Los Angeles, the ship is in NAVAREA XII (eastern Pacific). So the voyage spans IV and XII.

- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the east coast boundary of French Guiana to 07-00N out to 035-00W, from there to 067-00N and the coastline of Greenland”
- [IHO WWNWS16, MSI self-assessment for NAVAREA IV/XII (2024)](https://iho.int/uploads/user/Inter-Regional%20Coordination/WWNWS/WWNWS16/WWNWS16_2024_3.2-IV_XII_EN_Self%20Assessment%20NAVAREA%20IV%20XII.pdf): “From the coastline at 03-24S to 120-00W, then to 00-00, then to 180-00, then to 50- 00N, and then following the International Date Line to 67-00N.”

_No explanation published._

### 78I1

A vessel with an 18-hour ETA to the Panama Canal on a voyage from Miami loses the ability to communicate via Inmarsat-C. The most likely cause is?

A. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Southbury Land Earth Station.  
B. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Eastern Atlantic satellite.  
C. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Western Atlantic satellite.  
**D. An equipment fault resulting in a loss of signal from the satellite.**  

_No explanation published._

### 78I2

A vessel, before transiting the Panama Canal, on a voyage from Hawaii to Florida, loses the ability to communicate via Inmarsat-C. The most likely cause is:

**A. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Pacific satellite.**  
B. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Santa Paula Land Earth Station.  
C. The vessel has sailed beyond the coverage area of the Western Atlantic satellite.  
D. The satellite orbit is beyond the usable range of the SES.  

_No explanation published._

### 78I3

What would cause an FBB antenna to lose constant lock on the satellite?

A. An indication on a meter or on the display terminal of lowered transmit power.  
**B. Loss of the internal antenna pitch/roll sensors (IMS).**  
C. An indication of a very high antenna elevation on the display terminal screen.  
D. An indication that the antenna has reached its maximum travel in one direction.  

_No explanation published._

### 78I4

What maintenance function may the holder of a GMDSS Radio Operator license perform, or supervise the performance of, on an Inmarsat-C SES?

A. Adjust the station's EIRP (power output) for improved signal levels.  
B. Enter Azimuth and Elevation values correctly so the Inmarsat-C SES can find the NCS CC.  
**C. Remove stack deposits and other debris from the antenna to prevent degraded performance.**  
D. Adjust a reference oscillator or synthesizer to tune the unit to the NCS CC.  

_No explanation published._

### 78I5

What maintenance function may the holder of a GMDSS Radio Operator license perform, or supervise the performance of, on an Inmarsat-C SES?

A. Painting the antenna dome.  
B. Adjust the station's EIRP.  
**C. Adjust any front panel controls.**  
D. Adjust a reference oscillator or synthesizer.  

_No explanation published._

### 78I6

Which functions may the holder of a GMDSS Radio Operator License NOT perform on the Inmarsat-C equipment?

A. Maintain the antenna clear of soot, paint, etc.  
B. Log-on, traffic and log-off functions.  
C. Entry of position data and selection of LES.  
**D. Optimize performance by adjusting the transmitter EIRP.**  

_No explanation published._

## Topic 79. Equipment Faults and Maintenance-2

### 79I1

What immediate remedy can be used to correct shadowing of the satellite signal by a shipboard obstruction?

A. Relocate the mast or other obstruction.  
B. Raise the transmit power level.  
**C. Change the ship's course.**  
D. Increase the receiver gain.  

_No explanation published._

### 79I2

A vessel loses Inmarsat-FBB SES operation after a large course change. Which of the following could most likely cause this?

A. Shadowing of the SES antenna by clouds or other weather formations.  
B. Misalignment of the shadow correction filter.  
C. The vessel sailed beyond the footprint of the satellite coverage.  
**D. The ship’s superstructure is now blocking the satellite signal.**  

_No explanation published._

### 79I3

Which statement is correct regarding a method that a vessel experiencing problems with shadowing of an Inmarsat-FBB SES antenna by an on-board obstruction could use to attempt reliable communications?

**A. Change course to remove the shadow.**  
B. Change the Coast Station ID programming.  
C. Install a shadow correction filter.  
D. Switch from TELEX to voice mode.  

_No explanation published._

### 79I4

Which of the following conditions does not typically impair Inmarsat-FBB communications?

A. An obstruction, such as a mast, blocking the signal between the satellite and the SES antenna when the vessel is steering a specific course.  
**B. Normal precipitation from gales and storms.**  
C. A satellite whose signal is on a low elevation, below the horizon.  
D. Travel beyond the effective radius of the satellite.  

_No explanation published._

### 79I5

A vessel is experiencing problems tracking the satellite in an Inmarsat-C SES while at sea. The problem is least likely caused by?

A. Extremely heavy rain/snowstorms.  
**B. Local RF interference by in-port cell phone or other radio systems.**  
C. Shadowing caused by an obstacle, such as a mast, between the SES antenna and the satellite.  
D. The vessel is on the fringe of the coverage area of the satellite.  

_No explanation published._

### 79I6

Which functions may the holder of a GMDSS Radio Operator License not perform on the Inmarsat-C equipment?

A. Selection of LES for routine traffic.  
B. Maintain the antenna clear of soot, paint, etc.  
**C. Adjust the azimuth and elevation values.**  
D. Logon, traffic and logoff functions.  

_No explanation published._

## Topic 80. MF-HF Panel Controls

The main MF/HF transceiver settings are frequency (typed in or recalled as an ITU channel), mode (J3E for voice, F1B or J2B for telex) and power (only as much as needed).

- NBDP telex uses F1B or J2B with a 170 Hz shift. ([Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en))
- J3E is SSB voice with suppressed carrier. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))

### 80J1

Which modes could be selected to receive vessel traffic lists from high seas coast radio stations:

A. AM and VHF-FM  
**B. SSB and FEC**  
C. ARQ and FEC  
D. VHF-FM and SSB  

Coast stations announce traffic lists by voice (SSB) and by telex broadcast (FEC). To receive them you'd use SSB voice or FEC telex. ARQ is a two-way link to one station, and VHF FM and AM aren't used on the high seas.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.625-4, Direct-printing telegraph equipment employing automatic identification in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.625/en): “that for the broadcast mode a forward error-correcting (FEC) method should be used”

### 80J2

MF/HF Transceiver Power levels should be set:

A. To the highest possible level to ensure effective communications.  
B. To the lowest possible level at all times regardless of whether communications are effective.  
**C. To the lowest level needed to achieve the necessary propagation radius and communications range.**  
D. To the highest level possible so as to ensure other stations cannot "break-in" on the channel during use.  

Use the lowest power that reliably reaches the station you're working: enough for the range needed, but not so much that it causes unnecessary interference.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 80J3

Which statement regarding GMDSS MF/HF Transceiver frequency set-up is true:

A. Manual keypad entries are quicker and more certain than using the database.  
B. All consoles ensure that manual keypad entries are checked for legal outcomes.  
C. All consoles ensure that ITU channel recall from a database produces accurate and legal outcomes.  
**D. Using the manufacturer’s database typically produces a more swift and certain result.**  

Recalling ITU channels from the manufacturer's database is usually faster and more reliable than typing frequencies by hand, though it isn't infallible: databases differ between makes and must be checked.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 80J4

Which statement regarding GMDSS MF/HF Transceiver frequency set-up is true:

**A. Some consoles allow both manual keypad entry and ITU channel recall from a database or memory.**  
B. Transmit and receive frequencies must always be manually entered from the keypad.  
C. Transmit and receive frequencies must always be recalled from a database or memory.  
D. Frequencies in the manufacturer’s databases are always accurate and legal for use.  

Many consoles let the operator either type frequencies on the keypad or recall ITU channels from a built-in database or memory. Neither method is mandatory, and databases aren't guaranteed to be accurate.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 80J5

To set-up the MF/HF Transceiver for a TELEX call to a coast station, the operator must:

A. Select J3E mode for proper SITOR (NBDP) operations.  
B. Select F1B/J2B modes or J3E mode, depending on whether ARQ or FEC is preferred.  
C. Select J3E mode for ARQ and H3E mode for FEC.  
**D. Select F1B mode or J2B mode, depending on the equipment manufacturer.**  

Telex (SITOR/NBDP) uses frequency-shift keying, sent as F1B or, when the tones are fed into an SSB transmitter, J2B. Which one the console uses depends on the manufacturer. J3E and H3E are voice modes.

- [Recommendation ITU-R M.476-5, Direct-printing telegraph equipment in the maritime mobile service](https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-M.476/en): “The class of emission is F1B or J2B with a frequency shift on the radio link of 170 Hz.”

### 80J6

To set-up the MF/HF Transceiver for a voice call to a coast station, the operator must:

**A. Select J3E mode for proper voice operations.**  
B. Select J3E mode for proper SITOR (NBDP) operations.  
C. Select F1B mode or J2B mode, depending on the equipment manufacturer.  
D. Select F1B/J2B modes or J3E mode, depending on whether FEC or ARQ is preferred.  

MF/HF voice calls use J3E (single sideband, suppressed carrier). F1B and J2B are telex modes.

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “(6) Telephony (including sound broadcasting) E”

## Topic 81. MF-HF Frequency–Simplex, Duplex, Half-Duplex

Simplex uses one frequency for both directions; duplex uses a pair. Public coast stations work on paired (duplex) frequencies; ship-to-ship and private stations use simplex.

- HF public correspondence channels are ship/coast frequency pairs. ([47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371))
- NBDP also has paired ship and coast frequencies. ([47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361))

### 81J1

How are paired SITOR (NBDP) frequencies normally used?

**A. These are normally used for ARQ communications with coast radio stations.**  
B. These are normally used for FEC communications with coast radio stations.  
C. These are normally used only for Distress communications to limit channel interference.  
D. These are normally used for DSC communications with coast radio stations.  

Paired (duplex) NBDP frequencies, a coast frequency and a ship frequency, are used for ARQ telex with public coast stations. FEC broadcasts are one-way, distress NBDP uses simplex frequencies, and DSC has its own frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “The paired ship frequencies are available for use by authorized ship stations for NBDP and data transmissions.”

### 81J2

How are paired SSB frequencies normally used?

A. These are normally used for FEC communications with coast radio stations.  
**B. These are normally used for J3E communications with coast radio stations.**  
C. These are normally used for ARQ communications with coast radio stations.  
D. These are normally used for DSC communications with coast radio stations.  

Paired SSB frequencies (different ship and coast frequencies) are used for J3E voice with public coast stations.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “Working Carrier Frequency Pairs in the 4000-27500 kHz Band Channel Ship transmit Coast transmit”

### 81J3

For general communications purposes, paired frequencies are:

A. Normally used with private coast stations.  
B. Normally used between ship stations.  
**C. Normally used with public coast stations.**  
D. Normally used between private coast and ship stations.  

Paired (duplex) frequencies are normally used with public coast stations, for public correspondence. Ship-to-ship and private coast work uses simplex frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “Working Carrier Frequency Pairs in the 4000-27500 kHz Band Channel Ship transmit Coast transmit”

### 81J4

For general communications purposes, simplex frequencies are:

A. Normally used with public coast stations for routine telephone communications.  
B. Normally used between ship stations as well as public coast stations simultaneously.  
C. Normally used with public coast stations for routine SITOR (NBDP) communications.  
**D. Normally used between ship stations and private coast stations or for ship-ship communications.**  

Simplex frequencies are normally used for ship-to-ship communications and between ships and private coast stations. Public coast stations normally work on paired frequencies.

- [47 CFR §80.373](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.373): “These simplex frequencies also are available for use by authorized ship stations for business and operational radiotelephone communications.”

### 81J5

An ITU simplex channel frequency assignment is defined as:

**A. Transmit and receive frequencies must be identical.**  
B. Transmit and receive frequencies must be different.  
C. Transmit and receive frequencies may be different, depending on whether communications are ship-shore or ship to ship.  
D. Transmit and receive frequencies are different regardless of emission mode.  

On a simplex channel, the transmit and receive frequencies are the same.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 81J6

An ITU duplex channel frequency is defined as:

A. Transmit and receive frequencies may be identical if communications are ship-shore.  
**B. Transmit and receive frequencies must be different.**  
C. Transmit and receive frequencies must be identical.  
D. Transmit and receive frequencies may be different if communications are ship-ship.  

On a duplex channel, the transmit and receive frequencies are always different: one for ship transmit, one for coast transmit.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “Working Carrier Frequency Pairs in the 4000-27500 kHz Band Channel Ship transmit Coast transmit”

## Topic 82. MF-HF ITU Channels

HF ITU channel numbers combine the band and the channel: the leading digits give the band in MHz and the last two the channel.

- Channel 1601 is channel 1 in the 16 MHz band (16360 / 17242 kHz). ([47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371))
- Channel 2201 is channel 1 in the 22 MHz band. ([47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371))

### 82J1

Which of the following defines "ITU Channel 1216"?

A. Ch-12 in the 16 MHz band.  
B. Ch-1216 in the MF band.  
C. Ch-12 in the 16 kHz band.  
**D. Ch-16 in the 12 MHz band.**  

HF ITU channel numbers are the band in MHz followed by a two-digit channel number. 1216 is channel 16 in the 12 MHz band, just as 1601 is channel 1 in the 16 MHz band.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “1601 16360 17242”
- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “1201 12230 13077”

### 82J2

Which of the following is a valid 22-MHz ITU Channel?

**A. HF Ch-2206**  
B. VHF Ch-22  
C. Ch-22A VTS  
D. Ch-70 (DSC only)  

A 22 MHz ITU channel number starts with 22, so 2206 is channel 6 in the 22 MHz band. The other choices are VHF channels.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “2201 22000 22696”

### 82J3

What is meant by the term duplex "ITU channel"?

A. This refers to a vessel's SELCAL number.  
**B. A standardized series of frequency pairings for common use.**  
C. This refers to VHF channels 1-28 and 60-88.  
D. A series of frequency pairings used for ship-ship communications.  

A duplex ITU channel is one of a standard series of internationally agreed ship/coast frequency pairs, used in common by everyone.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “Working Carrier Frequency Pairs in the 4000-27500 kHz Band Channel Ship transmit Coast transmit”

### 82J4

ITU duplex channel 1604 would mean:

A. Ch-16 in the 4 MHz band.  
B. Ch-1604 in the MF band.  
**C. Ch-4 in the 16 MHz band.**  
D. Ch-4 in the 6 MHz band.  

HF ITU channel numbers give the band first, then the channel. 1604 is channel 4 in the 16 MHz band.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “1601 16360 17242”

### 82J5

What is a potential danger of using the manufacturer’s database to set up for operations using MF-HF ITU frequency assignments?

A. All manufacturer’s frequency databases are required to be standardized & accurate so that there is no danger.  
B. Using the manufacturer’s frequency database to setup the transceiver typically takes longer and is more prone to error than a manual setup.  
**C. Different manufacturers use different database numbering systems to incorporate the ITU assignments into the transceiver and therefore a potential for confusion exists.**  
D. Using the manufacturer’s frequency databases ensures that the transceiver is always restricted to the vessel’s licensed frequencies so that there is no danger.  

Manufacturers number their frequency databases in different ways, so the same channel can appear under different numbers on different equipment, and that can cause confusion. Databases aren't standardized or guaranteed.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 82J6

ITU Duplex channels are:

A. Frequency assignments specific to U.S. vessels only.  
B. VHF-FM frequencies.  
C. International Traffic Utility frequencies.  
**D. Internationally standardized assignments of frequency pairs for common use.**  

ITU duplex channels are internationally standardized pairs of ship and coast frequencies for common use.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “Working Carrier Frequency Pairs in the 4000-27500 kHz Band Channel Ship transmit Coast transmit”

## Topic 83. MF-HF Voice and Telex Channel Separation

Channel spacing follows bandwidth, so adjacent channels don't interfere: 0.5 kHz for NBDP and 3 kHz for SSB voice, and much wider for VHF FM.

- Voice channels: 3 kHz apart. ([47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371))
- NBDP channels: 0.5 kHz apart. ([47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361))

### 83J1

Which statement regarding bandwidth and channel spacing is correct:

A. Bandwidth values are a function of channel spacing values.  
B. Channel spacing values are not a function of bandwidth values.  
**C. Channel spacing values are a function of bandwidth values.**  
D. Bandwidth & channel values do not vary with emission mode.  

Channel spacing is set by the bandwidth of the emission: a wider signal needs channels spaced farther apart. That's why voice (3 kHz) channels are spaced wider than NBDP (0.5 kHz) channels.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 83J2

The purpose of ITU channel spacing is:

A. To minimize the number of voice & TELEX channels available.  
B. To make most efficient use of the radio spectrum, by using voice channels rather than TELEX channels.  
C. To make most efficient use of the radio spectrum, by using TELEX channels rather than voice channels.  
**D. To minimize the possibility of interference from adjacent channels.**  

Channel spacing keeps signals on adjacent channels from interfering with each other.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 83J3

Which statement regarding channel spacing and bandwidth is true?

**A. Both TELEX bandwidth and channel spacing values are less than voice bandwidth and channel spacing values.**  
B. Voice bandwidth is greater than TELEX bandwidth and therefore voice channel spacing values are less than TELEX channel spacing values.  
C. TELEX bandwidth is greater than voice bandwidth and therefore TELEX channel spacing values are less than voice channel spacing values.  
D. Both TELEX bandwidth and channel spacing values are greater than voice bandwidth and channel spacing values.  

Telex (NBDP) signals are narrower than voice, so both their bandwidth and their channel spacing (0.5 kHz) are smaller than voice (3 kHz).

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 83J4

The proper sequence of channel spacing from narrow to widest is:

A. SSB voice, SITOR (NBDP), VHF-FM voice.  
**B. SITOR (NBDP), SSB-voice, VHF-FM voice.**  
C. VHF-FM voice, SITOR (NBDP), SSB-voice.  
D. SITOR (NBDP), VHF-FM voice, SSB-voice.  

From narrowest to widest: SITOR/NBDP (0.5 kHz), SSB voice (3 kHz), VHF FM voice (25 kHz channels).

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 83J5

Communications with an emission of F1B/J2B would typically have a channel spacing of:

A. 0.3 kHz  
**B. 0.5 kHz**  
C. 2.8 kHz  
D. 3.0 kHz  

F1B/J2B (NBDP telex) channels are spaced 0.5 kHz apart.

- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “1 4210.5 4172.5”
- [47 CFR §80.361](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.361): “2 4211.0 4173.0 6315.0 6263.5”

### 83J6

Communications with an emission of J3E would typically have a channel spacing of:

A. 0.5 kHz  
B. 0.3 kHz  
**C. 3.0 kHz**  
D. 2.8 kHz  

J3E (SSB voice) channels are spaced 3.0 kHz apart.

- [47 CFR §80.371](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.371): “404 4074 4366 x x x x 405 4077 4369”

## Topic 84. MF-HF Modulation, Bandwidth and Emissions

Emission codes describe a signal: A3E is AM voice, J3E SSB voice without carrier, H3E SSB with full carrier, F3E FM voice, and F1B direct-printing telex.

- A, H and J are amplitude-modulated types; F is frequency modulation. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))
- B means telegraphy for automatic reception, such as telex. ([47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201))

### 84J1

For RF communications, "modulation" is best defined as:

A. Using a single carrier frequency with the proper power level.  
**B. The combination of information or intelligence on to a radio carrier frequency.**  
C. Setting up the transceiver with the correct bandwidth to ensure proper communications.  
D. The combination of the received frequency and oscillator frequency in the mixer.  

Modulation is combining the information (voice or data) with a radio carrier.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 84J2

For RF communications, "bandwidth" is best defined as:

A. The modulation technique required to ensure proper ITU channel spacing.  
B. The emission designation resulting from the desired modulation technique.  
**C. The portion of the radio spectrum consumed by a particular emission selection and modulation technique.**  
D. The portion of the radio spectrum reserved for frequency allocations by the ITU.  

Bandwidth is the slice of radio spectrum a signal occupies, which depends on the emission and modulation used.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._


### 84J3

In an AM broadcast signal using voice:

A. Varying the amplitude of the carrier and employing both sidebands without the carrier.  
B. Varying only the amplitude of the carrier, depending on Double or Single-Sideband operations.  
C. Varying the amplitude of the carrier and employing both sidebands and the carrier.  
**D. There is a carrier with constant amplitude and frequency with complex upper and lower sidebands varying in amplitude and frequency.**  

In ordinary AM (A3E), viewed as a spectrum, the carrier component stays at constant amplitude and frequency, and the voice appears as upper and lower sidebands that vary in amplitude and frequency. The overall waveform rises and falls, but that's the sidebands adding to the carrier. The carrier itself doesn't change, so D is the best description.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”

### 84J4

In FM communications, the information is applied to the carrier by:

**A. Varying the frequency of the carrier to convey the information to other stations.**  
B. Varying the amplitude or the frequency of the carrier, depending on Double or Single-Sideband operations.  
C. Varying only the frequency of the carrier, depending on Double or Single-Sideband operations.  
D. Varying the amplitude of the carrier and keeping the frequencies in the sidebands constant.  

In FM, the information varies the frequency of the carrier.

_General operating practice, not a quoted rule._

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “Emission in which the main carrier is angle-modulated: —Frequency modulation F”

### 84J5

The proper sequence of emissions corresponding to the sequence AM-Voice DSB, SSB-Voice without carrier, USB-Voice with carrier and FM-Voice, is:

A. A3E, H3E, J3E, F3E.  
B. J3E, H3E, A3E, F3E.  
C. H3E, A3E, J3E, F3E.  
**D. A3E, J3E, H3E, F3E.**  

AM double sideband voice is A3E, SSB voice with suppressed carrier is J3E, SSB with full carrier is H3E, and FM voice is F3E.

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “Emission in which the main carrier is angle-modulated: —Frequency modulation F”

### 84J6

The proper sequence of emissions corresponding to the sequence SSB-Voice without carrier, USB-Voice with carrier, FM-Voice and SITOR (NBDP) TELEX is:

**A. J3E, H3E, F3E, F1B.**  
B. H3E, J3E, F3E, F1B.  
C. J3E, H3E, F1B, F3E.  
D. H3E, J3E, F1B, F3E.  

SSB voice without carrier is J3E, SSB with carrier is H3E, FM voice is F3E, and SITOR/NBDP telex is F1B (digital, automatic reception).

- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “—Double-sideband A —Single-sideband, full carrier H —Single-sideband, reduced or variable level carrier R —Single-sideband, suppressed carrier J”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “Emission in which the main carrier is angle-modulated: —Frequency modulation F”
- [47 CFR §2.201](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-2.201): “(3) Telegraphy—for automatic reception B”

## Topic 85. MF-HF Voice Operations–Calling a Coast Station

To call a coast station by HF voice, listen first, then call on a channel it monitors using call signs and phonetics. Any properly licensed coast station worldwide can be used.

- Don't interfere with transmissions already in progress. ([47 CFR §80.101](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.101))
- Ships may use whatever frequency a foreign coast station designates. ([47 CFR §80.367](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.367))
- The ITU List of Coast Stations is a required publication. ([47 CFR §80.401](https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/section-80.401))

### 85J1

When placing a SSB MF/HF call to a Coast Station, you should always:

**A. Make sure the frequency is not occupied.**
